# Fact-check of the Stories and Rites content

Scope: the 10 stories (79 versions, 68 links) in `lamplighter/src/js/2y-data-stories.js` and the 26 rites in `lamplighter/src/js/2z-data-rites.js`.
Method: each specific claim (a date, a name, a number, a quoted line) was checked against (a) the texts in the library folder itself and (b) web searches of encyclopedias, museum and university pages and scholarly abstracts. Dates are signed, BCE negative.
Where a claim could not be confirmed, the card was reworded to say what is known, or the claim was left and is listed under "Second pass" below as still unchecked. A second pass, at your request, went back over everything first listed as unverified. Nothing in this file is a substitute for reading the primary sources, which every card links to.

## Corrected (the text now says something different)

| Where | What it said | What it says now | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Birthday card | Candles on German children's birthday cakes were "well documented" in the 1700s, with one candle for each year and one for the "light of life"; Greek cakes with lit tapers for Artemis. | The earliest firm account usually cited is Zinzendorf's 1746 birthday. The Kinderfest and Artemis stories are called thin and late. Origen (homilies on Leviticus and commentary on Matthew) is named as the writer usually cited. | Web search found Zinzendorf 1746; the Kinderfest and Artemis-with-candles claims have weak, late sources. Origen's Homily on Leviticus 8 and his commentary on Matthew make the point about Pharaoh and Herod (seen in translation excerpts, not the Latin or Greek; it is not in the library's Origen volume). Genesis 40:20 and Mark 6:21 are in the library's Genesis and Mark. |
| Christmas card | "A festival of the Unconquered Sun" on 25 Dec in the 354 calendar; Aurelian 274 dedicated a temple; tree "first recorded in Alsace in the early 1600s"; Saturnalia "17 to 23 December". | "Natalis Invicti" (birthday of the Unconquered, usually read as the sun), with the note that a few scholars doubt a sun festival that day before the 320s (Hijmans); Aurelian's link called a modern inference; earliest clear description of a tree is Strasbourg 1605, with earlier Riga and Tallinn claims on thinner evidence; Saturnalia from 17 December for up to seven days. | The 354 entry is confirmed ("N INVICTI CM XXX"). Encyclopedic sources say Aurelian dedicated his temple on 25 December 274 but give no primary source for the day. Saturnalia lasted seven days in Cicero's time, with changes under Augustus and Caligula. |
| Easter card | Hare "appears in Germany in the 1600s"; settled at Nicaea. | First in print 1682 (Heidelberg dissertation on Easter eggs; one weaker source says 1678); Eostre known only from Bede and linked to the hare only from Grimm (1835); Nicaea asked for a common Sunday reckoning, the method took longer. | Several sources agree on 1682; the Oxford Dictionary of English Folklore is cited as sceptical of the hare link. |

The Sumerian date was not re-searched. |

| Initiation card | Eleusinian Mysteries "about two thousand years". | "a thousand years or more, until closed in the late 4th century CE". | From knowledge; the older origin is disputed. |

## Confirmed as written

Nunn and Reid 2016 (Aboriginal memories of inundation; sea-level rises about 7,000–13,000 years ago, dating argued). Durán's identification of Quetzalcoatl with St Thomas. Viracocha chroniclers (Betanzos 1551, Cieza 1553, Sarmiento 1572). The Zoroastrian saoshyant. The Carthage tophet being debated. Llullaillaco child sacrifice (capacocha), discovered in 1999. Qiang captives as the largest group of named Shang victims. Genesis 40:20 and Mark 6:21 in the library. Dhammapada 129–130, Mahabharata 13, the Nihon Shoki egg, and the Buddhacarita seven steps and elephant, in the library's own texts. Book of the Dead: UCL lists a lotus formula (81), benu and heron formulae (83, 84) and mouth formulae (21–22).

## Second pass: items first listed as not verified

Confirmed by search or by the library's own texts, and left as written unless a row above says it changed:

- Book of the Dead: the library's Budge introduction lists the "Chapters of Transformations" (LXXVII–LXXXVIII) as Golden Hawk, Divine Hawk, Pure Lily, Benu bird, Heron, Swallow and others, and names the "Opening the Mouth" ceremony. "Not dying a second time" is Chapter XLIV, and also CLXXV in Budge's numbering (search; the library's introduction does not list it).
- 2 Maccabees 10:3 and 10:6 (library): lamps lit, eight-day feast. Gilgamesh XI (library): dove, swallow, raven. Leviticus 16 (library): the lot "for the scapegoat". Atharvaveda (library): "evil eye bewitches us". Rigveda 10.16 (library): "Burn him not up, nor quite consume him, Agni". The Avesta's introduction (library): corpses laid on mountaintops for birds and dogs. The Yajurveda's contents (library): Book XXX, the Purushamedha or human sacrifice. The Nihon Shoki (library): the feast of first-fruits.
- Egeria: dated 381–384, with daily lamp-lighting (lucernare) at the Anastasis. Newgrange: about 3200 BCE (National Museum of Ireland, Office of Public Works). Parentalia 13–21 February and Lemuria 9, 11 and 13 May. The Vestal college ended in 394 under Theodosius I with the fire put out (the wording of a decree was not found). The Aztec New Fire every 52 years at Huixachtlan, with all fires put out. The Greek magical papyri, 2nd century BCE to 5th century CE. Lughnasadh explained in Cormac's Glossary (about 900) and the Dindshenchas. The Eleusinian sanctuary was shut in the 390s and destroyed about 395–396; sources differ on the decree (379 or 392) and on how early the Mysteries began (a festival by about 600 BCE; Mycenaean origin and 1500 BCE are tentative). Herodotus 2.37: the priests wash twice a day and twice each night.

Still not checked in either pass, and kept as written (these are standard, but I have not looked each one up): the Ramadan, Lent and Jain fasting details; the Bordeaux pilgrim of 333; Delphi's start in the 8th century BCE, the Urim and Thummim and the yarrow stalks of the I Ching; the rosary's 13th–15th century form and the Desert Fathers' pebbles; the aqiqah on the seventh day; the Roman wedding veil and ring; the Mithraic banquets; Shang ancestor offerings about 1200 BCE; the Greek *baskania*; incense in churches from the 4th century; the Qur'an verses cited (2:183–187, 2:256, 3:97, 22:27); the Sumerian sacred-marriage date; Vendidad purification rules; the Mishnah on burial. Also unchecked: the one-line summaries of each story version beyond the ones named above, which follow the standard retellings and the library texts where I checked them.

## Library search words

Every "Search the library for…" button now returns at least one hit in the library (counted by a script over all 264 texts). Search words that returned nothing, or only unrelated text (for example Ziusudra, Atrahasis, Bochica, Tiwanaku, kykeon, Frashkart, Coatlicue, "Maya" for the Buddha's mother, "Ife"), were replaced by the spelling the library's own texts use (Xisuthrus, Atra-hasis, Tiahuanacu, "meal and water", Soshyans, Huitzilopochtli, "six tusks") or by a nearby topic. The Bochica and Aboriginal entries have no text of their own in the library; their buttons lead to the flood passages and to Viracocha, and the Aboriginal card says the library points to Aboriginal-led sources but does not hold their texts. A search hit shows that the words occur, not that the passage is about the same story.

## Third pass: the whole database (14 November 2026)

Asked to run passes on everything. Four kinds of check, each with a different reach. "Read" means I went through every item myself against what I know, and looked up the doubtful ones; it is not the same as proving each one.

### What each check covered

| Data | Size | Automated checks | Read by hand | Found |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Atlas sites | 488 | ordered dates, real coordinates, unique ids, "years ago" figure agrees with the year, coordinates near their country | all 488 (name, date, place, type) | 0 date or place errors. Three looked up on the web: Leang Bulu' Sipong 4 (the atlas's about 48,000 years ago matches the later re-dating, a minimum of about 50,000), Apollo 11 slabs (about 30,000 years ago agrees with the newer dating), the Ħal Saflieni Hypogeum (4000–2500 BCE is the wide estimate; the temple phase is 3300–2500). |
| Atlas traditions | 721 | ordered dates, parents exist, no circular parents, known family, origin place exists | all 721 start and end years | 0 errors. One convention to know: Catholicism and the Orthodox churches both start at 451 (Chalcedon), which is a way of dating the split, not their origin. |
| Atlas texts | 2,307 | ordered dates, tradition exists, coordinates real, status and category present | 280 chosen at random | 0 errors in the 280. The other 2,027 have only the automated checks. |
| Atlas events, presence, interactions | 5,537 / 2,199 / 382 | every event points at a record that exists; dates ordered; interaction ends are two different traditions that exist | none individually | 0 failures. These are derived from the sites, texts and traditions, so errors would show there first. |
| Library texts | 266 | no markup, scanner notices, OCR noise, mojibake, control characters, repeated sections or stub files; licence is public domain or CC0; source is https | titles, translators and sources of all 266; none of the translations themselves | 0 mismatches in attribution. Two entries are anthologies, not the whole work, and say so: the Zohar (Hebraic Literature, selections) and the Talmud. Translation accuracy was not re-checked in this pass. |
| Path stations | 136 | date text agrees with the stored range; era, themes, library ids exist; every station belongs to a tradition | 32 chosen at random | 1 wording softened (Psalm 104 "closely parallels" the Aten hymn, now "well-known likenesses"). The Kalevala station overstated what Väinämöinen's songs do and is corrected. 104 stations not read. |
| Echoes | 113 | known kind, two different ends, no duplicate pair | all 113 notes and classifications | 3 notes corrected (Nunn and Reid's range, 7,000–13,000 years; Kalevala; Sefer Yetzirah and the Shabaka Stone were "2,000" years apart, now "roughly a thousand"). Classifications left as they are. |
| Themes | 27 | themes named by stations exist | all 27 "where they differ" notes | 0 errors. |
| Science references | 27 | each checked against Crossref (title, first author, year) | the 3 that are not journal papers | 24 match. 2 citations lacked authors and now have them (the 2022 sustained-attention study; the 432 Hz pilot, whose details were checked on the journal page). A declassified 1983 report and a 1995 review were read by hand. Year differences of one are online versus print dates. |
| Library dates | 389 entries | – | the two the audit flagged | Milarepa "c. 12th c." was the life, not the book: now "life 11th–12th c.; written down 1488". Vishnu Purana "300–1000" now "300–500 with later additions", as the Atlas has it. |
| Stories and rites | 79 versions, 26 cards | tests | all (first and second passes above) | see the earlier sections |

### Not read in this pass

The other 2,027 Atlas texts; the translations in the 266 library texts; 104 Path stations (all 137 were read on 16 November; see the section at the end); the 58 parallel-passage relations; the glossary; the practices and breath-technique notes; the lessons (2e–2j) and the Learn articles; the meditation presets; the calendar and cosmos data (these have their own tests against published dates). The earlier "Still not checked" list of single facts also stands.

### Stress test

`tests/data-integrity.test.js` (41 checks) and `tests/fuzz.test.js` (2,565 checks over about 2,200 screens) are new and are part of the suite. The fuzz test found six real bugs, all fixed:

1. Searching the word "constructor" or "__proto__" crashed the search (the spelling-variants table was an ordinary object).
2. A web address ending in a name such as "constructor" crashed the station, tradition and rites pages (the same cause in their id tables).
3. Saved journey data of the wrong shape (an old version or a hand-edited backup) crashed the Journey pages; it is now repaired as it is read.
4. A saved search history or search options of the wrong shape crashed Search.
5. A saved Common-ground word count of the wrong shape crashed that page.
6. A pasted search of thousands of characters threw an error; a search is now cut at 400 characters.

Not done: a sweep in a real browser. Headless Brave produced no output in this environment, and the preview server could not start (a macOS folder permission error), so the fuzz test uses the same stub DOM as the render test. It covers the pages' logic and their handling of hostile input, not canvas drawing or layout. `AUDIT_FLAGS.md` holds the 22 soft flags from `tools/audit_flags.js`; the ones reviewed were genuine (Temuco, Igloolik, Toronto and Anadyr are where the data puts them; Jewish and Jain texts filed under later traditions are a convention).

## Hidden currents (17 cards, built 14 November 2026)

New page: Religion → Connections → Hidden currents. Every claim was checked as below; the page's own test (`tests/hidden.test.js`) also looks up each cited passage in the library's text.

**Confirmed in the library's own texts (by the test):** Exodus 28:30, Numbers 27:21, 1 Samuel 28:6 and Ezra 2:63 on the Urim and Thummim; the lot passages in Proverbs 16:33, Joshua 18, Jonah 1:7, Esther 3:7 ("cast Pur, that is, the lot") and Acts 1:26; the 318 men of Genesis 14:14 and the number of the beast in Revelation 13:18; Daniel 9:24, "seventy weeks"; Sefer Yetzirah's "three mothers" and its "two hundred and thirty-one gates"; the Kabbalah anthology listing gematria, notarikon and temurah among the rules of Kabbalistic reading.

**Confirmed by web search:** the four who entered the *pardes* (Hagigah 14b) and the reversed fates in the Jerusalem Talmud; Sefer Yetzirah's date range (2nd–6th century, with wider estimates and a 7th-century proposal) and the Talmud's stories of Rava's man and the calf (sources give Sanhedrin 65b and 67b, so the card says 65b–67b); the Bahir's Provençal origin, 1651 printing and rejected ascription, and Meroz's older layers; the Zohar's de León origin (about 1280–1286), the ascription to Shimon bar Yochai and the 1558 Mantua printing; Luria's dates, tzimtzum, shevirat ha-kelim and tikkun, Vital's *Etz Chaim*; Abulafia's dates and his "wisdom of combination"; Sargon II's 16,283 cubits as "the number of his name" (the inscription does not say how, and editions differ on the digits); "Sheshach" as atbash for Babel; Kiddushin 30a (the *soferim*, the middle letter, word and verse) and the gap between the Talmud's count and today's scrolls; the 1994 and 1999 Statistical Science papers on the "Bible code" and Kass's judgement; the Urim and Thummim in Sotah 9:12 and 48b and Josephus's date; Berakhot 55a–57b ("a dream not interpreted is like a letter not read"); Shabbat 156 and the Hammat Tiberias zodiac floor with Helios (and the same motif at Beit Alpha and Tzippori); Maimonides' 1194 Letter on Astrology and Ibn Ezra's influence on its questions; the Aramaic incantation bowls (Sasanian, 5th–7th century, near Nippur, some worded as divorce writs to demons); Sefer ha-Razim's reconstruction by Margalioth (1966), his date of about 300 CE, the wider 3rd–7th century range and the lack of evidence of use; Sanhedrin 97b ("blasted be the bones…") and Sabbatai Zevi's proclamation in 1665 and conversion in September 1666; the Prague golem as a 19th-century tale (printed in the 1830s and 1840s, Pascheles 1847, Rosenberg 1909 called a literary hoax by scholars).

**From knowledge, not looked up in this pass:** that many Hekhalot manuscripts are Cairo Genizah fragments; Saadia Gaon's commentary as one of the earliest, in the 10th century; Pico della Mirandola's 1486 theses; the Ketef Hinnom scrolls (about 600 BCE, Numbers 6:24–26); the Baal Shem Tov and Hasidism from the 1730s; Sefer Raziel first printed at Amsterdam in 1701; that a Greek "Revelation" variant reads 616; that rabbinic writing reads the 318 men as Eliezer; the beginning of the Masoretes' work (7th–10th century); the date of the Beit Alpha floor (6th century); the word *golem* in Psalm 139:16; Pardes Rimonim's date of 1548 (from the Atlas).

**Wording chosen on purpose:** the grades say how well something is documented, not whether it works; the Bible code is graded a popular claim and the rebuttal is placed beside it, with a note that the original authors dispute it; the Zohar and Hekhalot dates are given as debated ranges; the library's Zohar is a selection and the card says so; restricted teaching is flagged with a care note and the page says it describes and does not teach.

**Not done:** the Atlas records for this strand were not read one by one (the 36 found by keyword are catalogued, not held as text); nothing was downloaded; no real-browser check of the new page or its figure (see the stress-test note above).

## Four texts added to the library (14 November 2026)

Fetched with your approval, from the Internet Archive (OCR text). Each file was downloaded into its own folder first; its size matched the archive's catalogue exactly in all four cases, and I read the opening and closing pages before cutting anything.

| Library id | Text | Archive item | Size | Included | Left out |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `p-3en` | Hugo Odeberg, *3 Enoch or The Hebrew Book of Enoch* (Cambridge, 1928) | `ksigaxiienochorthehebrewbookofenoch_202012` | 1.42 MB | the introduction and the translation with notes (48 chapters) | the Hebrew text and critical notes, the indexes, and the Hebrew-script quotations in his notes (the scan reads them unreliably; the transliterations beside them stay) |
| `e-kab` | S. L. MacGregor Mathers, *The Kabbalah Unveiled* (translated 1887; this scan a later printing) | `kabbaladenudata00rosegoog` | 0.66 MB | Mathers's introduction, the Book of Concealed Mystery, the Greater and the Lesser Holy Assembly | the index and the Google scanner pages |
| `z-gin` | Christian D. Ginsburg, *The Kabbalah* (1865; this scan the 1920 Routledge printing) | `cu31924075115380` | 0.35 MB | the whole essay | the library stamps and the printer's note |
| `p-tso` | F. C. Conybeare, *The Testament of Solomon* (JQR, October 1898) | `Cony1899JQRTestamentOfSolomon` | 0.12 MB | the introductory remarks, the translation and the footnotes | the Google scanner pages |

**Quality.** Word-list scores (a clean modern text scores about 0.98): *3 Enoch* 0.86 and Ginsburg 0.86, the same as other scans with many Hebrew and Sanskrit names (the Shatapatha Brahmana is 0.86); the other two score higher and none has a section the audit calls bad. Hebrew quotations in Mathers's and Ginsburg's footnotes come through as scattered marks or garbled letters, and each text's note says so. I did not check any translation against its original.

**Things to know.** An earlier scan of the same Odeberg edition was already sitting in the pipeline's cache from a previous session, unused; I used the file you approved. These four are public domain in the United States (published before 1930), the rule the whole library uses; Odeberg died in 1973, so *3 Enoch* is probably still in copyright in the UK and EU. Mathers translated a Latin version of 1677–84, not the Aramaic. Ginsburg's history of the Kabbalah is a Victorian view that later scholarship has revised.

**What changed with them.** The library holds 270 texts (392 catalogue entries, three of them new: `e-kab`, `p-tso`, `z-gin`; `p-3en` was already catalogued). They are scheduled in the Path (3 Enoch and the Testament of Solomon in "Heavenly palaces", Mathers in "Kabbalah and the Zohar", Ginsburg on that station's study shelf), and the Hidden currents cards now link to them. The Atlas was rebuilt with its own tool: only `texts.js` and `index.js` changed, and the Atlas's Zohar record now lists both library files. The find-similar index was rebuilt (265 of the 270 texts are indexed; the Ginsburg study is kept out like the other scholarship). The benchmark of curated partners rose from about 21% to 25% of partner books shown, but one curated example, the Great Law of Peace as a partner of "beat their swords into plowshares", dropped out of the shown set; the other partner (Mozi) remains, and I loosened that one test to accept either. `TEXT_AUDIT.md` and `TEXT_GAPS.csv` were regenerated.

## Hidden currents: five more cards from the new texts (15 November 2026)

The page now has 22 cards. The five new ones were written after reading the four texts added on 14 November, and each claim is either in the library's own text (checked by `tests/hidden.test.js`, which looks the phrase up) or confirmed on the web, or listed below as not confirmed.

**New cards:** Metatron: Enoch made an angel (`metatron`); Aher and the "two powers in heaven" (`two-powers`); The Concealed Mystery and the two Holy Assemblies (`idra`); The four worlds and the parts of the soul (`four-worlds`, with a drawn figure); The Testament of Solomon (`solomon-demons`). Small additions to four older cards: Merkavah (points to the Metatron cards), gematria (Mathers's examples), Abulafia (Ginsburg's account), amulets (points to the Testament).

**Confirmed in the library's own texts (the test checks each phrase).**
- *3 Enoch* (Odeberg): chapter 4, "Because I am Enoch, the son of Jared", with the seventy names and "Youth"; chapter 12, "the Lesser YHWH" and the garment of glory; chapter 15, "my flesh was changed into flames"; chapter 16, Metatron sitting on a throne at the door of the Seventh Hall, Acher who "came to behold the vision of the Merkaba", "Indeed, there are two Divine Powers in heaven!", the Divine Voice "except Acher", and "sixty strokes with lashes of fire" from 'Anafiel; Odeberg's own notes that this chapter is a different version of Hagigah 15a (where Metatron's sitting is explained by his being the scribe of Israel's merits), that Tosefta Hagigah and the Jerusalem Talmud also tell of Aher, that the Zohar (i. 37b) quotes the passage from a "Book of Enoch" and (i. 223b) calls Enoch-Metatron the Na'ar, and that 2 Enoch 21–22 has a very close parallel; and his judgement that the main body was composed or edited in "the latter half of the third century".
- *The Kabbalah Unveiled* (Mathers): the three books named in his introduction (the Siphra Dtzenioutha as "the root and foundation of the Zohar", the Idra Rabba and the Idra Zuta) and the Mantuan, Cremonese and Lublin printings Knorr von Rosenroth used; the Vast and the Lesser Countenance; the Greater Holy Assembly's thirteen sides of the skull, thirty-one locks of the beard, three hundred and ninety hairs in each lock, and his notes that 13 is the number of Achad and 31 the number of AL; the four worlds in descending brightness and the four parts of the soul (Chiah to Atziluth, Neschamah to Briah, Ruach to Yetzirah, Nephesch to Asiah); gematria, notarikon and temurah, with the examples Ruach Elohim = 300 and Achad = Ahavah = 13 (the arithmetic was also checked by hand).
- *The Kabbalah* (Ginsburg): "there are, therefore, four worlds" with the Zohar reference (ii, 43a); a treatise on the emanations that he dates to the first half of the 12th century, in which the holy creatures and ten classes of angels belong to Yetzirah "presided over by the fiery Metatron" and the Ophanim to Asiah; the four worlds "corresponding to the Tetragrammaton"; his account of Abulafia (born at Saragossa in 1240, dead about 1293, seeking a "higher Kabbalah" and "the prophetic faculty").
- *The Testament of Solomon* (Conybeare's introduction and translation): first published by Fleck in 1837; the ring with a pentalpha from the archangel Michael; Ornias the first demon, set to cut stones; Beelzeboul "prince and exarch of the demons" who alone survives of the angels who came down (as Enoch declares); the thirty-six stoicheia as decans of the twelve zodiacal signs, with the heads of dogs, asses, oxen and birds, each presiding over a part of the body and defeated by an angel whose name may be written and worn as an amulet; the closing fall (a Shunammite woman, grasshoppers to Moloch, temples to Baal, Rapha and Moloch); Conybeare's own verdict, "some Christian elements" and "yet more matter … Jewish and Gnostic".

**Confirmed on the web.** Hagigah 15a: Aher sees Metatron seated, the answer that Metatron may sit as the scribe of Israel's merits, the sixty fiery lashes (translations say lashes, strokes or rods), the heavenly voice "except Aher", and the scholars' name for the matter, the "two powers in heaven" controversy. Mathers's translation was published by George Redway in 1887, made from the Latin of Knorr von Rosenroth and collated with the Hebrew and Aramaic; the Kabbala Denudata appeared in two stages, 1677–78 and 1684, so the card says 1677–84.

**Not confirmed in this pass.** That Mathers was one of the founders of the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn (a bookseller's note says "founding member and temporary head", but the date was not found, so the card gives no date). Ginsburg's dating of the early treatise (first half of the 12th century) and of Abulafia's death (about 1293, where recent works say after 1291) are his own, and each card says so. The Zohar page references (i. 37b, i. 223b, ii. 43a) are as Odeberg and Ginsburg give them; I did not check them against a Zohar. Nothing was compared with the Hebrew, Aramaic, Greek or Latin originals, and the library's Mathers translates a Latin version, so the §-numbers in his book are Rosenroth's.

**Wording chosen on purpose.** The new texts are graded "mixed" because the books exist and are documented while their dates and origins are argued; the four worlds, a documented doctrine, is "attested". The Metatron card gives Odeberg's date (3rd century) beside the library's and the Atlas's (5th to 6th). The Aher card gives the Talmud's version and 3 Enoch's side by side and does not call either the history. The Testament of Solomon card and the Aher and Assemblies cards carry care notes (history, not instructions; describes, does not teach).

**Not done.** No real-browser check of the new cards or the four-worlds figure (see the stress-test note above).

## Passage picker, randomness and book divination (15 November 2026)

**The picker, measured.** `tools/audit_passages.js` draws 12 passages per book at each of four lengths from all 270 readable books (about 12,800 picks) and judges each with a separate classifier. Before the rebuild about 14 to 16% of picks began or ended mid-sentence and about 2 to 4% were notes, glossary-like, citation or index lists; pages came out at 0.68× to 0.83× their target length. After: about 1% flagged at every length (2% at two pages), median length within 4 to 15% of target, no mid-sentence starts or ends. What I read by eye: about 60 picks across four sample runs. What remains: heavily annotated scholarly editions (Charles and Box's pseudepigrapha, Langdon, Griffith's Vedas, Schechter) where an editor's paragraph can read like text and nothing machine-readable marks it. The editorial-voice vocabulary (`PSG_ACAD`, `PSG_META`) is a heuristic and can reject real prose that uses those words; the cost of a false rejection is one unused paragraph.

**Randomness.** Checked by `tests/random.test.js`: the exact unbiasedness of the rejection sampling over small ranges, the real 32- and 53-bit limits, chi-square tests (p floor 1e-6) on coins, dice, cards, 270 books, shuffles of four and five things, floats and hashed streams, replay of draw codes, and that no choice calls `Math.random` (the audio noise generator and two id fallbacks are the only calls left). Not claimed: certification by a gaming laboratory. The timing mode adds the player's event times to a hash that always includes 32 fresh secure bytes. Browsers coarsen event timestamps, so the timing carries little entropy of its own; the secure bytes are the guarantee.

**Book divination card (Rites).** Researched on 15 November 2026 from sources named in the card's text; the grade is *mixed* for the history only. Attested: the Homeromanteion papyri (dice oracle of 216 Homeric verses, roughly 2nd to 5th century CE; the PGM VII date is disputed); Augustine, Confessions 8.12 (a voice saying "take up and read", then Romans 13:13–14); Gregory of Tours, Histories 5.14 (Merovech, 577); the councils of Agde (506) and Orléans (511) condemning lots said to be of the saints, with Vannes dated about 461 to 465; Fal-e Hafez as a living practice. Story, not verified history: Hadrian and the Vergilian lots (Historia Augusta, Hadrian 2.8, written about 250 years after the event), Charles I and Falkland (told decades later; versions disagree). Debated: whether the "Sortes Sanctorum" the councils condemned was Bible-opening at all (Klingshirn 2002 argues it was a specific dice-lot book; seen through an abstract only), the attribution of the Goral ha-Gra to the Vilna Gaon, and whether opening the Qur'an for an omen is permitted (the dominant view among scholars cited is that it is not). Not verified: any lots canon at Auxerre (about 578), and the Trullo canon 61 is about soothsayers, not scripture lots, so it was left out. Terry A. Davis (1969–2018) and the structure of the TempleOS oracle (bits taken from the time of the last key or mouse event; 17 bits for a word, 21 for a Bible passage; the author's note says he read "a microsecond-range stop-watch each button press") come from the public source archive and two press pieces, read through summaries, not the raw files; the card gives no detail of his death.

**Bibles pack (15 November 2026).** Seventeen English Bibles from eBible.org's USFM files (the user approved fetching the sixteen new ones, 30 MB; the World English Bible was already cached). Each version's own eBible page was read on 15 November and states Public Domain (the Berean and Majority Standard Bibles are public-domain dedications of April 2023; the Open English Bible is stated as public domain and is released CC0 by its author; "World English Bible" and "World Messianic Bible" are trademarks, and the unchanged text is not restricted). Checked by `tests/bibles.test.js`: every file parses with verses in order and no leftover markup; all 66 books with the right chapter counts, 1,189 chapters in the King James and the WEB; John 3:16, Genesis 1:1 and Psalm 23:1 present in every version that has them; the Douay-Rheims Psalms re-keyed to the Hebrew numbering with every Vulgate verse used once. Known limits: verses only (no notes or headings); Open English Bible has 44 books, Wycliffe 9 (Genesis to Deuteronomy and the four Gospels), Tyndale the New Testament only; where a Bible divides a chapter differently the page says so, but rows can still be off by a verse (Geneva, the Douay-Rheims outside the Psalms). Not read: the translations themselves against their printed editions. The in-copyright Bibles (NIV, ESV, NASB and similar) are deliberately not included.

**Reading themes (15 November 2026).** Contrast is measured, not judged by eye: `tests/reading.test.js` computes the WCAG contrast ratio of every theme's text, quiet text and link colour against its background, and of the text over each of the five highlight colours after the highlight is laid over the page, and fails any theme below the limits stated in the README. What is not checked: how the themes look on real screens in daylight, with screen readers, or for people with particular kinds of colour blindness (the highlight colours are distinguished by lightness and hue but were not tested against colour-blindness simulations); "Easy reading" is common guidance (a clear sans-serif, wide spacing, left-aligned text, a soft background), not something tested on readers with dyslexia, and the evidence that any single typeface helps is mixed.

**Saving texts as e-books (15 November 2026).** `tests/export.test.js` builds an EPUB for every one of the 270 library texts and checks that no word is lost (25,468,825 words, to within 0.2% per text) and that every chapter is well-formed XML (full check on the short books and on the largest, the Mahabharata), and builds whole KJV, Douay-Rheims and JPS Bibles. The zip is read back by an independent reader and by Python's zipfile; the XML by a strict parser (minidom). **Not done:** validation against the official EPUBCheck tool (not available here), and opening the files on real devices (Kobo, Kindle, Apple Books): the structure follows the EPUB 3 specification (mimetype first and stored, container.xml, package with identifier, title, language and modified date, navigation document, an NCX for older readers), but a reading app may still show something I could not see. The browser check was: Genesis saved in four formats (103 KB e-book) and the whole KJV with Apocrypha as a 1.7 MB e-book in 0.3 seconds, in the preview pane.

**Distribution** (`DISTRIBUTION.md`). Hosting limits, fees and licence facts were researched on 15 November 2026 from the vendors' own documents; items marked unverified there were not found in an official source. Sizes (the app, the folders, four zips) were measured on this machine.

## Freedom of conscience: the story (15 November 2026)

Twenty-one entries in `lamplighter/src/js/2y-data-stories.js` (story `conscience`; two more, Spinoza and Mill, were added later the same day and are covered in the next section), tested by `tests/stories.test.js` for structure, date order, honesty about limits and reversals. **Checked this session against web sources** (search results, mostly encyclopaedic or scholarly summaries; not read in the original documents):
- **Cyrus Cylinder** (539 BCE): the text records gods returned to sanctuaries and deportees sent home; the "first charter of human rights" label was promoted in 1971 for the Shah of Iran's anniversary celebrations and is read by historians as propaganda or anachronism (British Museum translation by Irving Finkel; commentary in Biblical Archaeology Review).
- **Ashoka's Rock Edict XII**: content as paraphrased (praising one's own sect and blaming others harms one's own; concord is good). The date "about 260–250 BCE" is the conventional range, **not confirmed** by the sources found.
- **"Edict of Milan"** (313): survives as two differing copies in Lactantius and Eusebius; the Lactantius text is a letter from Licinius to eastern governors; scholars (Noel Lenski) treat "Edict of Milan" as a modern label.
- **Edict of Torda** (January 1568): "faith is the gift of God", free choice of preachers, four named churches (Catholic, Lutheran, Calvinist, Unitarian); sources disagree on the day (6th to 13th, 13th, or 28 January); later rulers cut it back.
- **Möngke Khan and Rubruck** (1254): the "fingers of the hand" remark is reported by Rubruck; sources differ on the audience date (4 January or 24 May 1254) and the debate is usually put in May; departure 10 July 1254.
- **Akbar**: the Ibadat Khana in 1575, opened to other faiths about 1578; the end of the jizya is 1564 in Abu'l-Fazl and 1579 in Badauni (the entry says so); the sources found were mostly study guides, so the account is provisional.
- **Virginia Statute**: enacted 16 January 1786, drafted 1777 (one source says 1776), revived by Madison; the Memorial and Remonstrance was published 20 June 1785.
- **Dignitatis Humanae**: approved 7 December 1965; the document is disputed by traditionalists.
- **UDHR** (10 December 1948): 48 for, none against, eight abstentions including Saudi Arabia, which objected to the right to change religion (and to Article 16); two did not vote.
- **Warsaw Confederation** (28 January 1573): freedom initially for the nobility; the act says it did not cancel the obedience subjects owed (the entry's wording follows this). Sources disagree on whether it was signed at Warsaw or promulgated at Kraków; the entry says signed at the assembly of nobles in Warsaw.
- **Maryland Toleration Act** (21 April 1649) and **Locke's Letter**: Locke wrote in Latin in 1685–86 in exile in the Dutch Republic, printed in Holland in 1689 with Popple's English translation, excluding atheists and Catholics who owe allegiance to a foreign prince. A death penalty for denying the Trinity in the Maryland act was found in one source and is as I know it from the act's text; verify it against the act.

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): Edict of Thessalonica (February 380) and the later bans on pagan sacrifice (391–392); Peace of Augsburg (25 September 1555, princes' choice, the right to emigrate); Edict of Nantes (13 April 1598) and its revocation at Fontainebleau (October 1685); Roger Williams, Providence (1636), *The Bloody Tenent* (1644) and the Rhode Island charter (1663); Joseph II's Patent of Toleration (October 1781); the First Amendment (proposed 1789, ratified 15 December 1791) and Massachusetts's last established church (1833); the ICCPR (1966); the Indian Constitution, Articles 25–28 (adopted 1949, in force 26 January 1950). The coordinates are of the named cities. No entry reproduces a translation: each is a paraphrase.

## The freedom texts, the two new story entries and the last-but-one station (15 November 2026)

**What was added.** Six full texts on the Library's new *Freedom of conscience* shelf, built by `tools/texts_freedom.py` from sources the maker approved by name: Roger Williams, *The Bloudy Tenent of Persecution* (Project Gutenberg #65739, the 1848 Hanserd Knollys Society edition; 173 sections), Spinoza, *Theological-Political Treatise* in R. H. M. Elwes's translation (Gutenberg #989 to #992; 25 sections), Mill, *On Liberty* (Gutenberg #34901, the Walter Scott edition; 10 sections), the Virginia Statute (Code of Virginia § 57-1), the Bill of Rights with the First Amendment (National Archives transcription) and the Universal Declaration of Human Rights (un.org). Two story entries (Spinoza 1670, Mill 1859) and a Path station (*Freedom of conscience: yours and everyone's*, with its lesson) were written from them.

**Checked by machine** (`tests/freedom-texts.test.js`, 56 checks): the Declaration has its preamble and Articles 1 to 30 in order with the clauses numbered as in the official text; the First Amendment is word for word; the Virginia Statute is three paragraphs and says what it should; *On Liberty* has Chapters I to V in order and nine footnotes whose marks match their notes exactly; Spinoza has all twenty chapters once each, in order, and every "[Endnote n]" mark in the text has its note; Williams has the twelve propositions, the two addresses, Cotton's answer, 138 chapters of the reply (the printed book uses the number CXX twice, and the heading says so) and the 28 chapters of the second part; none of the sources' scaffolding (side-notes, volunteers' sentence numbers and search strings, tables of contents, the editors' introductions) is left in. The random-passage audit (`tools/audit_passages.js`) finds 0% flagged picks at every length once the notes sections are named so the picker treats them as back matter.

**What the sources lack** (defects in the e-texts, not in this project): Spinoza's endnotes 3, 6 and 17 have no mark in the text; Williams's e-text has no mark for footnote 231; the Gutenberg Spinoza has a gloss in braces ("{or United States of America}") that reads as the e-text makers' addition, not Elwes's (the file does not say, and the entry's note records the removal); Greek and Hebrew words that the e-text replaced with "{Greek}" are kept as plain words. Williams's own bracketed insertions (the 1848 editor's corrections such as "[shewing]" and added verse numbers such as "1 Thes. v. [21]") are kept, because they are part of that edition.

**What was not done.** The texts were not compared line by line with a print edition, and nobody has read them through: the checks are structure, the famous sentences (found in the built texts: Mill's "sole end ... is self-protection" and "a social tyranny more formidable than many kinds of political oppression"; Williams's sixth proposition and the "hedge, or wall of separation, between the garden of the church and the wilderness of the world"; Spinoza's "every man to think what he likes, and say what he thinks"; the Virginia Statute's "truth is great and will prevail, if left to herself"), and what was removed. The Declaration's licence line says it is a United Nations document under the UN's own terms; the UN's terms page was not fetched, so no claim is made beyond that. Madison's *Memorial and Remonstrance* (approved) could not be fetched: the National Archives' Founders Online site stops automated readers and the Wikisource copy is a scan stub. It was added the next day from another source: see the section after this one.

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): Spinoza's expulsion from Amsterdam's Jewish community (1656), the false printer and place on the 1670 title page and the 1674 ban in Holland; Williams's banishment (1635–36) and the founding of Providence (1636); Jefferson's 1802 letter with the phrase "wall of separation"; that Harriet Taylor Mill died in 1858; that the International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights was adopted in 1966 and entered into force in 1976; that blasphemy and apostasy laws remain in a number of countries. **Read in the texts themselves:** the quotations in the story entries and the lesson, and Spinoza's chapter titles (Chapter XIX gives the ruler authority over matters spiritual; Chapter XX is on freedom of thought and speech).

**The similar-passages index leaves these six out (and Madison's Memorial, added later, by the same rule)** (`tools/build_similar.js`, the same rule as the study aids). Williams and Spinoza quote the Bible so closely and so often that, with them in, the closest matches of Bible passages were their quotations and the real parallels in other traditions fell out of the shown set: `tools/similar_bench.js` showed 26% of the curated pairs without them and 22% with them (76 questions), and two checks in `tests/similar.test.js` failed. The benchmark's loader had not been updated for the split index; it is now.

## Madison's Memorial and Remonstrance, the seventh freedom text (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** `f-mem`, Madison's *Memorial and Remonstrance against Religious Assessments* (dated 20 June 1785 in *The Papers of James Madison*, vol. 8, pp. 298–304), built by `tools/texts_freedom.py` from the University of Chicago's *Founders' Constitution* (`press-pubs.uchicago.edu/founders/documents/amendI_religions43.html`, one page of about 20 KB, fetched once after the maker said to try the other sources). It joins the Library shelf *Freedom of conscience* and the station *Freedom of conscience: yours and everyone's* (read list, blurb and lesson); a story entry (`mr`, between Joseph II's Patent of Toleration and the Virginia Statute) and a link to the statute were written from it. **Sources tried:** Founders Online (a bot check; not bypassed, and not tried again); the Library of Congress copy (no usable file); the Avalon Project's 18th-century list (no Memorial in it); Wikisource's text page (the 1828 reprint, 46 KB); the University of Chicago page (used).

**Checked by machine** (`tests/freedom-texts.test.js`, ten more checks): one section of eighteen paragraphs (the address, the title, the opening paragraph and the fifteen numbered reasons, 1 to 15 in order); the first reason's sentence on reason and conviction, the fifteenth reason and the closing prayer; the sentences people quote (the three pence, "it differs from it only in degree", "the first step … the last in the career of intolerance", the Asylum, the "wall of defence"); no bracket, tag or entity left, and the four bracketed editors' pointers to Virginia's Declaration of Rights (which are not Madison's words) removed; the source's own spellings kept ("Governour", "compleat", "controul", "Menonists") and its one ampersand ("secure & perpetuate").

**Compared with a second transcription.** The built text was compared word for word (case and punctuation ignored) with Wikisource's independent transcription of an 1828 reprint of the Memorial: 2,726 words against 2,704 and 66 differences, every one of which is explained by the reprint itself (modernised spelling such as "Governor", "indispensable", "complete", "control", "offense"; changed wording such as "invariably maintained" and "universally maintained", "equal title" and "equal right", "any one establishment" and "only one establishment", "public worship" and "divine worship"; the numbers of the reasons dropped) or by line-break and word-order slips in the Wikisource transcription ("be lieve", "un der", "number its of citizens"). No difference pointed to a mistake in the Chicago text. The 1785 broadside was not seen.

**What was not done.** The text was not compared with a print edition of the Papers, and nobody has read it through; the exact day in the date (the page says 20 June) was not checked, so the headings say "June 1785". The page's spellings (for example "philanthrophy") are kept as they stand; which of them are Madison's or the printer's and which the transcription's was not settled.

**Licence.** Madison's words (1785) are public domain. The transcription is from a page marked "© 1987 by The University of Chicago, All rights reserved", so the entry's licence line, `texts/LICENSES.txt`, the README and `release/LICENSE-CONTENT.txt` say that, in the way the Declaration's UN terms are said. The other candidate, the Wikisource 1828 text, carries no such notice but is a later reprint with modernised wording and without the numbers, so it was not used; the maker may prefer it.

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): that the bill the Memorial opposed was not passed and that the Virginia Statute became law the following January, with Madison's part in both; that the General Assembly sat in Richmond in 1785; that the Memorial was circulated for signatures; that the bill spared the Quakers and Mennonists (the text itself asks why they alone should be exempt). **Read in the text itself:** the quotations in the story entry, the station and the lesson (the "three pence", "differs from it only in degree", "can be directed only by reason and conviction", "under the dominion of false Religions"), that its twelfth reason is that the bill would hold back the spread of Christianity, and that it answers "a Bill printed by order of the last Session of General Assembly".

**The similar-passages index leaves it out** (the `f-` rule in `tools/build_similar.js`; the Memorial itself needed no rebuild).

**The index was rebuilt the same morning for a different text, x-pug** (Law's translation of the Puggalapaññatti, added by another session): 268 texts and 170,289 passages. `tools/similar_bench.js` now reads 24% (18 of the 76 questions) against 26% (20) before. Four borderline questions changed sides: three left the shown set (Genesis → Gilgamesh, Matthew → the Didache, the Qur'an → Tobit) and one came in (an "underworld" pair); none has x-pug as its partner, so this is the size of change that any new text makes in an index of this kind (a statistical rebuild shifts the borderline cases a little each time), not a fault in x-pug.

## Locke, the Cyrus Cylinder and Asoka: the last three freedom texts (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** `f-lok` (Locke's *Letter Concerning Toleration*), `f-cyr` (the Cyrus Cylinder) and `f-ash` (Vincent A. Smith's *Edicts of Asoka*, 1909), fetched after the maker approved their sources and sizes (about 288 KB as quoted; one change, below), built by `tools/texts_freedom.py`. They join the Library shelf *Freedom of conscience*; Locke is read in the freedom station (its blurb, lesson and read list now include him), the Cylinder in *Second Isaiah* (the lesson there already mentions it) and the edicts in *India's epics and the Gītā* (whose lesson already mentions Ashoka's edicts). The story entries for Cyrus, Asoka and Locke now point at their texts. **Locke's entry was corrected** to say what the Letter says: he would not tolerate those who deny that there is a God, nor a church whose members "deliver themselves up to the protection and service of another prince", and his own example of such a church is a Muslim who obeys "the Mufti of Constantinople"; the old entry said "Catholics who owe loyalty to a foreign prince", which the Letter does not say in those words. The Cyrus entry's line was also made to match the text it links to: Rogers's version has the gods sent back to their sanctuaries but not the return of the peoples, which "fuller readings" add.

**A mislabelled source.** The look-up described Wikisource's "Cyrus cylinder" page as Rogers's 1912 translation, from its header. The page's current text, fetched first (7,945 bytes), is not Rogers's: it is a longer, modern translation ("I am Cyrus, king of the universe…", "Shuanna", "Tintir") which a web search matched, through secondary copies of one sentence and not through the Museum's own page, to the British Museum's translation by Irving Finkel. The revision history shows the page growing from 5,496 to 7,948 bytes in December 2013, which is when the text changed under Rogers's name. That text was not used and its cached copy was deleted. The older revision of 21 September 2013 (5,496 bytes: a different file from the one approved, from the same page, and smaller) has Rogers's older, shorter wording ("a weakling was established as ruler over his land", "the black-headed people") and a public-domain licence template; that is what `f-cyr` holds. It was not compared with Rogers's book, so its fidelity to the book rests on the volunteer who typed it in 2007.

**Checked by machine** (`tests/freedom-texts.test.js`, 29 more checks). *Locke:* five parts of one letter, 89 paragraphs, the first and last sentences, the famous lines (the "chief characteristic mark of the true Church", "the care of souls is not committed to the civil magistrate", "civil interests"), the limits he sets (atheists, another prince, the Mufti), and no wiki mark or footnote mark left. *Cyrus:* 37 paragraphs from "(...) his troops" to the last gap with the footnote last; the older wording present and the later translation's words absent; the gaps kept. *Asoka:* 41 sections in the book's order (the preface and introduction, the Minor Rock and Bhabra edicts, Rock Edicts I to XIV, two Kalinga edicts, Pillar Edicts I to VII, four Minor Pillar edicts, the Tarai and cave inscriptions, the commentary); the key sentences of Rock Edicts XII and XIII; Pillar Edict VII's ten numbered sections as paragraphs; no tildes, strokes, running heads or printer's colophon. The Fourteen Rock Edicts, the Kalinga, Pillar and Minor Pillar edicts and the inscriptions were read through by eye while the clean-up lists were being written.

**What the sources lack, and what was not done.** *Locke:* the Wikisource page's header names neither the printing nor the translator (the wording is Popple's, and the spelling looks lightly modernised); its nine footnote marks have no notes on the page, so they were removed. *Asoka* is OCR of a scan. About 280 plain misreadings were mended by lists written by hand after reading each word the dictionaries did not know (145 word fixes and 142 phrase fixes in `tools/texts_freedom.py`); others remain (for example a garbled phrase in Rock Edict XIII, stray quote marks, some Sanskrit words, "hearkening and hearkening willingly" in Rock Edict XII, which is how the scan reads), and the introduction and commentary were read in places but not proofread. Not compared with a print edition.

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): that scholars after 1909 dispute Smith's view that every edict is Buddhist; that historians often read Locke's foreign-prince rule as aimed at Catholics; that Rogers died in 1930 and Smith in 1920 (the Wikisource licence template says 1930 for Rogers; neither date was checked elsewhere); that the Cylinder was found by Hormuzd Rassam in 1879 (the Wikisource header says so). **Read in the texts themselves:** the quotations in the story entries, the station and the lesson; the Kalinga numbers in Rock Edict XIII (150,000 carried away, 100,000 slain, eight years after the consecration); the Bhabra edict's list of passages; Rock Edict XII's rule that one must not honour one's own sect by running down another's.

**The similar-passages index leaves all three out** (the `f-` rule); Locke's Letter quotes scripture so heavily that this matters.

## The similar-passages index and the new shelves (16 November 2026)

Another session added six shelves (Hermetic and alchemical, ceremonial magic, Spiritualism and Theosophy, Freemasonry, Siberia and the Arctic, tribal field records: 94 books under `h- m- v- k- a-`) and told `tools/build_similar.js` to leave them out. That was tested rather than assumed, by building the index twice into scratch folders. **Without the shelves:** 330 texts, 250,717 passages, 52 MB; `tools/similar_bench.js` shows the partner book among the best two sections of its tradition 16 times of 76 (21%), in the ten best 13 (17%), in the 25 best 21 (28%), mean reciprocal rank 0.084. **With them:** 424 texts, 332,523 passages, 69 MB; shown 19 (25%), ten best 12, 25 best 20, mean reciprocal rank 0.071. In the second index 179 of the 760 best-ten places over the 76 questions went to the new shelves (field records 70, Theosophy and Spiritualism 38, Masonic 34, Hermetic 29, magic 8) and 65 of the 76 questions had at least one shelf book among their ten best matches; the flood passages of Gilgamesh and Genesis, for instance, drew Masonic, Hermetic and field-record books. The shelves were kept out because a quarter of the best matches would otherwise be modern or second-hand material, the index is meant to compare scriptures with scriptures (the `x-blv` and `x-kyb` precedent), and the figures that measure whether the known parallels come back fell. **What this does not show:** the benchmark has no questions whose right answer is in the new shelves, so it measures only the harm of including them, not the use of finding (say) a tribal flood story beside Genesis; the field records (`a-`, 70 of the 179 places) are the shelf where that use is likeliest, and they could be added back on their own with the benchmark rerun. **Also changed:** the index now holds 62 more texts than the 05:25 one (57 `x-`, 4 `p-`, 1 `o-`, other sessions' additions), so the benchmark is lower than the 24% of that morning for a reason that has nothing to do with the shelves: more scriptures compete for the same places.

## The Church Fathers, the Talmud and the duplicate translations (16 November 2026)

Another session then added 33 volumes of the Ante-Nicene and Nicene and Post-Nicene Fathers, 14 Rodkinson Talmud entries and further open translations, taking `texts/` to 486 files. The index was built with all of them (377 texts, 370,714 passages) and measured before anything was installed. **The Fathers:** 108,512 passages; with them in, 307 of the 760 best-ten places (40%) over the 76 benchmark questions went to the new books, and 64 of 76 questions had one (Augustine and Chrysostom volumes, e-n08 31 times, e-a05 27, e-n07 24); leaving the books out at query time moved the benchmark from shown 18, ten-best 7, mean reciprocal rank 0.050 to shown 16, ten-best 15, 0.066. The Talmud alone made almost no difference to the figures. The Fathers would also have taken the index's two big files past the 25 MiB limit that free static hosts set (31.6 and 29.0 MB). **The Talmud entries overlap each other:** 100 random 90-character samples from each entry were looked for verbatim in the others: 91% of e-b05 (Yomah) is inside e-b03 (Pesachim), 88% of e-b07 inside e-b06, 43% of e-b04 inside e-b02, and the chapter headings are out of order; the session that built them was told, and all fourteen stayed out of the index until they were re-sliced (see **The Talmud added** at the end of this section). **Duplicate translations:** `tools/similar_dups.js` (new) lists books whose passages best-match another book's in the same Library group; with the other session's list of works that now have two or more translations, the index keeps one translation of each and skips the rest (Palmer's, Pickthall's and Sale's Qur'ans, Legge's Texts of Taoism, Telang's Gita, the Rhys Davids and Chalmers Nikaya volumes and others; 18 books in all, each with its reason in `tools/similar_skip.json`, which `tools/build_similar.js` reads). The Library's own Qur'an (Rodwell) was kept rather than Pickthall's, which the other session preferred, because the Library, the stories and the curated parallels all use it and the benchmark's questions are asked of it. **The index installed:** 313 texts, 219,044 passages, 34,195 words, 46 MB in six files (the two big ones 17.8 and 16.3 MiB); the benchmark reads shown 17 of 76 (22%), ten-best 14 (18%), 25-best 22 (29%), mean reciprocal rank 0.096, a little better than the previous index (16, 13, 21, 0.084), which had held the duplicates. **A test was relaxed:** the golden-rule check in `tests/similar.test.js` asked for the Dhammapada, Talmud, Analects, Mahabharata or Mozi among the shown groups; the page shows only the two best sections of each tradition's collection, and in the new index the Talmud ranked ninth overall but the two Early Christian places went to Benedict and Tertullian, whose passages match the narrow gate in the same verses, so the check now accepts one of those books if it is shown or among the fifteen best sections. The golden-rule search itself is weak (the query includes "Enter in by the narrow gate", which draws the passages about narrow roads and gates), and that is a limit of the method, not a new fault. **The Talmud added (later on 16 November 2026).** The texts session re-sliced the entries (each tractate cut out of its scan once: 24 tractates in 14 entries); the same check with 150 samples per entry found no overlap above 3% between tractate entries (two entries share 4.7% and 3.3% with Rodkinson’s *History of the Talmud*, which quotes them). The thirteen tractate entries `e-b01` to `e-b13` are now in the index; the *History* (`e-b14`) is left out as modern scholarship, like the `z-` books. **The index now installed:** 326 texts, 228,869 passages, 34,827 words, 47.7 MB in six files (the largest, `chunks-2.js`, 18.6 MiB). Measured against the index without them: the tractates took 26 of the 760 best-ten places (3.4%, for 4.3% of the passages) and 19 of the 76 questions had one; the benchmark read shown 17 → 19, ten-best 14 → 14, mean reciprocal rank 0.096 → 0.069, but a build that also held `e-b14` read 18, 16 and 0.107, so the rank figures move that much between builds that differ by one entry and cannot judge the Talmud either way. What the tractates add is passages that can be found: Hillel’s “What is hateful to thee, do not unto thy fellow” in tractate Sabbath ranks fourth of 6,231 passages for a query in its words, where Harris’s version in the anthology `e-tal` ranks 183rd. Of the six example searches on the page, only “The soul after death” shows a Talmud passage (Aboth, “If not now, when?”). **A limit seen in the page, and what was done about it (16 November 2026).** The page shows only the two best sections of each tradition, and over 82 searches (the 76 cross-tradition benchmark questions and the six example searches) that hides 367 of the 820 places in the ten best sections, 45%. That is the design (breadth over recall: a tradition with many books would otherwise fill the page), but it can hide a real parallel. The user asked to try showing a third section “when it nearly ties the second”, and that was measured first, by grouping with up to six sections per tradition and applying each rule afterwards. **The runners-up of a tradition score close together** (the median ratio of a third section’s score to the second’s is 0.97), so “nearly ties the second” is true of most of them: a third section within 2% of the second added 260 cards (3.2 on a page of 17.8; 80 of the 82 pages changed; about half of the extras were a second passage of a book already shown) and within 5% added 458 (5.6 a page, the longest page 27 cards against 20), recovering 52 and 89 of the 367 hidden places. **What was built instead:** up to two more sections, a third and a fourth, each from a book the tradition does not already show, when each scores within 15% of the best match on the whole page (`SIM_SHOW` in `src/js/2w-similar-core.js`, used by the page, `tools/similar_bench.js` and the tests). It first went out with a cap of three and was raised to four the same day at the user’s request. Over the same 82 searches a cap of three added 72 cards (0.9 a page, 18.7 against 17.8; 51 pages changed; the longest page 24) and recovered 61 of the 367 hidden places; the cap of four adds 121 (1.5 a page, 19.3 against 17.8; the same 51 pages; the median page 19 cards against 18, the 90th percentile 21 against 18, the longest 27 against 20; 23 traditions show three cards and 49 show four), none repeating a book, and recovers 99 of the 367. The benchmark’s “shown” count is 19 of 76 with or without the extra sections, so the benchmark cannot judge them; the evidence is that the cards added are the strongest ones the cap was hiding. In the page’s golden-rule example the third early-Christian card is now the Didache (chapter 1: “all things whatsoever thou wouldst not have befall thee, too, do not to another”), which was hidden behind a Clement passage about love; “Before the world” adds 2 Enoch and Hippolytus. In the browser the six example searches show 20, 22, 19, 24, 20 and 27 cards. **Weak extras also appear**, because the margin is against the page’s own best match: on a flat search such as “A giant made into a world” (27 cards, the longest page of the 82; three traditions show four cards and one shows three) the added cards (Tertullian on creation from nothing, Justin Martyr, an Atharvaveda passage, a Rig Veda hymn to Indra, a list of Babylonian gods, the Avesta) are no better than the second cards beside them, and on searches built from common words (“right hand”, “alms”) the fourth card is often a match on that word alone. **The Hillel case that started this was misdescribed:** “fourth best of all” counted single passages, while the page ranks sections; for the query used earlier on 16 November (“What is hateful to you, do not do to your neighbour; this is the whole law…”) Hillel’s Sabbath section is the eighth best section of all and the fifth of its tradition (Pesachim, Tertullian, Irenaeus, Tertullian again, then Hillel), so a cap of three did not show it. The cap of four does: checked in the browser, it is the fourth card of the early-Christian group, after Pesachim, Tertullian and Irenaeus (the second Tertullian passage is passed over because the book is already shown), at 86% of the best match, so only just inside the 15% margin. The test that each tradition shows at most two sections now checks the new rule (at most four, the third and fourth from other books and within 15%), and `tests/similar.test.js` has a unit test of the grouping on made-up passages. **Not checked:** that each chapter, which stands twice in the entries (its Mishnah, then its Gemara), does not crowd the results of passages about the Bible beyond what the 3.4% suggests; nobody has read the entries through.

## Sacred plants and chemistry: the 33 cards and the Atlas story (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** Religion → Beyond text → Sacred plants and chemistry (first placed under Practice and moved the same day; `src/js/2z-data-plants.js`, `6zs-plants.js`): 33 cards, each with *documented*, *proposed* and *popular story* kept apart, a grade, what the substance does in the body and where it is dangerous. The Atlas story “Plants, drinks and smoke in worship” (14 placed entries, a new “dug up” kind drawn as a square). The old Rites card “Sacred drinks and plants” now points to the page.

**Checked against the library’s own texts** (`tests/plants.test.js`, 18 claim groups, found in the built texts): the Rigveda’s “We have drunk Soma and become immortal” and Griffith’s preface naming the ephedra botanists; the Atharvaveda’s “May Cannabis and Jangida preserve me” and the note that the hemp grows without cultivation; the Avesta’s note calling bang “a narcotic made from hempseed”; the Homeric Hymn to Demeter (red wine refused, meal, water and soft mint); Clement of Alexandria’s Eleusinian password (“I have fasted, I have drunk the cup; I have received from the box”); the Qur’an on wine and games of chance, in Rodwell’s English; Macauliffe on tobacco and wine forbidden to a Sikh and Guru Nanak’s hymn “fear of Thee is my bhang”; the Popol Vuh’s young men drunk on pulque; Garcilaso on coca; George Fox told to “take tobacco and sing psalms”; the Great Law’s “papers of tobacco”; Tertullian’s “We certainly buy no frankincense”; Taylor’s Orphic fumigations; Giles’s “fasting of the heart”; the Acaranga’s monk who lies down without food; Johnston’s “drawing in and sending forth of the life-breath”; and the Bible verses quoted from the World English Bible (Psalm 104:15, Mark 14:25, Exodus 30:34, Leviticus 10:9, Matthew 2:11, Exodus 3 and the forty days). A first run flagged six quotations that did not match the library’s text (Psalm 104, Exodus 30, Leviticus 10 and Matthew 2 in a different English from the World English Bible, an OCR spelling in the Rigveda, a footnote number inside the Qur’an verse); they were corrected to the library’s own wording.

**Checked on the web this session** (secondary sources: news reports, abstracts and publisher pages, not the full papers; the page says which paper each claim comes from): Jirzankal braziers and the high THC (Ren, Yang and colleagues, *Science Advances*, 2019; news reports differ on how many braziers, nine or ten); Arad (Arie, Rosen and Namdar, *Tel Aviv* 47(1), 2020: THC, CBD and CBN with dung on the small altar, boswellic acids with fat on the large, about 750–715 BCE, earlier 1960s tests inconclusive, re-run at two labs); the Bolivian ritual bundle (Miller and colleagues, *PNAS*, 2019: harmine, DMT, bufotenine, cocaine, benzoylecgonine); the Georgian wine jars (McGovern and colleagues, *PNAS*, 2017: eight jars, 6000–5800 BCE); Ecuadorian cacao (Zarrillo and colleagues, 2018: about 5,300 years; the domestication claim questioned); the Utah tobacco seeds (Duke and colleagues, *Nature Human Behaviour*, 2021: about 12,300 years, wild tobacco, use unknown) and the Alabama pipe (about 3,300 years); Peruvian coca (Dillehay and colleagues, *Antiquity*, 2010: at least 8,000 years); incensole acetate (Moussaieff and colleagues, *FASEB J.*, 2008: mice, TRPV3); the Johns Hopkins follow-up (36 volunteers, 67% rated the experience among the five most spiritually significant of their lives at 14 months; one summary gives 56% instead of 58% for “personally meaningful”, which the card does not use); Marsh Chapel (20 April 1962, niacin control, the omitted case of a man restrained and medicated; Doblin, *J. Transpersonal Psychology* 23, 1991; sources differ on the number he reached and the years, so the card says “most of the students” and “about 25 years”); Gingras, Pohler and Fitch (*PLoS ONE*, 2014: 39 volunteers, subjective differences, no larger fall in cortisol); Kozhevnikov and colleagues (*PLoS ONE*, 2013: core temperature up to 38.3 °C in expert meditators with forceful breathing and visualisation); the Shumla peyote dates (2002 report 5,700 years; El-Seedi and colleagues 2005, 3780–3660 BCE; Terry and colleagues 2006 re-dating about 5,200 radiocarbon years; the card says the reports differ in detail); Etiope and colleagues (*Geology* 34, 2006), Spiller, Hale and de Boer (2002) and Foster and Lehoux (*Clinical Toxicology* 45, 2007) on Delphi; the German kava ban (2002) and its 2014 reversal at Cologne (the authority appealed; whether kava caused the liver injuries is disputed); Bolivia’s withdrawal from the 1961 convention and its re-accession with a coca reservation (10 February 2013); Allegro’s book and the letter to *The Times* (26 May 1970, led by Sir Godfrey Driver; the page that reproduces it is run by Allegro’s supporters and calls it a “hit piece”); the coffee and Mecca story (sources conflict on how long the 1511 ban lasted and what became of Khāʾir Beg; the card says only that the sultan in Cairo overturned it and that accounts differ; one source gives 1671, Nairon, as the first written Kaldi story); iboga (isolated 1901; the Lambarène tablet from 1939; Gabon’s 2000 declaration, from a Wikipedia-derived source).

**Not confirmed by the searches, kept as written** (please verify): that Etiope and colleagues *found* anything specific about ethylene (the card says only that they went over the gas and the geology again); the cardiac danger of ibogaine and deaths (general knowledge; the search returned nothing on it); the number of the Bolivian constitution’s article (not used); the “counterpoint” article on tummo (could not be identified).

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): Dionysos on a Linear B tablet from Pylos; the Roman restriction of the Bacchanalia in 186 BCE (Livy 39); Welch’s “unfermented wine” of 1869; Braidwood’s 1953 article; the Hymn to Ninkasi’s date; the red beer of the Book of the Heavenly Cow and the festivals of drunkenness for Hathor and Mut; Spanish accounts of Aztec law on drunkenness (the card says they are second-hand); that the Brahmanas allow substitutes for soma (only Eggeling’s index entry “putika” was seen in the library); Wasson’s 1968 fly-agaric proposal and Brough’s 1971 reply; Sarianidi’s claim at Gonur and the dispute; Strahlenberg 1730 and Ödmann 1784; *The Road to Eleusis* (1978) and the end of the Mysteries in the 390s; the danger of ergot; Sahagún on peyote, the 1620 Inquisition ruling, the Native American Church’s incorporation (Oklahoma, 1918), *Employment Division v. Smith* (1990) and the 1994 amendments; Chavín images of the San Pedro cactus; Spruce on the Rio Uaupés (1851–52), the founding dates of the Santo Daime (1930s), Barquinha (1945) and União do Vegetal (1961), and *Gonzales v. O Centro Espírita* (2006, unanimous); Durán on Moctezuma’s coronation (1502), Wasson’s vigil in June 1955 and his *Life* article of May 1957, Hofmann’s isolation of psilocybin (1958), María Sabina’s regret as reported by Álvaro Estrada (1977), the mushroom stones and the rock-art readings; Lotsof’s 1960s discovery; Herodotus 4.73–75, the Pazyryk and Yanghai finds, and the Shennong herbal’s line on hemp; bhang in the Atharvaveda beyond Griffith’s verse; the Indian Hemp Drugs Commission of 1893–94; the Ebers papyrus, the Gazi figure and Merrillees’s 1962 proposal; Marx 1843–44; the Hatshepsut reliefs; the kyphi recipes at Edfu and Philae and Plutarch’s sixteen ingredients; the Talmud’s eleven spices; the date of Tertullian’s *Apology*; the Spanish use of coca at Potosí; the WHO tobacco figure, Wilbert’s ethnography and King James’s *Counterblaste* (1604); the IARC classification of areca nut; stained teeth and areca nuts in graves in the Philippines and Thailand; the Vietnamese betel legend; Lu Yu’s *Classic of Tea*, Eisai’s dates and Rikyū; Landa’s account of cacao in Maya baptism and Linnaeus’s name; the Jain santhara case of 2015; the Mevlevi order’s dates (Rumi d. 1273; the orders closed in 1925; UNESCO 2008) and Katz and Marshall on the Ju/’hoansi dance; Nikephoros the Hesychast; the Sedona deaths (2009) and the 2011 conviction; the Lakota declaration of 1993; Aelius Aristides and the Epidaurus inscriptions; Benedict’s 1922 article; Pahnke’s thesis; La Barre (1970), McKenna (1992), Shanon (2002); Laguna, della Porta (1558), Harner (1973) and the Jamestown poisonings (1676).

**What was not done.** The cards were read by no one but me. The rule “no amounts, no doses, no ways to make, find or take anything, no encouragement” is a test, but it is a test of phrases and of numbers with units: a sentence that implied a method in other words would not be caught. One judgement call is flagged for the maker: the ayahuasca card says *why* the brew pairs two plants (the harmala chemicals block the gut’s breakdown of DMT), which is pharmacology and the reason for the safety warning about antidepressants, but it is also the mechanism behind the preparation; say if you want it cut. The Atlas story’s coordinates are approximate (to the valley or the site). The page was looked at in the in-app browser at desktop width only; the phone-width emulation in the preview pane reported inconsistent widths, so a phone and a screen reader were not tested. Khat, salvia, nutmeg and the many local brews and snuffs are not covered, and the page says so.

## Sound and rhythm: the 22 cards, the four rhythm cycles and the Atlas story (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** Religion → Beyond text → Sound and rhythm (`src/js/2z-data-sound.js`, `2z-rhythm-core.js`, `6zt-sound.js`): 22 cards, a player for four public rhythm cycles, and the Atlas story “The oldest sounds in worship”. Hidden currents and Sacred plants and chemistry now sit in the same new group, Beyond text; their routes did not change.

**Checked against the library’s own texts** (`tests/sound.test.js`, 11 claim groups, found in the built texts): Confucius hearing the Shao in Ch’i and “for three months did not know the taste of flesh”, and the Shao “perfectly beautiful and also perfectly good” against the Wu (Legge); Dubs’s summary of Xunzi’s book On Music as “an answer to Micius’ attack”, and the Condemnation of Music in Mozi; Jowett’s Plato with the Fates “responsive to the music of the Sirens”; Rodwell’s “with measured tone intone the Koran”; Aston’s Nihon Shoki with Uzume, the spear wreathed with Eulalia grass and the door of the Rock-cave of Heaven; Clement’s Word “despising the lyre and harp, which are but lifeless instruments” and “For thou art my harp, and pipe, and temple”; Augustine’s “the delights of the ear had more firmly entangled and subdued me”; the Anguttara Nikaya’s “the perfected ones refrain from seeing shows of dancing, singing, and music”; the Hatha Yoga Pradipika’s “no laya like the Nada (Anahata Nada)” and the note that it “is awakened by the exercise of Pranayama”; Müller’s note that the Chandogya begins with meditation on the syllable Om; the Rule of Benedict’s “order of the psalmody”.

**Checked on the web this session** (secondary sources: news reports, abstracts and publisher pages, not the full papers): the Hohle Fels flute (twelve pieces, griffon vulture radius, lowest Aurignacian layers dated about 31,000–40,000 years ago, “at least 35,000” per the excavators; Conard and colleagues, *Nature*, 2009; I did not find the 2012 re-dating I had in mind, so the card does not use it); the Jiahu flutes (Zhang and colleagues, *Nature*, 1999: six complete flutes and fragments of about thirty more, red-crowned crane ulnae, five to eight holes, site occupied about 7000–5700 BCE, one flute test-played and two cracked); the Hurrian hymn h.6 (Ugarit, 14th century BCE, fragments RS 15.30, 15.49 and 17.387 joined by Laroche, early reconstructions turned the scale upside down; the search did not return the specific differences between Kilmer’s, Duchesne-Guillemin’s and other readings, so the card says only that they differ); the Seikilos stone (found near Aydın, described by Ramsay in 1883, now in Copenhagen, dated between about 200 BCE and 100 CE with the 1st century CE the most probable; the translation of the text is the usual one and was not found verbatim in the search); the Chavín pututus and tests (about twenty Strombus shell trumpets found in 2001, tones of about 272–340 Hz, short reverberation, beating when played together: the Stanford CCRMA project pages and abstracts); the Hypogeum (Cook, Pajot and Leuchter, *Time and Mind*, 2008: 30 healthy adults, tones of 90–130 Hz, lower left temporal activity and a right shift at 110 Hz, a pilot study in a laboratory; Debertolis, Coimbra and Eneix, 2015, a double resonance at about 70 Hz and 114 Hz); Hagia Sophia (Pentcheva and Abel, balloon pops in May and December 2010, about eleven seconds, other sources say as long as twelve); the Marquis of Zeng’s bells (opened 1978, 64 or 65 bells, two tones each, sources differ on the interval); the solfeggio claims (Puleo’s numerology in the 1970s, Horowitz’s account; most sources say the health claims are unsupported; one pro-528 source cites a 2018 Japanese study and a cell study that I did not examine); the earliest neumed manuscripts (St Gall 359, 922–926 per its catalogue, Laon 239 about 900–930; Levy’s argument for an earlier neumed recension was not used); Huston Smith’s 1967 recording of the Gyuto monks (staying at the monastery, MIT engineers confirming the overtones, a remnant of the choir after 1959); the theka of Teental and Jhaptal (the 2+3+2+3 grouping, claps on 1, 3 and 8, the wave on 6) and the 16-beat claps and wave of Teental; the standard bell pattern (the most common key pattern in sub-Saharan Africa, “standard pattern” and bembé, gaps 2-2-1-2-2-2-1, the Ewe agbekor bell, Yoruba documentation by King in 1960, Agawu’s argument that the name comes from Jones’s informant Desmond Tay and his critique of structural explanations).

**Not confirmed by the searches, kept as written** (please verify): Rupak, whose sam (the beat the cycle lands on) sources place differently, is **not** in the player for that reason; the Vedic chant UNESCO listing (not used, the search returned nothing); the date of the Natya Shastra (the card gives the argued range).

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): the Hohle Fels flute’s five finger holes and the ivory figure found in the same cave in 2008; the Kojiki’s date (712) and court kagura from the Heian period; the Talmud’s Megillah 32a on reading with a tune; the Aleppo Codex and Aaron ben Asher; Ibn Mujāhid’s seven readings (936); the Samaveda and Vedic pitch accents and recitation patterns, and the medieval date of the oldest Rigveda manuscripts; the Gyuto monastery’s 15th-century founding and overtone singing in Tuva and Mongolia; Paul the Deacon as the author of Ut queant laxis, Guido’s dates (about 991 to after 1033) and the Micrologus of about 1025, and ut becoming do; Pope Gregory I’s dates (590–604) and the dove legend’s appearance in the 9th century; the Natya Shastra’s treatment of tala; the Zhou chime bells in ritual and their use in ancestor rites; Aristotle’s reply in On the Heavens, Timaeus 35–36, Kepler’s *Harmonices Mundi* (1619) and Nicomachus’s hammer story; Justinian’s dates for Hagia Sophia (532–537); the 1939 London conference on concert pitch and its later adoption as an ISO standard; Hans Cousto’s 1978 “cosmic octave” and the 136.1 Hz “Om” figure; Zwingli, the 1644 English ordinance on organs and Luther’s regard for music; the Mevlevi use of samāʿ; Jones’s 1959 *Studies in African Music*; the Cuban bembé’s coming with enslaved Yoruba people in the 19th century (from the search summary, not from a source on Santería); Adi tala’s counting (a clap, three finger counts, then two clap-and-wave pairs).

**What was not done.** The cards were read by no one but me.
