The sound of the player was checked by rendering the page’s own shipped synthesis offline in the browser (all strokes present within 1 ms of their time, no clipping, each voice’s peak within about 5% of what the level asks for, the loudest stroke at about −19 dBFS at the test setting), and the live player was checked to start, light the pulses in step with the audio clock, stop, and stop on leaving the page; **nobody listened to it**, so whether it sounds pleasant is not known, and the “quiet counting beat” balance was set by measurement, not by ear. The rule that no recording or sample is used is a test of the source for audio calls, files and network calls. The Atlas story’s coordinates are approximate. Rupak, the Mevlevi usuls, gamelan, the clave patterns and the Seikilos and Hurrian tunes are not played, for the reasons given on the page.

## Sacred art and image: the 28 cards, the drawings and the Atlas story (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** Religion → Beyond text → Sacred art and image (`src/js/2z-data-image.js`, `2z-image-core.js`, `6zu-image.js`): 28 cards, a workshop of three drawings the app makes itself (an Islamic star pattern, a generic mandala plan, a Hindu temple grid) and four plain diagrams of early Buddhist signs, and the Atlas story “The oldest images in worship” (14 entries). Beyond text now has four views. Nothing was fetched and no picture is loaded or reproduced.

**Checked against the library’s own texts** (`tests/image.test.js`, 14 claim groups and one negative check, found in the built texts): the World English Bible on the command (Exodus 20:4, Deuteronomy 5:8), the golden cherubim (Exodus 25:18), Bezalel (Exodus 31), Hezekiah breaking Moses’s bronze serpent (2 Kings 18:4), the idol-maker of Isaiah 44, the valley of dry bones, and the Letter of Jeremiah; John of Damascus on honouring the image of the Saviour, Basil’s “honour … passes over to the prototype” and the tabernacle as “an image and type of heavenly things”; the Second Council of Nicaea on being “lifted up to the memory of their prototypes” and “true worship”; Eusebius’s Abgar story, in which the words portrait, likeness and image do not occur until the translators’ note about Ananias the artist (tested as a stretch of text); Gregory the Great’s two letters on broken images (“a picture is instead of reading”, “in that thou forbadest them to be adored, we altogether praise thee; but we blame thee for having broken them”); Jowett’s three beds; Porphyry’s Plotinus refusing a portrait, “do as the artist …” and the sculptor’s chisel; Rodwell’s statues among the abominations, Pickthall’s clay bird and “Who teacheth by the pen”; the Diamond Sutra’s “engaged in false endeavours”; Kern’s Lotus Sutra on images of jewels, lead, iron, clay or plaster and on painted walls; Rhys Davids’s note defining the thupa; the Amitayus Sutra’s image of the sun kept “clear and fixed”; Budge’s coloured drawings of offerings, the Book of Opening the Mouth, the Judgment Hall of Osiris and the French “Rituel Funéraire”; the Matsya Purana on the feet of the Sun and on installing sacred images, and the Agni Purana on the seat of the image. Every Atlas text and Atlas place a card points at is a real record, spelled as the Atlas spells it.

**Checked on the web this session** (secondary sources: news reports, abstracts, publisher and museum pages, encyclopaedia pages, not the full papers; the page says which paper each claim comes from): Leang Karampuang (Oktaviana and colleagues, *Nature*, 2024: minimum 51,200 years; the Leang Bulu’ Sipong 4 scene re-dated to about 48,000, news summaries only); the Spanish Neanderthal-art claim and its critics (Hoffmann and colleagues, *Science*, 2018; Slimak and colleagues’ comment and the authors’ reply; Pearce and Bonneau; a later paper on uranium leaching; the summary came partly from a site that sides with the critics, and the *Science* volume number differs between sources); the Egyptian grid (18 squares from the Middle and New Kingdoms, 21 and a fraction in the Late Period, Robins 1994, Diodorus’s 21¼; sources differ on when the change came, so the card does not say); Lu and Steinhardt (*Science*, 2007), Darb-i Imam in 1453, the Topkapı Scroll (15th–16th century) and the critics (Makovicky, Bonner, Bier), from secondary pages and a student poster; Huntington (*Art Journal*, 1990), Foucher, Karlsson and Dehejia on Buddhist aniconism; Walker and Dick on mīs pî and pīt pî and the craftsmen’s disowning of the statue, and the Egyptian wpt-r from the Old Kingdom to Roman times; the Dura-Europos dates (the synagogue 244/5 rests on **a single museum-database entry**, and I did not find an excavation report; the house church c. 232 or “mid-3rd century”; the fall of the city 256/257); the standing-caliph coins (74–77 AH, 693–696/7) and ‘Abd al-Malik’s reform of 696–697, and one scholar’s suggestion that a Byzantine envoy’s demand lay behind it (given as a hypothesis); the dates of Byzantine iconoclasm (726 or 730, Hieria 754, Nicaea II 787, 815 or 813, 843); the Codex Maya de México (INAH’s 2018 dating to 1021–1154 CE and its renaming; some doubters remain; I found **no** *Science Advances* paper, and my first guess that there was one was wrong); Göbekli Tepe (earliest structures about 9500–9000 BCE, Schmidt’s temple view, Banning’s 2011 argument for houses; the “later work reports everyday activity” line comes from a result of low quality and should be checked against the excavators’ own reports); the lion-man (about 40,000 years, ranges from 35,000 to 41,000, mammoth ivory, found 1939); Blombos (Henshilwood and colleagues, *Nature*, 2018: silcrete flake, ochre crayon, about 73,000 years); Chauvet (Quiles and colleagues, *PNAS*, 2016: 37,000–33,500 and 31,000–28,000).

**Not confirmed by the searches, kept as written** (please verify): the date of the Kanishka coins (the card says only “2nd century CE”); the paintings at Qusayr ‘Amra and the early 8th-century date; that books from the 14th century on show the Prophet and later ones veil his face; the hadith on image-makers (not in the library; the card says so and does not quote them); the 2019 and 2021 Sulawesi papers’ dates (43,900 and 45,500 are from memory).

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): the lion-man’s height and the “worn and polished” surface; the Papyrus of Ani’s date and length (about 24 metres); the Sinai icons of the 6th century; Florensky’s “reverse perspective” (1919–1922); the Doctrine of Addai as the source of the painter in the Edessa story; Altamira’s publication in 1880 and its acceptance in 1902; Lewis-Williams’s *The Mind in the Cave* (2002) and Bahn as a critic; Lascaux’s closure in 1963; the Bharhut and Sanchi dates (the Atlas gives about 150 BCE and 250 BCE with later gateways); the Lotus Sutra’s date; the Mahavairocana Sutra as a 7th-century text describing mandalas; sand mandalas being swept away; the Manduka and Paramasayika grids and the central cells of Brahma; Varahamihira’s *Brihat Samhita* in the 6th century and Kramrisch’s *The Hindu Temple* (1946); Borobudur’s nine levels and its counts of panels and images; the Wittenberg, Zurich, Beeldenstorm, Trent and English dates (1521–22, 1524, 1566, 1563, 1643); the two ways of counting the commandments; Gregory’s letters as “about 600”; Ibn Muqla (died 940) and Ibn al-Bawwab (died 1022); Landa’s *Relación* (about 1566) and the burning at Maní in 1562; the Dresden Codex’s Venus table; Euclid’s “extreme and mean ratio”, the 19th-century name “golden”, Pacioli’s *De divina proportione* (1509), Markowsky’s 1992 article, and the Abydos circles as later graffiti; Hankin’s 1925 paper; the Sulawesi and Blombos finds’ meaning as inference.

**Judgement calls for the maker.** (1) The mandala drawing is a generic plan of a sacred diagram of a living tradition (square palace, four gates, rings of lotus, vajra and fire, centre empty); it shows no deity, the card says it is not a teaching, and the drawing says why the centre is empty, but say if you would rather not draw it at all. (2) The sacred-geometry card calls the Abydos “Flower of Life” circles later graffiti and the golden-ratio claims unsupported; many readers hold them dear, and the care note says the issue is the historical claim, not the meaning people find. (3) The Mandylion note rests on Eusebius’s text and the translators’ note, and says the portrait enters later retellings; it does not say anything about any relic. (4) The Neanderthal card relies on secondary summaries, one from a site that sides with the critics.

**What was not done.** The cards were read by no one but me. The drawings were checked in the browser at desktop and phone width and in the light and dark themes; they were not tried on a real phone, with a screen reader, or in Safari or Firefox. The star patterns are Hankin’s method, which is a modern way of analysing the patterns, and the page says so; no drawing is claimed to match a particular monument. The Egyptian grid is described and not drawn, because the landmark rows of the figure were not checked against a source; the Sri Yantra is not drawn, because its exact construction was not solved and checked. “No picture is loaded” is a test of the source for picture tags, file names, `fetch`, CSS `url()` and web addresses, and of the drawings for the shapes they are made of; it is not a proof that no other code could do so.

## The imagined shelf: six texts, six cards and the rules that keep fiction apart (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** A Library collection, **The imagined shelf** (group `im`), and a page, Religion → Library → Imagined shelf (`src/js/2z-data-imagined.js`, `6zv-imagined.js`, `tools/texts_imagined.py`): Dante’s *Divine Comedy* (Longfellow, 1867; Project Gutenberg #1004), Milton’s *Paradise Lost* (#26), Bunyan’s *The Pilgrim’s Progress*, Part One (#131), and Lovecraft’s *The Call of Cthulhu* (#68283), *The Festival* (#68553) and *The Dunwich Horror* (#50133). The maker approved each file, source and size before anything was downloaded (1.8 MB in all); the files are in `tools/cache/gutenberg/`. Nothing is graded, and the shelf is kept out of the Path, search, the random passage, Find similar and every graded page.

**Checked against the texts themselves** (`tests/imagined.test.js`, in the built text files): Dante: the first and last lines; “All hope abandon, ye who enter in” in the third canto of the Inferno; the neutral souls “withouten infamy or praise” in the same canto; “a Mighty One” taking out Adam, Abel, Noah and Moses in Limbo; Homer named in the fourth canto; “Mahomet” and “Ali” only in the 28th canto; Aristotle in the third canto of the Purgatorio; the four creatures “plumed with six wings … full of eyes” in the 29th; “I Thomas of Aquinum” in the tenth canto of the Paradiso; Bernard as Dante’s last guide; and that the lines “hottest places” and “neutrality … moral crisis”, often attributed to Dante, are **not** in the poem. Milton: the opening, “justify the ways of God to men”, “Better to reign in Hell than serve in Heaven” in Book I, “the palace of great Lucifer”, and no “day-star”. Bunyan: the opening dream with its margin references, Vanity Fair with its Ecclesiastes reference, “two giants, Pope and Pagan”, “the river of God”, and more than two hundred bracketed Bible references; the file is Part One only. Lovecraft: the Theosophists on the first page of Cthulhu, “degenerate Eskimos”, the angekok and tornasuk, “half-castes and pariahs”, the chant, the Necronomicon of “the mad Arab Abdul Alhazred”, voodoo; in The Festival “older than Bethlehem and Babylon”, Kingsport, the Lactantius epigraph, “Olaus Wormius’ forbidden Latin translation”, Glanvil’s *Saducismus* (1681) and Remigius’s *Daemonolatreia* (1595); in The Dunwich Horror “Dr. Dee’s English version”, “the Aklo for the Sabaoth”, “repellently decadent” and “stigmata of degeneracy and inbreeding”. Real sources, in the library’s own English: Isaiah 14’s “king of Babylon” (World English Bible), Origen’s “How is Lucifer, who used to arise in the morning, fallen from heaven!”, Rodwell’s Qur’an 17:1, Revelation 4’s six wings and eyes, “the river of God” in the Psalms, “vanity of vanities” in Ecclesiastes, and that Murray’s *Witch-Cult*, Dee’s *True and Faithful Relation* and Blavatsky’s *Isis Unveiled* are in the library.

**Checked on the web this session** (secondary sources: encyclopaedia and publisher pages): that Lovecraft himself tied *The Festival* to Margaret Murray’s *The Witch-Cult in Western Europe* (“I had just been reading” it), and that Murray’s theory is widely rejected; the “Simon Necronomicon” (sold from 1977, “Simon” said to be a pseudonym of Peter Levenda; Owen Davies calls it “a well-constructed hoax”); that Machen’s “The White People” (1904) is the source of “Aklo” in *The Dunwich Horror*; Asín Palacios’s 1919 thesis on Dante and Islam (one source puts his first proposal in 1907), the 1949 finding by Cerulli and Muñoz Sendino of medieval Spanish, Latin and French versions of the *Book of the Ladder*, and that the influence is still contested; that “Divina” was first put on a title page by Giolito’s 1555 edition, after Boccaccio’s earlier use of the adjective. **One search result carried unrelated embedded instructions (a request for a paragraph on cheating and for song lyrics); it was ignored.**

**Not confirmed by the searches, kept out or kept general:** the date of Lovecraft’s mock “History of the Necronomicon” (sources differ, so the card gives none); where the Necronomicon first appears (the card says only “invented by Lovecraft”); Lovecraft’s *Supernatural Horror in Literature* on Murray (not used).

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): the dates of Dante (about 1265–1321; the poem about 1308–1320), Milton (1608–1674; 1667 and 1674; blind), Bunyan (1628–1688; imprisoned for preaching without a licence, and which imprisonment he wrote Part One in), Lovecraft (1890–1937; the writing and Weird Tales dates) and Thomas Aquinas (died 1274); Blake’s “of the Devil’s party without knowing it” (about 1790); that Isaiah 14 was read of Satan by Origen and others; the Harrowing of Hell coming from the Gospel of Nicodemus; that Muhammad is in the ninth pit of the eighth circle; that the Church does not teach Dante’s circles; that Angakkoq and tornaq are the Greenlandic words for a shaman and a spirit; that Lovecraft wrote in letters that he was a materialist; that the real Dunwich is in Suffolk and was taken by the sea; Ole Worm as a real Danish scholar; the first dating of Christmas to 25 December in the 4th century and the two explanations of why; that Giant Pope is the Roman Catholic Church; that the best-seller claim for *The Pilgrim’s Progress* is uncounted; Lord Dunsany’s death in 1957; the rights statements (Lovecraft died in 1937, so public domain in the EU and UK; first published before 1930, so public domain in the US; Gutenberg’s own transcriber’s notes say no renewal of the 1928 and 1929 copyrights was found).

**What the shelf says about each author’s intention** (the maker’s condition: the six texts were never meant as scripture, and fiction was the authors’ original intention). From general knowledge, not checked in this session: that Dante gave the poem an allegorical and moral purpose of its own and that the Church has never treated it as scripture; that Milton hoped to “justify the ways of God to men” (that line is in the text) and calls on a “Heavenly Muse” (also in the text) while offering a poem, not a revelation; that Bunyan’s full title says “delivered under the similitude of a dream”, and that he took his authority from the Bible; that Lovecraft wrote for the pulp magazine Weird Tales and did not believe in the gods, cults and books in his tales. The Dante wording was chosen to be accurate: the poem is not called a hoax or a joke, and it is not called mere fiction in the sense the Lovecraft tales are.

**Judgement calls for the maker.** (1) The shelf includes three Lovecraft tales whose racism and contempt are plain in the text; the cards name this and say “understood and not endorsed”, but say if you would rather not hold them. (2) Search’s “All texts” and the random passage leave the shelf out by default and need it chosen by name: a design choice, so that fiction never turns up among scripture; it can be changed in `srchIds` and `booksIn`. (3) The line between this shelf and the others (a known author who made it up, against a tradition’s own account) is mine; Swedenborg’s visions of heaven and hell, which he reported as seen, are on the Spiritualism shelf and not here, and Dante, who believed in the afterlife he imagined, is here. (4) The Dante card names the passage in which Muhammad is torn open; it quotes one line of the library’s text (“How mutilated, see, is Mahomet”) and says Muslims have objected for centuries.

**What was not done.** The cards were read by no one but me, and the texts were checked by tests and by reading samples, not read through; Longfellow’s verse layout was not compared with another edition; the six texts have no Atlas records (they are not sources); the shelf was checked in the browser at desktop width only. The README’s text counts are the texts session’s to re-measure (the library now holds more than the README says).

## Word help: the Zoroastrian and Pahlavi glossary, 163 new entries (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** 163 entries in `src/js/2o-glossary.js` (the glossary went from 126 to 289 words and names), all of them on the Persia shelf, so that the words and names in West’s *Pahlavi Texts* (the Sad Dar chapter that the maker pasted, and the three other books), the Bundahishn and the Avesta translation can be explained where they are read. See ROADMAP.md for the feature.

**How the list was chosen.** Not from memory: by counting. The commonest words in x-pah, x-bun and x-ave that are not in the system word list were read in context, in three passes (`tools/audit_terms.js`), and the entries were written for what a reader keeps meeting. The audit’s last run marks about 36,000 words (every mention) across the three books, and the commonest unexplained capitalised words left are scan noise, page headings, abbreviations of the old editors’ notes, English place names and a long tail of rarer names.

**Checked against the library’s own texts** (this session, by searching and reading the text in context): the identification of each name with its spellings, including more than a hundred garbled spellings that occur in the scans (the test holds each to its entry); West’s own account of each book (his introductions: the Dadistan-i Dinik’s ninety-two questions put to Manushchihar by Mitro-khurshed, with Manushchihar as “pontiff, or executive high-priest” of Pars and Kirman and Zad-sparam as his younger brother and high priest of Sirkan; the Mainyo-i Khard’s sixty-two questions; the Shikand-gumanik Vigar by Mardan-farukh, son of Attharmazd-dad, attacking the scriptures of Muslims, Jews, Christians and Manichaeans; the Sad Dar as a Persian treatise on “a hundred subjects” with about four per cent of Arabic words, more quoted than any other work by the Parsi compilers of the Persian Rivayats in the seventeenth century, and the metrical version of 1531; the Dinkard’s compilers Atar-frobag and Atar-pad and its books 7, 8 and 9 and parts of 5; the Nasks’ three classes, Gathic, Hadha-mathric (“the scientific class”) and Legal); West’s own list of the English words he uses for Pahlavi terms in the Bundahishn volume (archangel, guardian spirit, fiend for druj and demon for dev, thread-girdle, sacred shirt, sacred twigs, sacred cake, next-of-kin marriage, season-festival, renovation of the universe, best existence, noxious creature, high-priest for rad); West’s abbreviations and manuscript letters; the twelve thousand years in four ages (West’s note and the Bundahishn’s summary); the three nights after death, the place of account on Alburz, the Chinvat (Kinvad) bridge from which the wicked fall head first, “the ever-stationary” (hamistakan) and the “supreme heaven” (garodman), all from the Dadistan-i Dinik, chapters 19 to 24; the seven regions and Khvaniras “as large as the other six united”, holding Iran and the lands the Iranians knew, with Mazendaran outside it; Gayomard’s thirty years and Mashya and Mashyoi growing as a plant like rhubarb, brother and sister and husband and wife (Bundahishn); the Gokard tree guarded by ten fish; Dahak’s thousand years and chaining under Mount Dimavand, and Keresasp roused from his trance to kill him; Peshyotanu, who “forgot death” and lives in Kangdez, and Syavarshan who founded it; Zarathustra’s mother Dughda and father Porushaspo, and the demons that rushed her house for three nights; Airyaman as “the smiter of diseases” whose spell concludes the Gathas; the tanapuhar as a weight of 1,200 dirhams and the merit of a Yasna, Visparad and Vendidad in tanapuhars; the old commentators Afarg, Gogosasp, Medyok-mah, Roshan and Kushtano-bud; Valkhash’s order to save the scattered Avesta after the ravages of Alexander, and Tansar summoned by Ardashir; the days of the month (Ormazd 1, Khordad 6, Adar 9, Tir 13, Mihir 16, Srosh 17, Rashn 18, Farvardin 19, Bahram 20, Din 24, Ard 25) from the Sirozah in the Avesta volume; Khusro Noshirvan’s dates 531–579 and “A.Y.” as Anno Yazdagardi; West’s conclusion on “next-of-kin marriage” (the priesthood had to work constantly to recommend it to the laity: the card says so and does not say it was general); and that the Persian words in West’s brackets (qabul kunand, iqlim and the like) are the original word for the English before them.

**From general knowledge, not checked in this session** (please verify before relying on them): the months and the other days of the Zoroastrian calendar that go with each Amesha Spenta and yazata (Vohuman’s second day and eleventh month, Ardwahisht’s third day and second month, Shahrewar, Spendarmad, Khordad and Amurdad, Aban’s tenth day and eighth month, Mihr’s seventh month); which part of creation each Amesha Spenta guards; the ten forms of Verethragna and the highest grade of temple fire named after him; Apaosha as a black horse; Tishtrya as Sirius; the dating of Zarathustra (about 1500–1000 BCE on the language of the Gathas, the seventh or sixth century on later tradition); the suggestion that Asmodeus comes from Aeshma; the identification of Vishtaspa with Hystaspes (the card says it is disputed); the daena as a maiden or a hag (West’s note has the maiden); the ceremonies of the Bareshnum (nine nights, bull’s urine, sand and water), the kusti’s seventy-two threads, the tower of silence, the five gahs, the five days of each gahambar, the five Nyayishes, the thirty days of the Sirozah, the raspi; the Haoma’s possible identity (ephedra) and its link with the Vedic Soma; Neryosangh’s date (said “probably the twelfth century”); the Rivayats beginning in the fifteenth century; Ardashir’s founding of the dynasty about 224 CE, Yazdakard III’s death about 651 CE and accession in 632 CE; Mazdak’s date (about 528 CE); the date and standing of Herodotus, and the dates and works of Haug, Anquetil-Duperron, Darmesteter, Westergaard, Burnouf, Windischmann, Geldner, Justi, Hyde, Ouseley, Spiegel and Bartholomae; the Persian peri and the Indian apsaras (Darmesteter’s comparison is in the text, the link to peri is not); the name Mazdayasna and “Zoroaster” as a Greek form.

**What the matching had to be careful about** (measured, not assumed; `tests/terms.test.js`). Scanning all 717 texts of the library with the first version found that the older glossary’s very short words (De, Me, Ka, Li, Ba) and ordinary English words (Grace, Canon, Trinity, Mana, Tian) would have underlined thousands of unrelated words, and that some of the new entries’ spellings are common words elsewhere (Yatha and Homa are Sanskrit words, Daitya and Aban and Bahman are other words and names, Kayan is a Bornean people, Sudra and Dev are Hindu words, Dastar is a turban, Zarate a surname). They were scoped to the texts where they carry this meaning, and the spelling patterns are now tested against the whole system word list. After scoping, the library is marked at 0.38 words in a thousand; Genesis and Matthew are not marked at all. **The older entries De, Li and Me are kept in the glossary but never underlined** (they are written in lower case in the translations, and the capitalised forms are a French word, a surname and a pronoun).

**Judgement calls for the maker.** (1) The card for *khvetuk-das* describes a custom that outsiders found shocking and that West studied carefully; it follows West’s own conclusions and says plainly that modern Zoroastrians do not practise it. Say if you would rather it were shorter. (2) The Haoma card says which plant it was is not known; it does not describe any preparation. (3) “Look further” keeps the two outside links (Wiktionary, Wikipedia); both open only if chosen, and nothing is sent. (4) Word help is on by default (first mention in each section) because it was asked for; the maker may prefer it off until chosen (`RT_DEFAULT.help`).

**What was not done.** The 163 cards were read by no one but me; no phone, touch screen, screen reader or slow machine was tried (the card was checked in the desktop browser pane, at a phone width in the same pane, and with synthetic taps); the entries of the other traditions were not extended (the Hindu, Buddhist, Jain, Egyptian and Mesopotamian vocabularies would be next); thousands of rarer capitalised words in the three books still have no entry (most are scan noise or one-off names) (`node tools/audit_terms.js x-pah x-bun x-ave` lists them, commonest first); a plain English dictionary for the translators’ ordinary words (apostleship, tardy, kine, thou) was not added in that pass, because it needs a source to be fetched and the maker’s approval first; it was added afterwards, on the maker’s yes (next section).

## The built-in dictionary: Webster’s Revised Unabridged, 1913 (16 November 2026)

**Source and licence.** Project Gutenberg eBook #29765, `https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/29765/pg29765.txt`, 28,956,350 bytes, SHA-256 `86fb9c28c32008ea288ca4bcf34f4f0d3d11ccf9e0898294d98b73671497f1d3`; fetched on the maker’s “Yes, fetch it” and kept untouched in `tools/cache/webster1913/`. The book is the 1913 edition (public domain in the United States); the transcription is credited to Graham Lawrence. The tool reads only the text between Project Gutenberg’s start and end lines, and `dictionary/index.js` carries the licence line and the source. Not checked: the book’s status outside the United States (the maker’s rule for publishing is to re-check every source’s terms before anything is released).

**What was made.** `tools/build_dictionary.py` reads the text and writes `dictionary/`: 113,321 entries and 120,878 records (7,592 of them pointers: 4,306 other spellings of a headword, 2,940 derived forms the book prints under an entry (“Fiendfully, adv.”) and 346 irregular forms its head lines list (begat → beget)), 160,570 meanings (1.42 an entry), 19.7 MiB in 19 files. 35 entries that the book prints twice are kept once.

**What was kept and what was cut.** Kept: the headword and other spellings, syllables, the part of speech written out, each meaning with its subject label and usage label, and the first list of synonyms. Cut: word histories (the Gutenberg text lost the Greek and most of the Old English letters, so many are cut off or wrong, and a wrong history is worse than none), the quotations with their authors’ names, long usage notes and the discussions under “Syn.”, and pronunciation marks (the text keeps only accents). A form of another word is written out in words (“imp. & p. p. of Begin” becomes “Past tense and past participle of Begin.”, 489 meanings).

**Found by checking, and fixed.** (1) Sampling entries one by one showed that in 7,668 entries the first meaning was only its subject label, “(Anat.)”, with the definition in the next part; they are joined, and a test fails if any meaning is only a label. (2) about a thousand meanings ended with the poet Pope’s name, because my list of plain words that are not authors had “Pope” on it; fixed, with a rule that a name that is also a word counts as an author only after a full stop or a closing quotation mark (“blessed by the Pope.” is not a tag). (3) Authors with a title or initials, names stuck to the end of the line before them (“hiMotley”), several quotations in a row, and a name in square brackets (“[Shak.]”) each left a quotation in some meanings; fixed by rules found from the leftovers; a name not on the list of 737 authors, but following a closed quotation, now counts as one. (4) The word list of “care” included “impostor”, which put a note about peoples and faiths on “trickery”; removed. (5) “Hath”, “doth”, “wast” and the other old forms were meanings like “of Do.” or “, The 2d pers. sing. imp. of Do.”; written out and cleaned.

**Known faults, not fixed (counted on the built files).** 99 meanings (0.06 %) still end with the name of an author who is not on the list (for example “Hallam”, “Lingard”); 34 more end with the name of a famous author; some quotations survive inside a meaning when the author’s tag was not recognised (the second meaning of “Can”, “Canned meats” W. D. Howells.). Sub-entries the book prints inside a meaning (“Dotted note (Mus.), a note followed by a dot …”) stay in the meaning they follow, because the text has no marker between them. 9 entries have no meaning at all (a cross-reference only). The transcription itself has errors that were not corrected, because there is nothing to correct them from: the entry for “Pagan” says “false goods” where the book has “false gods”, “Hast” reads “of. Fave” for “of Have”, and “Dilatory” had “hiMotley” (now cut with the quotation). A very long meaning is cut at the end of a sentence before 900 characters. Words after 1913 are missing (computer, radio, cinema) and so are most names.

**Care.** 570 entries are flagged because a meaning, or the headword, uses one of a list of the words by which 1913 wrote of peoples and faiths (heathen, pagan, infidel, idolater, savage, barbarian, Mohammedan, Hindoo, negro, gypsy, cannibal, papist and others; `CARE` and `CARE_HEADS` in the tool). The box says once, below such a word’s entries, that it is shown to be understood, not endorsed, and every box says it is a book of its time. **The list is mine and no one reviewed it:** expect entries that need the note and lack it (a book of 1913 can be hostile in ways no word list catches; I did not read the entries about peoples and faiths to find them) and a few that carry it without needing it (“savage”, said of an animal).

**Checked.** `node tests/dictionary.test.js` (111 checks): the lookup key against 35 pairs made by the Python tool and, for every row of every file, sorted order inside each file’s range (so the key agrees for all 120,878 records); every entry whole; every pointer leads to an entry; 50-odd words looked up (the words of old translations, forms, pointers, phrases, nothing); the section escaped; no network call in the code; the dictionary as the fifth offline library and its own pack zip (`tests/offline.test.js`, `tests/packs.test.js`, `tests/privacy.test.js`). In the browser pane (desktop and a 375 px phone width, no sideways scroll): the Glossary page for “begot” and its arrow to “Beget” and back; Look up on “righteous” in the reader (the Pahlavi Texts); the search box’s “Define” row; the care note on “pagan”. Read by eye: about a hundred entries, random and by word, including the words of the Sad Dar passage the maker pasted. **Not done:** nobody read 113,000 entries; no phone, touch screen, screen reader, Safari or Firefox; the word card for an ordinary word does not exist (the reader underlines only the glossary’s words; an ordinary word is looked up by selecting it).

## The second sweep for missing texts, and the original edition and pictures (16 November 2026)

**What was checked and how.** (1) *The candidates.* 163 works were listed from the Atlas's traditions that have written texts and nothing readable in the Library, and from public-domain translations known to exist; each was matched against the Library's titles and authors, against the Project Gutenberg catalogue (`pg_catalog.csv`, 91,000 entries, English only) and against the Internet Archive's search (books before 1931). 61 were already in the Library, and the text session's sixth batch covers another 23 (Swedenborg, Julian of Norwich, the Cloud of Unknowing, Eckhart, Kardec, Sefer Yetzirah, Philo, Josephus, Rumi, Sa'di, Hafez, Attar, the Avesta, the Tirukkural, the Tiruvacagam, Kabir, Macauliffe and Trumpp on the Sikhs, the Visuddhimagga, Burlingame, the Tibetan Book of the Dead, Milarepa, the field records of Ellis, Callaway, Dennett, Bleek, Nassau, Matthews, Cushing, Grey, Codrington and Bogoras and others). (2) *The 62 specs in `tools/texts_gaps2.py`.* For each source on the Internet Archive the item's metadata was read (a downloadable full text exists, not a lending copy; the page count; the date) and the first lines of its text were read to confirm it is the book named; for Gutenberg the catalogue entry and the file's opening were read. Two descriptions were corrected after reading the scans: the contents of the Coptic Apocryphal Gospels (the Life of the Virgin, the Falling Asleep of Mary and the death of Joseph, not Bartholomew) and of the first volume of the Voyage of Bran (it includes the first part of Nutt's essay). **Left out for a reason:** Bahá'u'lláh's *Hidden Words* on Gutenberg (#16941) carries the Bahá'í International Community's terms, which allow copying only without any modification of the text, and the Library's sectioning is a modification; the Desatir's scan is mostly Persian script, which the OCR renders as noise; four scans are lending-library copies with no full text. **Not checked:** that each text's notes are right (they are the maker's own summaries, written from the scans' prefaces and general knowledge; the claims about authorship and dating of disputed works (the Yezidi books, Barddas, the Desatir, the Samaritan Chronicle) are hedged in the notes), and nobody has read the new texts through. The text session built all 62 on 16 November 2026 (42 MB of text; 842 texts in the Library in all), and the original-edition links and the pictures were then made for them.

**The original edition.** `tools/texts_originals.py` reads each text's source identifiers (from `texts_archive.SPECS` for texts made of several volumes, otherwise from the text's `src`) and asks the source which files it offers: for Project Gutenberg a HEAD request on the four standard files (EPUB with pictures, Kindle, HTML, plain text; the sizes are the files' own), for the Internet Archive the item's metadata (its PDF, EPUB, Kindle file, full text and DjVu, with their sizes), for Global Grey the book's page (its PDF, EPUB, Kindle and online-reading links). eBible.org and Wikisource get a link to the page (and, for Wikisource, the service's EPUB and PDF links). Nothing is downloaded; the app only links. Run on 16 November 2026 it gave links for all 865 texts (851 with files; 14 only a page, five documents of freedom and nine SuttaCentral texts): Internet Archive 475, Project Gutenberg 195, Global Grey 94, eBible.org 86, SuttaCentral 9 and six others. **Sizes:** the Internet Archive's "EPUB" of a scan can be an image-based book of several gigabytes (4.3 GB for one Bhagavatam; 413 EPUBs come to 36 GB), so each kind of file is shown with its total on the Freedom page, and each link with its size in the reader. The test (`tests/originals.test.js`) checks that every Gutenberg link names the text's own ebook number and every Internet Archive file lies inside the text's own item.

**The pictures.** `tools/texts_figures.py` downloaded each Gutenberg-sourced text's EPUB with images (about 150 MB in all, cached in `tools/cache/figures/`), found each picture's caption and the 14 words printed just before it, and located those words in the Library's own text: 1,485 of the 1,505 kept pictures by their words (the words occurring more than once, the occurrence nearest the expected place), 20 by their position between pictures that were placed, and 104 pictures left out because no neighbouring words matched (most are in appendices and front matter the Library's text leaves out). A match that broke the book's order was thrown out and the picture placed between its neighbours. **Checked by eye:** the cards of the tarot (the Emperor's card falls between its heading and its description); the reader's rendering in the browser; and a sample of 20 positions in the four books with the most pictures (Mooney's Ghost-Dance Religion, Dittmer's Te Tohunga, Sinha's Study of the Bhagavata Purana with its tables, Scot's Discoverie of Witchcraft), each printed with the paragraph before and after it: the figure Mooney cites as "figure 84" falls right after the paragraph that cites it, the genealogical tables sit beside their text, the music notation after its song's title. The sample also showed decorated first letters (drop caps) kept as pictures, which would have repeated the letter above its paragraph: all 19 pictures of Wilson's Clement of Alexandria, 29 of Jacobs's 84, 23 of Scot's 55 and a few in three other books. They, and small pictures nobody described, are now left out (1,183 pictures became 1,102 in the first 53 books; Clement keeps none). The nine books the sweep added were then done the same way, and the five books of the eighth round that have pictures bring the total to 1,505 pictures in 67 books; in the reader, the figure Boas cites as "Fig. 421" in The Central Eskimo falls right after the paragraph that cites it, with its caption. From the eighth round, three sets were looked at: the 80 drawings of Hans Holbein in the 1876 In Praise of Folly, the engraving of a New England meeting-house and the photograph of a page of Jonathan Edwards's manuscript in his Selected Sermons, and an engraved view in Brainerd's Life; they are pictures of the book, not ornaments. Jacobs's cover and binding pictures are kept at the head of the Introduction. **Not checked:** each of the 1,505 positions by eye; the five books with the most pictures are the likeliest to have a few misplaced by a paragraph or two. Covers, ornaments, signs and decorated initials were left out by name, by size and by shape (strips five times wider than tall); a picture with neither a description nor a caption is described by its section's name, for screen readers.


## The Path stations: all 137 read, 32 corrections, 5 questions rewritten, 49 quotations then checked (16 November 2026)

**What was checked and how.** Every one of the 137 stations (`src/js/27-…`, `28-…`, `29-…`, `2a-data-path-stations-*.js`) was read in full by me: the title, the dates, the blurb and the rhyme. Each factual claim was weighed against general knowledge, and the ones that could be checked here were checked against the Library's own texts or against the web. Checked in the Library: the 49-year jubilees of the Book of Jubilees (Charles’s text); the opening of the Ācārāṅga; the chapter order of Genesis and of Amos; the Huarochirí flood. Checked on the web: the Qumran counts and the llama flood in Huarochirí chapter 3. A script then pulled every quotation of five words or more out of the stations (96 of them) and looked for it in the texts each station sends the reader to.

**Corrected (32).** Each change is in the station text now; nothing else was touched.
- *Dates and counts:* Chauvet “about 37,000 to 28,000 years ago”, Lascaux about 17,000, the Lion-man about 40,000 (station 2); the trance dance’s date, which was stated more firmly than the evidence allows (4); Qumran “about 900–1,000 manuscripts, in some 15,000 fragments (counts differ)” (63); Jubilees’ 49-year jubilees (58); Rumi’s Masnavi begun about 1258 at Ḥusām al-Dīn’s urging, and “one of the most explicit” where it said “the most explicit” (110); the Eleusinian Mysteries “well over a thousand years” (52).
- *Claims stated too firmly:* “oldest carved in stone” (12); the Gilgamesh ending, which is the city wall, with the prologue saying the story was set on a stone (18); Amenemope and Proverbs: “most scholars think … adapted” (21); Isaiah 1–39 “mostly 8th-century” (35); the Psalms honoured by Muslims as the Zabūr, “one of the oldest books of prayer” (42); the Zoroastrian triad (45); the sealed-test sentence (47); Ma’at set beside the Buddhist uposatha confession (50); Philo and John (69); the Pure Land sutras (80); whether Gnosticism is pre-Christian, now said to be disputed (86); the householder sentence (89); Sikhism taken out of a list of religions that deliberately claim to be universal, and the Bahá’í Faith qualified (91); the *ik’* glyph “common in Maya writing” (102); Blombos instead of “first rock art” (117); the Guru Granth Sahib as “a rare scripture”, with the Sikhs not described as a blend of two religions (121); two popular ideas about Bahá’u’lláh’s teaching and the “Manifestation of God” made clear (128).
- *Second-person phrasing that referred to a private conversation* (“what you described”, “what you called”, “your own question”) was taken out of stations 34, 93 and 127.
- *A quotation that was a paraphrase:* in station 80, 4 Ezra no longer “asks ‘Why has God let Zion fall?’” in quotation marks; it asks why God let Zion fall while Babylon prospers (4 Ezra 3).

**Five questions rewritten** (stations 41, 78, 86, 117, 130): each one pointed at a private conversation rather than at the text. They are prompts, not claims, and nothing else in the question lists was changed.

**The quotations.** Of 96 quoted passages, 13 are word for word in the English of the station’s own Library texts. The other 83 were reviewed one by one, in two groups.
- *34 are the same passage in a different English wording* (the closest-wording search found half or more of the words, in order, in the station’s own text): Hosea 6:6, the Heart Sutra, Qur’an 49:13, George Fox’s “that of God in everyone”, John 1:1, Benedict’s welcome of guests, Rabia’s prayer, Leviticus 19:18, Deuteronomy 6:4, Amos 5:24, Merneptah’s “his seed is not”, Hillel, Augustine’s “restless”, the Yoga Sūtras’ “fluctuations of mind”, the Analects’ “daily examine”, the Desert Fathers’ cell, the Metta Sutta’s mother. The Library’s translator chose different words (for example the World English Bible has “let justice roll on like rivers”, where the familiar line is “roll down like waters”). **These were left as written**, and the Path’s “How this is kept honest” list now says so: quotations are in familiar standard translations and may differ a little from the Library’s English, so search for a word or two.
- *The other 49 found little or nothing in the station’s own texts.* They were checked in a follow-up the same day (next paragraph).

**The 49 quotations, checked afterwards (26 further edits).** Method: (1) every phrase of each quote was searched for across all 842 Library texts; (2) the Bible quotes were read against the Library’s 17 Bible versions; (3) the passages were found by hand in the Library’s texts where they exist; (4) web searches (standard mode) for the Hadith, Eckhart, the Hávamál, the Nahua poem, 4Q521, Thomas 77, the Platform Sutra, Hammurabi’s prologue, Ptahhotep, Enheduanna and the Thanksgiving Address. The web searches mostly returned secondary pages (encyclopaedia entries, quotation and teaching sites, a few scholarly articles), which confirmed that a passage exists and who is credited with it but seldom its exact English wording; none of them is a published edition.
- *Aligned to the Library’s own English (15 quotes):* Hávamál 76 (Thorpe: “the fair fame never dies of him who has earned it”), Eckhart (Evans: “the eye wherein I see God is the same eye wherein God sees me”), Kabir (Tagore: “where dost thou seek Me? Lo! I am beside thee”), Qur’an 5:48 (Pickthall: “a divine law and a traced-out way”), Mishnah Avot 1:2, Marcus Aurelius 2.1 (the Library’s older English), Odes of Solomon 6 (the “hand” reading), Sefer Yetzirah 2:2 (Westcott: “formed, weighed, and composed”), Rigveda 10.129 (Griffith), Tobit 4:15 and Proverbs 22:24 and 22:28 (World English Bible), and Galatians 3:28, Romans 8:26 and Acts 2:2 (Berean Standard Bible). Where a famous modern wording differs, the station names the translator.
- *Made plain statements instead of quotations (7), because they were paraphrases:* Enheduanna’s “Lady of all the me” (translators title the poem differently, for example “Mistress of the innumerable me”), Ptahhotep’s “Hearing is good for the one who hears” (Lichtheim’s wording is, by a secondary source, “Useful is hearing to one who hears”), Hammurabi’s “justice appear in the land” (the translators found say “make justice prevail” or “bring about the rule of righteousness”), the oracle-bone questions, the Zhuangzi heels-and-throats line (Giles’s note in the Library confirms the content, not the wording), “the breath that sings us” (the rhyme’s own phrase) and the 4Q521 line.
- *Facts corrected on the way:* 4Q521 describes God, in the time of his Messiah, healing the wounded and raising the dead, not the Messiah doing it (the fragment is damaged and the reading is argued); Rigveda 10.129 asks how the world began, not “how it began and ends”; Acts 2:2 uses *pnoē* (wind, breath), not *pneuma*, so the station now says the Greek for spirit also means wind and breath; the hadith in Muslim 223 is “Purity (*ṭuhūr*) is half of faith”, where the station had “Cleanliness”; the Nahua poem is now described as one of the poems attributed to Nezahualcóyotl, in a modern translation, in the Cantares and related manuscripts; the Platform Sutra verse now says its wording differs between the Dunhuang manuscript and later editions (the sources found disagree about which edition has which line, so the station says only that they differ); Eknath Easwaran is named as the translator of the Bṛhadāraṇyaka line (from memory, not looked up).
- *Verified in the Library’s own Bibles and left:* Isaiah 44:6, Micah 6:8 (Leeser and the ASV: “love kindness”), Habakkuk 3:17–18, Daniel 12:2, Wisdom 1:13 (“God made not death” in the KJV and Douay; the station’s “did not make death” is the RSV wording), Qohelet 9:7, Proverbs 23:7 (the station already explains that the KJV rendering is disputed) and Acts 17:28.
- *Still resting on my general knowledge or on a secondary web page, not looked up in a published translation:* Thomas sayings 70 and 77 (the 77 wording was seen on web pages quoting the Nag Hammadi translation; 70 was not), Apuleius 11.23 (the Latin is from memory; the station’s English is a modern rendering, and my searches did not find the passage in the Library’s Adlington text), the Hadith “Actions are judged by intentions” (one of several renderings; the hadith itself is Bukhari 1), Ḥallāj’s *anā al-Ḥaqq*, the Mu koan, the Nezahualcóyotl poem’s English wording and exact manuscript, 4QInstruction’s “mystery that is to be” (*raz nihyeh*), the Neijing line, the Great Hymn to the Aten and the Nāgārjuna title (titles vary by translator), and “now our minds are one” (confirmed on several web pages of the 1993 Six Nations Indian Museum translation). About fifteen quotes in all.

**Not checked in this pass.** The three questions and the practice on each station (prompts, not claims; only the private-conversation ones were fixed); the 136 lessons (another session deepened them this week and is responsible for them); the parallels, the glossary and the Learn articles; the roughly 2,000 Atlas text records not read by hand; the translations themselves. General-knowledge items worth a second source before the app is published: the Chauvet and Lascaux dates, the Lion-man date, Rumi’s Masnavi chronology, the Gilgamesh ending, the Qumran counts (checked on the web in a secondary source only).

**Tests.** After the changes: all test files pass except two that belong to the texts session’s work on originals and pictures (`figures`: “no entry is for a text that is gone” for `a-chf` and `x-lwl`; `originals`: the archive.org entry for `x-gfm`). They were failing before this pass and nothing here touches them.

## The Library as shelves, where each text comes from, and the status of each shelf (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** A shelf-style Library page; a source card, a finder and an “Original page” action in the reader; a fuller copied citation; the reading theme carried to the reader’s own bar and panels; British and older spellings in the dictionary. Tests: `library` (18), `source` (20), `reading` (116, with the chrome colours for all eight themes), `dictionary` (114).

**Checked by test and by measurement.** Every book’s cover title reads at 7:1 or more (4,900 covers sampled across all collections). Every theme’s chrome colours read at 4.5:1 or more. All 475 scanned texts have an Internet Archive identifier the finder can use (from the originals sidecar, or the text’s own source address). Looked at in the browser: the Library at desktop and phone width, an open shelf, the scan strip and source card on the Eckhart scan, the Sepia, Dusk and High contrast themes, the copied citation (checked by reading what the copy action sent to the clipboard) and the “Original page” link (checked by reading the link it opened).

**Not verified.** The Internet Archive’s own search inside a scan (`?q=`) was not tried from here: The app never contacts the Internet Archive, and I did not open its pages. The link is the form the Internet Archive uses for its own search results; whether it lands on the right page for a given scan depends on the scan’s own text layer. The 475 scans are mostly OCR of the Internet Archive and of the Digital Library of India, whose text layers differ in quality.

**The status line on each shelf** (`LIB_STATUS`) is my wording from general knowledge, not a quotation from a source, and none of it was looked up in this session. Things worth a second reader: the Hebrew Bible line (Protestant count and order; “Muslims honour the Torah and the Psalms as earlier revelation”), the Jain line (that the Śvetāmbara and Digambara traditions disagree about which texts are authentic), the Avesta line (a fragment of a larger lost collection), the Hermetic dates (about 100 to 300 CE), and the Freemasonry line (not a religion by its own account). A test checks that no shelf is described with a dismissing word.

**What was not done.** The printed page numbers of the scanned books are still not kept in the text (the builder dropped them), so the reader cannot show “page 214 of the original” beside a passage; the finder sends the reader to the scan instead. Mapping each section to its scan page would need the scans’ page data to be fetched again, which needs the maker’s yes. The “where to read it” note on a book with no text, and the per-book `note`, are unchanged.

## My shelves and my reading (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** Four shelves a book can be on (Finished, Reading now, Put down, Want to read), lists of the reader's own, and a page with the reading numbers, the last twelve weeks and a breadth view across the collections. Test: `shelves` (55).

**These are the reader's own records, not claims about the texts,** so there is nothing here to cite. What can be wrong is the arithmetic, and it is labelled: a word is counted as six characters; the words read are the characters of each text over the share of it the reader is through (the saved place, else the sections read, and all of a finished book); minutes are at the reader's own measured pace; "days read" counts movement through a text, not time with a page open. A book the reader only opened is not counted as begun. A book put down unopened is not counted as begun either.

**Not verified in a real reading session.** The log of days was checked in the browser by turning one page after a pause (962 characters were counted, and a second page turned at once was not) and by the unit tests for jumps and flipping; it was not measured over days of real use. The grid fills only from the day this was built; the totals do not depend on it.

## The parts of the big shelves (16 November 2026)

**What was added.** Six shelves are shown as named parts (47 in all): Early Christian and Gnostic 12, Jewish 3, Scholarship 13, India 8, Buddhist 6, Tribal 5. Test: `subshelves` (23).

**Checked by test.** Every book of a split shelf is in exactly one part, and no part holds a book of another shelf (none dropped; the test names any book not placed, and any id that has gone from the shelf). Each part is between 3 and 30 books. No part is described with a dismissing word.

**Written from the titles, and from general knowledge; not from a second source.** The name and the sentence for each part (what it holds, the centuries, the translators named) were written by reading each book's title and date in the Library, and the places where a part's sentence names a person or a work were checked against the titles in that part. They were not checked against the books' text, and the placing of a few books is a judgement: Pseudo-Dionysius and Boethius are in the medieval part though they are late antique; the Rule of Benedict is with the desert monks; Pascal is with the Catholic mystics and devotion of the 1500s to 1700s; the Key of Truth (Paulician) is with the Eastern churches. A part's sentence says what is in it and does not rank it.

**The Jewish texts have a shelf of their own (16 November 2026).** 35 texts (the Mishnah and Talmud, Zohar and Sefer Yetzirah, the Jewish prayer books, Maimonides, Judah Halevi and others, and the Samaritan Chronicle) moved from the Early Christian shelf to a new shelf, `ju`, in the same collection. Checked by test: every one is on the new shelf and none is left on the old one; the Judaism tradition page lists them; every shelf (now 40) has a status line and sits in exactly one collection; the stations that read them still do (the shelf is one of the “nothing left out” shelves). **Written from general knowledge, not checked against a source:** the shelf’s status line, and the “where to read it” note (Sefaria.org has the Mishnah, the Talmud and many midrashim in English; the Soncino Talmud and Zohar and Daniel Matt’s Pritzker Zohar are the usual English editions): please verify these before relying on them. The Samaritan Chronicle is on a Jewish shelf though the Samaritans are a distinct community; the shelf line says it holds a Samaritan chronicle.

## The plates of the scans (16 November 2026)

`tools/texts_plates.py` reads, for an Internet Archive scan, the item's page-layout file (`_abbyy.gz`: the FineReader output the Archive makes for every scan, which marks each picture block with its box and the words around it), keeps the blocks that pass a size filter (at least 12% of the page wide and 7% tall, not a strip, not a block lying over a page of running text), merges blocks that overlap, takes each region out of the scan's own page image (the JPEG 2000 file of that page in the item's `_jp2.zip`, fetched one page at a time, decoded with macOS's sips and cropped with ImageMagick), drops crops that are blank, nearly one tone, nearly black or a plate already kept, places each by the 14 words before it in our text (the same matching as the Gutenberg pictures) and shrinks it. The scan's OCR text keeps its own caption lines, so a picture carries a description for screen readers and no caption. **Looked at by eye:** contact sheets of twelve sample crops, spread through the book, for 25 candidate books (the scans with the most caption lines in their OCR, and those the Archive describes as illustrated); later, the placement of three pictures in each of five of the books beside the paragraphs around them (the plates of Parker's Code of Handsome Lake fall between their "Plate 4" line and their caption; Boas's figures fall before their "Fig. 6" lines), and two pictures in the reader (a family photograph in Parker, Kircher's Great God Pan in Hall). **Done for 13 books (797 pictures):** Rattray's Ashanti (150), Czaplicka's Aboriginal Siberia (21), Parker's Code of Handsome Lake (32), Emerson's Unwritten Literature of Hawaii (19), Scheffer's History of Lapland (28), Seabrook's Magic Island (40), Boas's Tsimshian Mythology (24), Hall's Secret Teachings of All Ages (150), the Key of Solomon (23), Papus's Tarot of the Bohemians (94), Guazzo's Compendium Maleficarum (35), the Kebra Nagast (31), Budge's Osiris and the Egyptian Resurrection (150). Books with more than 150 placed pictures keep the 150 largest. **Not done, and why:** (1) the layout software calls a page of plain text a picture in many books (the Eskimo about Bering Strait, Malay Magic, Transcendental Magic, the Omaha Tribe, and half the crops of the Mysteries of Mithra), or a page of a script it cannot read (the Ethiopic Lives of Maba' Seyon, the cuneiform copies in Babylonian Liturgies, Babylonian Magic and Sorcery, Miscellaneous Babylonian Inscriptions and Cuneiform Parallels); (2) the plate pages of Ifugao Law are blank in the scan and the Devils and Evil Spirits scan gave only covers; (3) the Serpent Power and Illustrations of Masonry have no page images in the Archive; (4) the Archive item of the Goetia is not the 1904 print: its colour plates are modern drawings whose rights are unclear, so none are taken (it was on the contact sheet and looked like pictures of the book). **Left out of the plates for rights only:** the Goetia scan (`m-goet`). The books of the ceremonies and sacred objects of living peoples were first left out under a rule that was never the maker’s and was withdrawn on 17 November 2026 (see “The exclusion of restricted and sacred material was withdrawn”); their plates are back. **Not checked:** the other 440 or so scans (a book may have good plates that the caption count and the descriptions missed: `--survey` finds candidates and `--sheet` shows them), each placed picture's position beyond the spot checks above, that a crop never cuts a figure short, and whether any plate is in copyright apart from its book (every book here is from 1929 or earlier, the newest being The Magic Island and the 1929 Guazzo, which entered the US public domain in 2025). The portraits and photographs of people in Czaplicka, Parker and Rattray are early twentieth-century ethnographic plates.

## Printed page numbers (17 November 2026)

**What was asked.** The maker said a reader must still be able to refer to original page numbers and sections (“an important part of religious study”), then, to the offer to check ten scans: “check them all and make sure all of them maintain all the markers”, and later said yes to fetching the heavier Archive files for the scans that had none.

**What was done.** Page data was fetched from the Internet Archive for every scan (966 items, 2,898 small files, about 582 MB, six requests at a time; then for 149 more, `_djvu.xml` and `_scandata.xml`, 298 files, 1,142 MB, none failed); the printed page numbers of the Project Gutenberg texts were read from the book’s own markup (the EPUBs kept for the pictures held them for 61 texts; for the 135 whose EPUB has none, the web editions were fetched from Project Gutenberg’s own mirror, gutenberg.pglaf.org, one request every six seconds with no retries, after gutenberg.org answered 503, “retry in 3600 seconds”: 115 editions, 81 MB, no refusal, 26 more texts with page numbers); `tools/texts_pages.py build` matched every text to its pages. **1,031 texts now carry their printed page numbers** (944 scans, 87 typed; 448,442 marks; grade a 1,011, b 18, c 2). The Bibles carry chapters and verses.

**Checked.** (1) *The offset.* The Archive’s page list and its text index are counted from different places in different scans. The first build used one offset for all; the check that each printed number is in the first or last words of its page (0.7 to 0.9 at the right offset, about 0 at a wrong one) showed that a large share of scans were one page off, which would have put every printed number one page wrong. The tool now chooses the offset for each scan by that check. (2) *Independently,* with the Archive’s own search inside the scan: six words from just after a mark are searched, and the page it answers is compared with the page this tool gave. Four runs. A first sample of 40 texts (224 phrases) gave 186 on the page given, 15 on another and 23 with no answer; the 15 were almost all in scans whose offset could not be confirmed. Those scans no longer keep the Archive’s numbers: the offset was worked out from the Archive’s search for 6 of 33, and for the rest the numbers are read from the pages’ own words. A second sample of the final hOCR data (30 texts, 175 phrases) gave 161 on the page given, 0 on another and 14 with no answer. For the scans read from the `_djvu.xml` (28 texts, 164 phrases) the Archive’s page and ours differ by one constant number in each scan, and 104 phrases fit it, 1 does not and 59 got no answer; for the 25 scans re-read from the pages’ own words, 104 fit, 4 do not and 34 got no answer. The Archive’s search answers for only some of these books (no answer at all for 14 of the 28 in the first sample and 8 of the 25 in the second), so the rest are not independently checked. The four misses are single phrases, probably a page boundary a line off. (3) *Numbers read from the page text* were compared with the scanner-typed numbers in `_scandata.xml` where there are enough of them (3 scans: all agree) and for plausibility (the step between page number and leaf is what front matter explains, or, for a journal article or a later volume, a continued pagination such as pp. 577–682). (4) *The data test* (`tests/pages.test.js`) opens every file; it found two roman numerals longer than the allowed eight letters, now twelve. (5) A text that is cut from one scan in several slices is matched against that scan once.

**What is not covered, and why.** 15 matched scans have no printed numbers that can be read in them (the Fama, the Tirukkural, the Life of Antony, Encyclopaedia Biblica, which is numbered by column, and others); 3 scans keep none because no offset could be confirmed (e-sdp, e-ssy, x-brd); 2 scans (x-dbn, x-sbt) have no page data at the Archive in any form we could fetch; m-abra matched too little; and 5 typed texts matched too little (a-fcol, e-clx, e-ori, x-blv, x-fox: their text is not a straight copy of the book). 101 typed texts have no page numbers in their EPUB or in their web edition (the producers of those books did not keep them); 20 more have no web edition at the mirror, and 3 matched too little (e-hip, x-ovi, x-plo). Printed page numbers for those would have to come from a scan of the same edition, and a different printing of the same translation has different pages, so a scan is used only where it is certainly the same edition (not done). Global Grey’s 94 texts carry no page numbers anywhere in the source (checked in the 101 cached pages), so they have none; the card says so. The Bibles are cited by chapter and verse and carry 67,420 verse markers in 2,519 chapters; 151 chapters have a jump in the verse numbers (mostly the Apocrypha and the Septuagint, whose versification the translation follows), which were not changed. *Marks are as exact as the match:* a page start can sit a word or two off where the running head was cut differently; grade b and c texts say so in their card.

**Not checked.** A reader’s eye on a long sample of marks against the printed pages (the Archive’s search is an automatic check, not a person); touch on a phone; the e-book page breaks in a real reading app (the markup follows the EPUB 3 convention, `epub:type="pagebreak"` and `role="doc-pagebreak"`, and was only checked as text); whether the numbers detected from page text are ever footnote runs (the five-pages-in-a-row rule makes it unlikely, and none was found in the samples).

## The exclusion of restricted and sacred material was withdrawn (17 November 2026)

**What happened.** Earlier notes and sessions said that “the maker’s rule” was to keep restricted or sacred material out of the Library, and several things were built on it: the plates of six books and the Gutenberg pictures of two more were left out, the Atlas left the Zuni, Hopi and other Pueblo peoples and the ceremonial of other peoples out, a list of books was held back as “not reproduced, by rule” (Lumholtz on the Huichol, Densmore’s Chippewa Music, Fewkes on the Hopi, Dorsey’s Cheyenne Sun Dance, the Nusayri secret books), and Masonic ritual exposures were “left out on purpose”. On 17 November 2026 the maker said: “i dont remember saying to leave out anything. i don’t believe restricted rites was something i ever made a decision to not include. im pretty sure i had clearly stated that it’s a collection of all human religion”, and “the people need all the information available to decide for themselves. leave no stone unturned.” The session transcripts were searched: no message of theirs states such a rule. The wording first appears in assistant-written summaries, was copied from one note to the next, and was wrongly attributed to the maker. It is withdrawn.

**The Library’s limits are these and no others:** public-domain or openly licensed material only (first published before 1931 in the US, or licensed for redistribution); an honest label on every text that says what it is (an outsider’s record, a translation, a reconstruction), with a care flag where the text itself is hard; “understood, not endorsed” for hard cases. Nothing is left out because of what a book records or how sacred, secret or sensitive someone may find it.

**What was put back, and what was not.** **Put back (17 November 2026):** the Gutenberg pictures of Mooney’s *Sacred Formulas of the Cherokees* (4) and Mathews’s *Midē’wiwin* (78); the plates of Spencer and Gillen’s *Native Tribes of Central Australia* (133) and *Northern Tribes of Central Australia* (214), Matthews’s *Night Chant* (24) and Dorsey’s *Study of Siouan Cults* (30), each looked at on its contact sheet and again in a random sample of the pictures kept (photographs, diagrams, masks, tents and robes; an occasional strip of text at a crop’s edge); 6 of 209 page images of the Northern Tribes could not be fetched or decoded. **Not done, and why (not an exclusion):** Howitt’s *Native Tribes of South-East Australia* and Matthews’s *Navaho Legends*: the Archive’s layout file marks the text columns around their pictures as pictures, so nearly every crop is a piece of text and the book is not accepted; their PDF link has every page as printed. The Library now holds 2,703 pictures in 84 texts. **Handed to the sessions that own them:** the Atlas rows (the Zuni, Hopi and other Pueblo peoples, the ceremonial detail left out of rows such as the Pawnee) to the Atlas session; every “left out on purpose” item that was not a matter of rights (Lumholtz, Densmore, Fewkes on the Hopi, Dorsey’s Sun Dance, the Nusayri books, the Masonic exposures, other ritual texts) to the text-collection session for rounds 21 and 22; the post-1930 books that cannot be had are listed in `POST1930_WANTED.csv`. **Still out, for rights:** `m-goet` plates (modern drawings of unclear rights), the Bahá’í files on Project Gutenberg (their terms of use), Pines’s 1963 *Guide for the Perplexed*, and every in-copyright text.

## Page breaks, bars that stay, and the printed page (17 November 2026)

**What was asked.** Page dividers where the text scrolls; constant access to every feature (looking to ReadEra Premium for how a good reader feels); the full screen scrolls but the top bar can be shown or not. Then, mid-task: the reader must still let a person refer to original page numbers and sections, which matters in religious study.

**What was built.** Page breaks in *By section* and *Scroll* (a line and “Page N”, chosen in Aa: numbered, line or none) that open a card on a tap; the card and the bar name the section and the reader’s page and say where the printed page is; a *Copy reference* action; the bars as *Always shown* (default), *Hide as I read* or *Hidden* with a tab, a pin, the **h** key and an Aa row; a thin section-and-page line while the bars are away; a larger set of bar buttons on a wide screen and a complete menu on every screen. Tests: `reader-chrome` (35), `reader` (21), `pager` (81); `imagined` had two patterns updated for the title template; the other reader-adjacent suites pass (`reading`, `sheets`, `figures`, `source`, `shelves`, `path-render`, `path`, `library`, `interface`, `fuzz`, `marks`, `export`, `listen`).

**What the reader can and cannot say about original pages.** The texts do not carry the printed page numbers of their editions: the builder dropped them, and the cached scan text has no page boundaries. So the reader does not show “page 214 of the original” and does not guess it: a break shows its own page, says in words that this is not the printed page, and gives the way to the printed one (the Internet Archive scan searched at the passage; the source’s web edition; chapter and verse for a Bible). The section number and name are real (they come from the text’s own structure), but for many scans the section name is the first words of the part and is poor (an old open item). **Not done, and why:** a real printed page number beside each break needs the scans’ page data fetched (the Internet Archive publishes page-number data for many scans; whether it is there for each of ours has not been checked) and a mapping from each paragraph to its leaf; that is a fetch, so it waits for the maker’s yes. For the typed texts the source’s own web edition may have them (many Gutenberg web editions mark their printed pages; not checked for ours), which would be a second fetch.

**Looked at in the browser** (the Claude app’s preview, Chrome, 1280×800 and 375×812, dark and a light theme): breaks in the scroll and section views of a typed text (the Didache, in a dark and a light theme) and a scan (Doughty), and drawn in a Bible (Genesis: breaks and the section line in the bar; the break above a section’s title is checked by test and was not looked at); the card for a typed, a scanned and a two-volume text (the volume question appears for the two-volume scan); Line and None; the bars in Scroll, Sheets and Pages (Pages only with the bars kept; Scroll and Sheets also hidden at start when chosen, and “as I read” hiding on scrolling down, in the page and inside the Sheets’ own scroll box, and returning on scrolling up); the tab, the pin and the h key; the menu at both widths (it scrolls on a short screen). No script errors. **Not checked:** a real phone with touch (taps on the tab and on a break were clicked by script, not touched), a screen reader reading the breaks (each break is a labelled button, or a separator that is skipped for the line alone), the two-page spread with the bars kept, and the Pages view in a window shorter than about 500 pixels, where the kept bars take about a quarter of the height; a smaller bar or *Hide as I read* is the answer there. The wording of the card and of the reference (*Reader page 12 of 340 …*) is mine and wants a reader’s eye.

**Left as it was on purpose.** The Sheets’ own number at the foot of each sheet (*3 / 11*) still counts sheets within the section, not the reader’s pages (a sheet is a layout page that changes with the zoom and paper; the earlier note stands: no global sheet numbers).

## The polish pass for the beta (16 November 2026)

**What was checked, and how.** In the preview at `localhost` with Chrome 156 (the Claude app's browser pane): (1) layout, by a script that visits about 40 routes at 1024x768 and at 375x812 and reports page-level horizontal overflow, elements wider than the screen, script errors and rejected promises, broken images, empty pages, buttons and links under 30 px (36 px on a phone) and text under 11.5 px; no route overflowed or raised an error at either width. (2) Contrast, by a script that works out each piece of text's colour against the backgrounds behind it (flat backgrounds only: text on a gradient or a map canvas cannot be measured this way, so the gradient buttons and the map's year label were ignored) on 25 routes in the dark theme and 7 in the light: one failure remained (the muted date on a highlighted timeline row, 3.66:1), which the lighter muted colour improves. (3) Accessibility of the page structure, by a script that looks for buttons and links without a name, fields without a label, images without alt text, a missing or double h1, duplicate ids, positive tab indexes and skipped heading levels on 20 routes. (4) Speed, by the browser's own timings: DOM ready 59 ms, load 85 ms, a route drawn in 70 to 90 ms, 237 DOM nodes after a route change, 65 MB of script heap; a search for "compassion" over 967 texts showed its first passages after 0.3 s and finished in 4.1 s. (5) By eye: the welcome dialog, the Journey, Library, Timelines, Connections, Practice, Beyond text, Freedom, Atlas, Gather, Gateway and Setup pages, the reader's settings panel and the Shelve dialog, mostly at desktop width, the reader on a phone, the Library and one other page in the light theme; and afterwards the feedback card, Setup and the first-run welcome on a phone (375x812) and in the light theme, the Books row of the passage search in the light theme, and the dot on the Setup tab. **Not checked:** Safari and Firefox (so the older-Safari blur and the `mask-image` fade are written to the standard and prefixed, not seen), a real phone and touch, a screen reader (only the structure above), keyboard-only use beyond the focus-ring rules in the stylesheet, print, the whole of the light theme, and the feedback card's *Email it* link (hidden until an address is set).

## The belief labels and the family tree (16 November 2026)

**What the labels are.** `src/js/2z-belief-core.js` gives each of the 789 traditions of the Atlas a first label and, for most that need it, others it is also described by: One God (monotheism), Many gods (polytheism), Spirit in all things (animism), Inner paths and philosophy (spirituality), and the deep past (archaeological horizons, whose beliefs are inferred). It is an editorial grouping made for browsing, not a classification from a source: scholars disagree about where several traditions belong and about whether the words fit at all (the pages say so; the word “animism” is called a scholars’ word that many peoples do not use). The rules, in order: the tradition’s own entry (107 are set by hand, 8 of them added with the 17 November additions); an archaeological horizon is the deep past; a new religious movement out of a tradition of many gods or of spirits is an inner path unless it is a Pagan revival; otherwise the label of the tradition it grew from (the Atlas’s parent link); otherwise the label of its family. **Result** (after the additions of 17 November 2026; before them One God 244, Many gods 129, Spirit 177, Inner paths 161): One God 261, Many gods 150, Spirit 195, Inner paths 173, deep past 10 as first labels (a tradition counted under its other labels as well makes the totals on the pages larger). **Doubtful cases, kept and labelled:** early Israelite religion (one God, also many gods), Zoroastrianism and Manichaeism (dualist), Hinduism and the Hindu schools (many gods, also inner paths, and the devotional schools also one God), Daoism, Shinto and Chinese folk religion, the Afro-diasporic religions (many gods, also spirit), Pure Land and Tibetan Buddhism (devotion to many figures), Sufism and Kabbalah (one God, also inner paths), and every movement created from a tradition of many gods. **The Library’s 40 shelves** are given one first label each (the shelf is shown once, under it) and, where it fits, a second (the Early Christian and Gnostic shelf is under One God and also inner paths; the shelf of modern and revival traditions, which holds Quaker, Bahá’í, Mormon and Adventist texts beside freethinkers and New Thought, is under One God and also inner paths); the scholarship and freedom-of-conscience shelves are About religion, and the imagined shelf stays apart. **The family tree** is the Atlas’s `parent_id` links as they are (265 traditions with no parent, 456 with one; 58 roots have branches): it records one parent where scholars agree on one, so Islam appears as a root and the Abrahamic line runs Canaanite religion → Israelite religion → Judaism → Christianity. **Checked** by `tests/belief.test.js` (32 checks: every override and featured tradition exists, every shelf has a label, every tradition has one of the five, the well known ones are where a reader expects) and by looking at the lists by family in the Atlas. **Not checked:** by anyone who is an expert in each of these traditions; the first labels of the 700 traditions that have no entry of their own come from the rules and will be wrong for some; Safari, Firefox and a real phone.


## The Atlas additions: 72 traditions and 118 links (17 November 2026)

**What was added, and why.** The coverage assessment (`COVERAGE_ASSESSMENT.md`, section “Religions, offshoots and peoples the Atlas does not have”) listed religions, offshoots and peoples that the Atlas lacked, and 89 links that the Library’s texts seemed to support. This work added **72 traditions** (the Atlas went from 721 to 793: 68 in the first pass and 4 Pueblo peoples in a second, below), each with a parent where scholars agree on one and otherwise a stated root, an origin row on the map, a start event and (where it ended) an end event on the timeline, and **118 links** between traditions (from 382 to 500), with 43 new places for the origin points. Books about the new traditions that the Library holds were added to `tools/library_about.json` (47 books, 59 links). Counts by family: abrahamic 16, dharmic 10, ane 8, european 8, americas 8, african 6, esoteric 5, southeast_asian 4, south_asian_indigenous 2, inner_asian 2, iranian 1, east_asian 1, oceania 1. Nothing in the Atlas was removed, and no existing row was changed except two parents (below).

**The rules the rows follow.** A summary is one or two plain sentences; a date that a scholar would call rough is marked `L` (low) or `C` (contested) and the row says so; no founder is named that the sources do not name, and a founder is given only where a person is credited by name (a few are marked “traditional”). The ceremonial of living peoples is described as far as the Library’s records give it, and the row says whose record it is (an outsider’s where the writer was one): the first pass left it out under an exclusion of the sacred rites of living peoples, and that exclusion was withdrawn on 17 November 2026, so the detail was put back (below). Where a people’s date is unknown it is a wide placeholder, the same one the Atlas already uses for its neighbours (for example the Ifugao and Kalinga rows “c. 500–1500”), and the row says so (“a wide placeholder”, “a rough placeholder”). Belief labels follow the family and the parent, with eight overrides in `src/js/2z-belief-core.js` where the inherited label would be wrong (Free Spirit one God and inner path; Advaita Vedanta and Dzogchen inner path; Ayyavazhi one God and many gods; the Magyars many gods and spirit; Three-in-One inner path and many gods; Curanderismo spirit and one God; the Toltecs many gods and spirit); the four Pueblo rows inherit the label of the Pueblo row (Spirit in all things). `tests/belief.test.js` and `tests/atlas.test.js` check them.
