**Two older rows changed parent.** Ossetian traditional religion now grows from the new Sarmatian and Alan religion (which grows from Scythian religion; the Ossetians are the Alans’ descendants), and the Cult of Cybele now grows from Phrygian religion (the Mother goddess of Phrygia). Their lineage depth and root were recomputed (`tests/atlas.test.js` checks both). Nothing else of the 721 original traditions, 2,199 presence rows, 382 interactions or 5,537 events was altered; the handoff’s own fixtures in `tests/atlas.test.js` now run on those original rows only (the additions are listed in `tools/atlas_src/handoff/added_2026-11-17.json`) and the added rows have checks of their own.

**Left out.** *Itelmen (Kamchadal) religion*: the Library’s only account is the scattered remarks in Czaplicka’s *Aboriginal Siberia* (82 mentions, from Steller and Krasheninnikov of the 18th century: birth customs, one yearly ceremony, “witches” who explain dreams), and she calls the information “meagre”; that is too little to describe their religion without guessing. Separate rows for each Naga people (the Library’s four books are of the Angami, Sema, Lhota and the Manipur Nagas) and for Perennialism apart from Traditionalism were not made; they are one row each (“Naga religions”, “Traditionalism (Perennialism)”). Qalandar and Malamati are one row (“the way of blame”). The Ekoi are in, but the Library holds no book on them (its two Talbot books are of the Ibibio), so that row rests on general knowledge. The Zuni and the other Pueblo peoples were left out of the first pass under the exclusion that was withdrawn on 17 November 2026; they are in now (next paragraph).

**The second pass: the Pueblo peoples, and the ceremonial put back (17 November 2026).** (1) **Four rows** for the Pueblo peoples, children of the older Pueblo religion row: Zuni (A’shiwi) religion, Hopi religion, Keresan Pueblo religion (Acoma, Laguna, Zia and the Rio Grande Keres) and Tanoan Pueblo religion (Tewa, Tiwa and Towa). Each has an origin point (Zuni Pueblo, Oraibi, Acoma Pueblo, San Ildefonso Pueblo), a start of c. 1300–1400 (the Atlas’s Pueblo row says c. 1300; the confidence is `L` because the villages are older), a start event on the timeline, and says in its summary that it is an outsider’s record. They rest on the Library’s copy of Hodge’s *Handbook of American Indians* (1907–10), whose articles on the Zuñi, the Tewa and on ceremony, and its entries for the Keresan pueblos and the Hopi clans, were read; Cushing’s *Zuni Folk Tales* (a-zuni; not read for the row) and Lummis’s *The Man Who Married the Moon* (t-lumm; read for the Isleta) are mapped to the rows. Library books on the Pueblo peoples listed at the same time (Fewkes, Voth and Dorsey on the Hopi, Stevenson on the Zuñi and the Sia: a-fkt, a-fws, a-fft, a-vpw, a-vso, a-vmr, a-stz, a-sia) are mapped to the rows in `tools/library_about.json` and were not read for them. (2) **Two links** from the Pueblo religion row to the Navajo and Apache rows (influence, c. 1500–1700 CE): the Handbook says the masks and dry-paintings of their ceremonies were probably borrowed from the Pueblo tribes. (3) **Thirteen rows had their ceremonial put back**, each from the Library text that was read for it: the Pawnee (Fletcher’s *The Hako*), the Omaha (Fletcher and La Flesche), the Bontoc Igorot (Jenks), the Tinguian (Cole), the Karen (Marshall), the Shan (Milne), the Naga (Hutton), the Andamanese (Radcliffe-Brown), the Kamba (Lindblom), the Lango (Driberg), the Thonga (Junod), the Shrauta ritual tradition (the Shatapatha Brahmana) and the Aghori (Oman, a hostile outsider’s record). The summaries now say what those records say, and each row’s cells below say what was read and what a specialist should still add. The sources are public-domain books in the Library; where the writer was an outsider the row says so.

**What each tradition rests on, and what a specialist should check.** A row that begins “Library:” rests on a Library text that was read (or searched for the names the row gives); a row that begins “General knowledge” or “Web sources” rests on textbook history or on the web page named, and any Library book listed after it is there to be read, not something checked. 39 rows begin with a Library text; 33 rest on general knowledge or the web: Edomite religion, Ammonite religion, Kassite religion, Luwian religion, Illyrian religion, Iberian religion (pre-Roman Spain), Celtiberian and Lusitanian religion, Gaulish and Gallo-Roman religion, Sarmatian and Alan religion, Beguines and Beghards, Fraticelli (Spiritual Franciscans), Pelagianism, Diggers (True Levellers), Muggletonians, Maronite Church, Melkite Greek Catholic Church, Nubian Christianity (Nobadia, Makuria and Alodia), Serpent-handling churches (Signs Following), Ni‘matullāhī order, Qalandar and Malamati currents (the way of blame), Advaita Vedanta, Ayyavazhi, Siddha Yoga, Rimé movement (Tibetan non-sectarian movement), Shambhala Buddhism (Shambhala International), Three-in-One teaching (Sanyi jiao), Curanderismo, Astrology (Western), Mesmerism (animal magnetism), Bavarian Illuminati, Traditionalism (Perennialism), Cargo cults (Melanesian cargo movements), Ekoi (Ejagham) religion. Every row also says what a specialist should still check.

| Tradition | Dates in the Atlas | Rests on | What to check |
|---|---|---|---|
| Philistine religion (`philistine`; ane, a root) | confidence M | Library: Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 9, “Philistines” (Macalister): the Medinet Habu record, Dagon at Gaza and Ashdod, the cult lasting to the Maccabees; Macalister, The Philistines (x-phi). The fall of the Philistine cities to Babylon (604 BCE) is general knowledge. | The end date (604 BCE, the Babylonian conquest of Ashkelon and Ekron) and the Aegean origin, which the ERE of 1917 puts as “Caphtor” (Crete) and recent work argues from pottery and the Ekron inscription (not in the Library). |
| Edomite religion (`edomite`; ane, child of `canaanite`) | confidence L | General knowledge (Assyrian royal records name Edomite kings “Qaus-malaka” and “Qaus-gabri”; the Hebrew Bible; the Idumeans). Library: Encyclopaedia Biblica and Hastings’s Dictionary of the Bible (articles on Edom) were not read for this row. | The start date (the Hebrew Bible places Edom earlier than any Assyrian record, c. 800 BCE), the Qos worship, and the end of the kingdom (6th century BCE) against the Nabataeans and Idumea. |
| Ammonite religion (`ammonite`; ane, child of `canaanite`) | confidence L | General knowledge (Hebrew Bible; Assyrian records from 853 BCE; the Amman Citadel inscription; 1 Maccabees 5 for the Ammonites in the 2nd century BCE). | Milkom as the national god, and the dates (same wide window as the Atlas’s Moabite row). |
| Kassite religion (`kassite`; ane, a root) | confidence M | General knowledge (Kassite dynasty of Babylon, conventional dates c. 1531–1155 BCE; the god lists and the kudurru boundary stones). Nothing in the Library is about the Kassites alone. | The list of Kassite gods and the start date (the Kassite dynasty is dated 1595–1531 BCE by different chronologies). Kept as a root: the Kassites took up Babylonian religion but did not derive from it. |
| Phrygian religion (`phrygian`; ane, a root) | confidence L | Library: Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 9, “Phrygians” (Ramsay; read: settlement before 1000 BCE, the Mother goddess first in the older religion). Showerman, The Great Mother of the Gods (x-gmg) is in the Library, not read for this row. | The end window (the Phrygian language and cults lasted into the Roman period) and the place of Sabazios and Men, which the Library’s ERE article treats at more length than the one sentence here. |
| Lydian religion (`lydian`; ane, a root) | confidence L | Library: Herodotus (x-hrd): the temple of Kybebe at Sardis burned in 498 BCE (V.102), the Lydian kings’ gifts to Delphi (I.14, I.50–51). | The wide end window, and whether to add the Lydian Artemis (Artimu) and the god Santas from the Lydian inscriptions, which were not checked here. |
| Lycian religion (`lycian`; ane, a root) | confidence L | Library: Herodotus (x-hrd) I.173 (read: the Lycians sprung from Crete). The Letoon and the Lycian language are general knowledge. | Both date windows (very wide: the Lukka lands are named in Hittite texts of about 1400 BCE, Lycian inscriptions are of the 5th–4th centuries BCE) and the gods named in the Lycian inscriptions, which were not checked. |
| Luwian religion (`luwian`; ane, a root) | confidence L | General knowledge. The Atlas’s own Hittite row says “absorbing Hattic, Hurrian and Luwian gods”; Garstang, The Hittite Empire (x-hte) is in the Library and was not read for this row. | Both date windows and the gods named; Luwian is a language group, not one state, so the row is a loose umbrella. |
| Dacian and Getic religion (Zalmoxis) (`dacian`; european, child of `thracian`) | confidence L | Library: Herodotus Book IV (x-hrd). The Dacian sanctuaries and the Roman conquest of 106 CE are general knowledge. | The start date (Herodotus is of the 5th century BCE; the Getae are older), the end window (Roman Dacia was given up about 271 CE), and whether Zalmoxis was a god, a priest-king or a hero, which the ancient writers dispute. |
| Illyrian religion (`illyrian`; european, a root) | confidence L | General knowledge (Roman-period dedications to Bindus Neptunus and Medaurus; sun and serpent symbolism in Illyrian art; Wilkes, The Illyrians, 1992). Nothing in the Library is about the Illyrians. | Everything: no Library text covers this row. The god names, the sun and serpent signs, and both date windows should be checked by a specialist. Kept as a root: the Illyrian peoples are not agreed to belong to any larger family of religions. |
| Iberian religion (pre-Roman Spain) (`iberian`; european, a root) | confidence L | General knowledge (the Iberian sanctuaries, votive bronzes, the Lady of Elche). Library: the Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 2, “Basques”, speaks of Iberian deities taken into the Roman pantheon (Strabo). | Everything: no Library text covers this row beyond Strabo’s remarks in the ERE. The date windows should be checked. |
| Celtiberian and Lusitanian religion (`celtiberian_lusitanian`; european, child of `celtic`) | confidence L | General knowledge (the Lusitanian inscriptions of Cabeço das Fráguas and Lamas de Moledo, the Celtiberian rock sanctuary of Peñalba de Villastar, the Botorrita bronzes). Nothing in the Library is about these peoples. | Everything: the god names, the Celtic parent (Celtiberian is Celtic; Lusitanian is the disputed part) and both date windows. |
| Pictish religion (`pictish`; european, child of `celtic`) | confidence L | Library: Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 10, “Picts” §6 (Ailred’s Life of Ninian; Adamnán’s Life of Columba: the druids of Brude and the spring worshipped as a god). | The Celtic parent (most scholars now call Pictish a Celtic language with a possible older layer, but the ERE of 1917 leaves it open), and the start date, which is that of the name “Picti” (297 CE) and not of the religion. |
| Gaulish and Gallo-Roman religion (`gaulish`; european, child of `celtic`) | confidence M | General knowledge (Caesar, Gallic War VI, which is not in the Library; the Coligny calendar; Roman-period dedications). MacCulloch’s volume of The Mythology of All Races (x-mr03) has the Celtic material and was not read for this row. | The gods named and the start window (the La Tène culture, not the religion, begins about 450 BCE). |
| Basque religion before Christianity (`basque_pagan`; european, a root) | confidence C | Library: Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 2, “Basques” (Strabo on the moon-night dances, the worship of nymphs, fountains and trees; the Latin dedications); Webster, Basque Legends (l-webs). | The date windows are those of the written evidence and are marked contested; the goddess Mari, now the best-known Basque figure, is left out because the Library holds no record of her. |
| Magyar religion before Christianity (`magyar_pagan`; european, a root) | confidence L | Library: Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics vol. 6, “Hungarians”: Isten, the táltos priests, white horses sacrificed, the 11th-century laws against the old rites. | The start window (the Magyars are older than their entry into the Carpathian Basin in 895; the religion is of the steppe) and the 1046 and 1061 pagan risings, which are not described in the Library. |
| Sarmatian and Alan religion (`sarmatian_alan`; iranian, child of `scythian`) | confidence L | General knowledge (Ammianus Marcellinus XXXI.2, not in the Library). Library: Herodotus (x-hrd) names the Sauromatae in Book IV; the Atlas’s own Ossetian row calls the Ossetians the Alans’ descendants. | The Ammianus passage, the wide end window (the Alans of the Caucasus kept their religion until about the 10th century), and the re-parenting of the Ossetian row under this one. |
| Beguines and Beghards (`beguines`; abrahamic, child of `catholicism`) | confidence M | General knowledge. Library: Lea, History of the Inquisition (x-lea) treats the Beghards and Beguines at length (searched, not read in full); the Catholic Encyclopedia (x-ce02, “Beguines and Beghards”) was not read. **The 118 links.** 80 come from `SUGGESTED_ATLAS_LINKS.csv` (80 of its 89 rows kept) and 38 are links of the new traditions. Each carries a type from the Atlas’s codes, a date or period written as its neighbours are (a “c.” where the date is rough), a place where one is known (the origin point of the tradition it ends at, where the event was there) and a one-line note that names the evidence. 30 of the 80 CSV links and 21 of the 38 new links were checked against a Library text and name it in the note; the rest are textbook history, recorded as such.

**The CSV rows that were dropped (9 of 89).** *Sethian Gnosticism → Manichaeism* and *Mandaeism → Manichaeism* (how far each shaped Mani is disputed, and no Library text settles it); *Mandaeism → Gnosticism* (Mandaeism is itself a Gnostic religion, so “influence” has no clear direction); *Mazdakism → Manichaeism* (the Library’s article on Mazdak, in the Encyclopaedia of Religion and Ethics, describes a communistic sect of the late 5th century and its Zoroastrian opponents and does not derive it from Mani); *Pythagoreanism → Orphism* (the direction is much disputed; Herodotus II.81 says the Orphic rites are Egyptian and Pythagorean); *Waldensians → Protestantism* (the Reformed faith influenced the Waldensians, not the other way: that link is kept as *Reformed Christianity → Waldensians*, from Comba); *Bektashi → Alevism* (“closest to” is not a derivation, and the Hasluck passages searched do not say it); *Molokans → Doukhobors* (Conybeare treats them as two separate Spiritual-Christian bodies and reports a schism among the Molokans over a leader’s teaching, not a descent from the Doukhobors); *Tendai → Shinto* (Sanno Ichijitsu Shinto is in neither Anesaki’s nor Reischauer’s book in the Library; the Shingon–Shinto link, Ryobu Shinto, is in both and is kept).

**Directions put right.** The CSV had 36 of its kept rows with source and target the wrong way round for the Atlas’s convention (28 “derive”, 3 “influence”, 3 “convert”, 1 “schism” and 1 “syncretism” rows). The Atlas’s rule is that a derive link runs from the tradition that came first to the one that arose out of it (Judaism → Christianity, Vaishnavism → Gaudiya Vaishnavism) and that a convert link runs from the religion taken up to the tradition whose people took it up (Christianity → Fijian religion, in the older rows), so Tijaniyya → Sufism became *Sufism → Tijaniyya*, Sōka Gakkai → Nichiren Buddhism became *Nichiren Buddhism → Sōka Gakkai*, Sikhism → Sufism (Baba Farid in the Guru Granth Sahib) became *Sufism → Sikhism*, and Lahore Ahmadiyya → Ahmadiyya (a schism) became *Ahmadiyya → Lahore Ahmadiyya*. 11 types were also changed where the CSV’s said too much or the wrong thing: Hòa Hảo is a *reform* of Buddhism, the Doukhobors and Rissho Kosei Kai are *schisms*, Bogomils → Cathars and the Salafi method taken from the Wahhabis are *influences* (Lea: the Concorrezan dualism arose “under the influence of the Bogomili”), Sarvāstivāda → Mahāyāna and Obeah’s Akan roots are *influences*, Shingon from Chinese esoteric Buddhism is a *transmission*, Sol Invictus and Mithras are *coexisting* cults, John Frum and the Māori are *derive* links from their parents. `tests/atlas.test.js` fails if a derive, schism or reform link runs from the younger to the older.

**Doubtful and kept, labelled.** 8 rows are marked contested (`C`): the Free Spirit, the Ranters, the Khlysty, Dzogchen, the Toltecs, the Andamanese, Basque religion before Christianity and cargo cults; the summary says why. Several dates are windows, not years: the Nubian church’s end (the 14th to 15th centuries), the Khlysty and Skoptsy end (the Soviet years to the 1970s), the Fraticelli’s end, and every placeholder for the field-record peoples. The Khlysty and Skoptsy ends, the Muggletonians’ end (1979), the Beguines’ end (the death of Marcella Pattyn, 2013), the Ayyavazhi, Siddha Yoga and Shambhala dates and the serpent-handling origin come from web sources and are marked so above. Nothing was added to the app that uses a server, an account or the network: it still sends nothing. The Sources list on the About the Atlas page now names these additions in one line (`sources.json`, added after the first build).

**Second reading, 17 November 2026.** A second reader went through all 68 rows and the 116 links against sources outside the Library: encyclopaedias and reference pages (Britannica, Encyclopedia.com, university and museum pages, the orders’ and movements’ own histories, and Wikipedia where nothing better turned up), and the Library’s own texts where a row rests on one; the map points were compared with the known positions of the places they name. Most of it stood. **Confirmed:** Qos as Edom’s national god and the Assyrian kings Qaushmalaku and Qaushgabri; Milkom and the ninth-century Amman Citadel inscription; the Kassite dynasty (1531–1155 BCE) and its gods Shuqamuna, Shumaliya, Ḫarbe and the weather god Buriaš; the Luwian gods Tarhunt, Arma and Kubaba of Carchemish; Bindus Neptunus and Medaurus, and the sun and serpent signs of Illyrian art; Ammianus on the Alans’ sword (XXXI.2.23); Marcella Pattyn, the last traditional Beguine (died 14 April 2013); the four Franciscans burned at Marseille on 7 May 1318; Pelagius and Caelestius condemned at Carthage (418) and Ephesus (431); Priscillian put to death at Trier in 385; the Diggers’ start on 1 April 1649; Philip Noakes as the last known Muggletonian (died 1979, though “last” is not certain); Saint Maron, John Maron about 685, and closer ties with Rome from the Crusades; Cyril VI Tanas and 1724; the Nubian conversions (543, 569 or 570, 580) and the baqt of about 652; Hensley and the 1908–1914 window for serpent handling; Shah Ni‘matullah Wali (1330–1431) and the order’s Deccan branch; Hamdun al-Qassar (died 884) and the Malamatis of Nishapur; Ayyavazhi’s 1833; Muktananda (1908–1982); Kongtrül and Khyentse as the Rimé leaders; Lin Zhao’en (1517–98); Mesmer in 1775 and 1778; the Illuminati’s founding on 1 May 1776 and the Bavarian edicts of 1784 to 1787; Guénon’s first book (1921); the Agnicayana filmed in Kerala in 1975; Kamma’s “History of the Movements 1855–1967” for Koreri; and 1027 as the traditional date of the Kālacakra’s arrival in Tibet (the tantra’s own date is not settled). **Corrected** (in `traditions.json`, `interactions.json` and `timeline_events.json`): Guénon was initiated into Sufism in 1912 and settled in Cairo in 1930 (the row said he became a Sufi in Cairo); the Council of Braga that condemned Priscillianism is dated 561 in recent books and 563 in older ones, and the second council, of 572, is not one of its condemnations (the row named both); the Beguines’ end is the death of the last *traditional* Beguine; Siddha Yoga’s successor was named in 1982 and has led it alone since 1985; the Toltec row now says that the chronicles disagree (the Anales de Cuauhtitlan put Topiltzin Quetzalcoatl at 923 to 947); the Magyar row says that Isten is the Library’s 1917 name for the high god and that whether it was his old name is debated; the Lycian row says their language is related to Luwian, not that it is Luwian; the link Christianity → Pictish religion now says that the Library’s article of 1917 has Columba baptise King Brude but that Adomnán does not record it and many historians doubt it; the link Thracian → Orphism now says that Greek tradition made Orpheus a Thracian. The note above on the Free Spirit, that “R. Lerner and others doubt it”, was wrong about Lerner (his study treats it as a spread of belief, not a tightly organised sect) and has been fixed. **Still open, not settled by this reading:** the Edomite start window, and the Illyrian, Iberian, Celtiberian and Gaulish rows, for which only general reference pages turned up; Baba Kinaram’s dates (1601–1771 in the hagiography, 1684–1787 in another account); whether Shambhala Training’s first weekends were in 1976 or 1977; the start of the Qalandars (about 1200 was not confirmed); the ends of the Khlysty and the Skoptsy; and the older Atlas row for the Tuka movement, which says 1873 where reference pages say 1885 (not one of the added rows, and not changed). Several of these checks rest on Wikipedia or its mirrors alone, so none of this replaces a specialist’s reading.


## High-control groups and contested movements: the first set of 13 (17 November 2026)

**What was added.** Traditions → **High-control groups** (`#/traditions/movements`; `src/js/2z-data-movements.js`, `6zy-movements.js`, `tests/movements.test.js`): 13 movements, 156 claims, 80 sources. Each claim has a grade (*court or official record*, *independent reporting*, *former members’ accounts*, *alleged*, *the group’s own statement*) and numbered sources; each card has nine labelled parts and a care note; the page opens with what the term means, why “cult” is not used as a verdict, and three cited yardsticks (Lifton 1961, the ICSA checklist, Lalich 2004) with a caution that they are lenses and not scores. The 13: Scientology, Peoples Temple, Heaven’s Gate, Aum Shinrikyo, the Branch Davidians, the Order of the Solar Temple, NXIVM, the Family International (Children of God), the Rajneesh movement, the Unification Church, Raëlism, the Twelve Tribes and the FLDS. The Unification Church and Raëlism are graded *Contested*. Seven more are listed on the page as “Not yet written up”, with the reason (Shincheonji, the Church of Almighty God, Falun Gong, Jehovah’s Witnesses, ISKCON, the Church Universal and Triumphant, Synanon).

**What was checked, and how.** *By tests only* (`tests/movements.test.js`, 60 checks): every entry has the fields of the record, a “how sure we are” and a care note; every claim has a grade and at least one source that exists; a *record* claim rests on a court record or official report; a *reported* claim on at least two different independent sources; a *former member’s account* and an *alleged* claim say whose they are; every legal outcome has a date and a place; every claim that names a living person (Raniere, Mack, Bronfman, Salzman, Jeffs, Sheela, Vorilhon, Hak Ja Han, Spriggs, Armstrong, Layton) is a court or official record, two independent sources, the group’s own words, or alleged with who says it; no word of accusation (cult, brainwashing, mind control, fake, scam, evil, satanic) outside quotation marks anywhere in the data or the page; nothing long enough to be a pasted scripture, and no quotation field; no text is marked readable unless it is a Library text that exists, in the public domain and dated before 1931 (only the FLDS card has one: the 1830 Book of Mormon and the 1902 Pearl of Great Price); every source address is https and either a direct link to a site of the group, an archive or a DOI, or a search at a library, a court database or an encyclopedia; the Atlas ids named exist; the page renders all 13 cards with the nine parts in order, every source number real, every outside link new-tab and `noopener noreferrer`, filters, search and hostile input. The page was looked at in the in-app browser at 1280 and 375 pixels wide (no sideways overflow; the tab strip scrolls; the filters, search and an opened card work).

**What was NOT checked, and the maker should know.** *No claim, date or number was checked against its source in this pass.* Nothing was fetched from the web (the maker’s rule is to state file, source and size first and get a yes, and no yes came), so every entry rests on general knowledge, with each source named by author, title, publisher and year so that it can be looked up. Every card says **Under review** until its sources are re-read, and no entry is marked *checked*. *No link was opened.* A search link finds the item by construction but may not put it first; the 12 direct links (the group’s own sites, `jonestown.sdsu.edu`, `irp.fas.org/congress/1995_rpt/aum/`, `icsahome.com`, and the DOIs for Mayer 1999 and Török 1997) are from memory and may be wrong. No source was read to see that it says what the claim says; for a *reported* claim the two sources named are the two that are known to cover it. The first things to check, by card, are the ones the writer was least sure of:

| Card | Check first |
|---|---|
| Scientology | the French appeal years (2012 and the later Court of Cassation); the number convicted in Operation Snow White (the card says “several”); the Lisa McPherson medical examiner’s finding (a blood clot in the lung after dehydration and immobility) and the 17 days; the 1982 repeal in Victoria and the 1980 end of the British bar; what Judge Breckenridge’s 1984 decision said; the census claim (a few thousand at most in England and Wales, Australia and Canada) |
| Peoples Temple | Larry Layton’s 1986 conviction, life sentence and the 1988 appeal; “about a third” of the dead at Jonestown were children; the count 918 = 909 + 5 + 4; the 1977 move to Guyana |
| Heaven’s Gate | 21 women and 18 men aged 26 to 72; that the medical examiner reported eight men castrated; the mid-1970s recruitment story |
| Aum Shinrikyo | 13 death sentences; the executions on 6 and 26 July 2018 (7 and 6); eight dead at Matsumoto and 13 in Tokyo; Aleph’s 2000 acknowledgement and compensation; Hikari no Wa’s 2007 founding “by a former Aum spokesman”; the 1995 dissolution and 1996 bankruptcy; the 1999 law |
| Branch Davidians | 76 dead on 19 April, nine survivors, “more than eighty” Davidians in all; the 1994 verdicts (“most” convicted of lesser charges) and the Fifth Circuit’s 1996 decision; the Danforth and Treasury findings; the “under seventeen” allegation |
| Solar Temple | every number (48 + 5 + 16 + 5 = 74), the dates, the Quebec couple and their infant son, the staged apparitions, the founders’ birth years; the court outcomes were not looked at at all (the card says so) |
| NXIVM | the seven counts; the pleas and sentences of Salzman, Mack and Bronfman; “about 2015” for DOS; the brand with the initials; that the programmes have stopped; the appeal (the card says it was not checked) |
| Family International | the 1995 Ward judgment (the year, the judge, exactly what was found); “flirty fishing” 1974–1987; the end of the communal Homes about 2010; “Teens for Christ” in 1968 |
| Rajneesh movement | 751 ill and 45 hospitalised; Sheela’s pleas and “less than three years of 20”; Rajneesh’s two counts, suspended sentence and fine; Bombay 1970, Pune 1974, Oregon 1981, the city in 1982; “thousands” of homeless people brought in |
| Unification Church | Moon’s 1982 conviction, 13 months served 1984–85 and the 1983–84 appeals; the 1978 Fraser report; what Barker found; the Tokyo order of 25 March 2025 and its reasoning; the Abe assassination facts |
| Raëlism | Vorilhon’s birth year and the date and place of the meeting; Clonaid in December 2002; the movement’s own count |
| Twelve Tribes | Chattanooga 1972; the 1984 raid (112 children, an order the next day); the Bavarian removal of about 40 children in 2013 and that they were returned (the card says the legal findings were not checked) |
| FLDS | the Utah verdict, sentence and reversal; the Texas 2011 verdict and sentence; the 2008 raid (439 children, the hoax call, the Supreme Court of Texas on 29 May 2008); the 1930s and 1953 history; the list of presidents |

**Update, 22 November 2026.** A first reading of these claims against sources has been made: see “High-control groups: first reading against sources (22 November 2026)” at the end of this file, with a record for each of the 156 claims. The paragraph above and the table are kept as written on 17 November.

**Judgement calls for the maker.** (1) **Whether the entries should ship as they are or wait for the check.** The page is reachable and carries an *Under review* banner on the page and on every card; nothing here has been released. (2) **Plain statements of hard facts**: that eight Heaven’s Gate men were castrated, that children died at Mount Carmel and Jonestown, the 1995 Ward finding, the sex-trafficking verdicts. They are in because the maker asked for harm to be stated plainly, each rests on a court or official source, and none is dwelt on. (3) **Raëlism and the Unification Church** are included as *Contested*, the first with no documented harm to members and the second with a founder convicted of tax offences and a Japanese order to dissolve its branch, because the maker’s phrase was “clearly cults”, and these two are the ones the press most often lumps in. If they should not be on a page that begins with Scientology, say so. (4) **Claims about living people**: Raniere, Jeffs, Sheela, Mack, Bronfman, Salzman, Layton and Armstrong are named in connection with court records; nothing is said of them that the record does not say. (5) **The seven “Not yet written up”** (above) should be chosen from, or added to, by the maker; the reasons for each are on the page. (6) **“Fair Game” and “disconnection”**: the Scientology card gives the Church’s account beside the former members’ (graded *former members’ accounts*, “said by”), and the Church’s denial in its own part; the wording is the most likely to need a lawyer’s eye. (7) **The word “cult”** is used on the page only in quotation marks, in the section that explains why it is not used, and in the tab’s search words (so that someone who types it finds the page).

**Not done.** The four movements the Atlas has no row for (Peoples Temple, the Solar Temple, NXIVM, the Twelve Tribes) point to the nearest row or say none fits; adding rows is the Atlas session’s work. The Atlas tradition pages do not link back to the cards (to keep out of other sessions’ files). A screen reader and a real phone were not tried. The *Under review* flag will need to be turned to *checked* entry by entry as the sources are read, and the “Search for it” links turned into direct links where a stable page is found.


## High-control groups page: the box of questions and the outside bodies (18 November 2026)

**What was added.** Above the explanation on Traditions → High-control groups: ten questions to ask any group before joining or giving it time or money, a list of where they come from, and “If you are worried about someone” with three plain points and six outside bodies, each tagged with its point of view (`MOV_ASK` and `MOV_HELP` in `src/js/2z-data-movements.js`, `movAskHTML` in `6zy-movements.js`, 16 new checks in `tests/movements.test.js`).

**What was checked, and how.** *By tests only:* there are exactly ten questions, each a question with a line saying what to look for; none names a god, a faith or a religion and none calls a group a cult, so each fits a gym or a company as well as a congregation; no run of six words of a question is a run of six words of the yardsticks the page already quotes (Lifton, the ICSA list, Lalich), and none of Lifton’s or Hassan’s own names for their categories appears; no word of accusation outside quotation marks; each body has a point of view in a few words and either its own https address (host from a fixed list, no key, no login, no search words) or no address and a line saying to search for its name; every outside link opens in a new tab with `rel="noopener noreferrer"`; the box is on the page once, above the cards, whatever the filters say.

**What was NOT checked, and the maker should know.** *Nothing was fetched and no page was opened.* Every description, year and address below was written from general knowledge, and the page itself says the addresses were not re-checked on the day:

| Item | Check first |
|---|---|
| INFORM (`inform.ac`) | that the address is its own site; “founded in 1988 at the London School of Economics by the sociologist Eileen Barker”; its stated aim (“accurate and balanced, neither for nor against”) in its own words; that it is still working |
| CESNUR (`cesnur.org`) | the address; “founded in 1988 in Turin”; “publishes articles and holds conferences”; the line that its scholars generally caution against judging a group by its label and that critics, among them support organisations, say this can understate harm (a *disputed* statement: check it against what each side says in print) |
| ICSA (`icsahome.com`) | “a United States body for former members, families and professionals”; “publishes guidance and research”; that its checklist is the one the page paraphrases (the address was already used as a source on the page) |
| Freedom of Mind Resource Center (`freedomofmind.com`) | the address; that Steven Hassan is a counsellor and a former member of the Unification Church; that the BITE model first appeared in his 1988 book; the line that some scholars dispute the term “mind control” and parts of his model (*disputed*) |
| Recovering from Religion (`recoveringfromreligion.org`) | the address; “a United States non-profit for people who are questioning or leaving a religion of any kind” |
| MIVILUDES (no link) | the name and what it is (the French government’s inter-ministerial mission on “dérives sectaires”); the line that the state’s use of that label is disputed by some scholars and religious groups (*disputed*) |
| The two new sources | Singer with Lalich, *Cults in Our Midst* (Jossey-Bass, 1995) as the place of her six conditions of thought reform; Hassan, *Combating Cult Mind Control* (Park Street Press, 1988) as the place of the BITE model |
| The advice | the three points under “If you are worried about someone” are stated as what support organisations and researchers *commonly advise*; they were not matched against any one organisation’s published guidance |

**Judgement calls for the maker.** (1) Whether a living person’s own centre (Hassan’s) belongs among the bodies, or only his book among the sources. (2) Whether CESNUR belongs, given that some support organisations criticise it; it is there so that the scholarly view of new religions has a named place beside the support view, and the line says so. (3) Whether a government body from one country (MIVILUDES) helps a reader elsewhere; it is the one entry with no link. (4) The point-of-view labels are ours: “Support and research for people affected”, “Academic information service”, “Scholarly centre on new religions”, “Former member and counsellor”, “Peer support for people leaving a religion”, “Government body”.


## Rounds 21 to 23 (17 November 2026): what the new notes rest on, and what was not checked

**What was read and checked.** For each of the 56 texts the title page, the edition statement and the ends of the scan were read; every note's edition, publisher and year come from the scan's own title page (or, for the sacred-texts keyboarding of Duncan, its notice of attribution, which is kept in the note and in `LICENSES.txt`). Four sources were found unusable and dropped for the reasons in `LEFT_OUT_SORTED.md`: the *Equinox* and Crowley's *Book of the Law* (O.T.O.'s typeset file, which claims copyright and may be copied only unmodified, and a 1938 edition with Crowley's later commentary), Finney's *Freemasonry* (a 1999 e-text that forbids any change), and the first two Archive items for Kautilya (a fifth and a seventh Mysore edition of 1956 and 1961 with "All rights reserved"); the Arthashastra is built from a later Mysore reprint of the third edition of 1929, whose text is of 1929 (public domain in the United States) and whose title page says “the late Dr. R. Shamasastry” (so it was printed after the translator’s death) and whose © line is the publisher’s; the text is that of 1929, but nobody has compared the two printings page by page. Crowley's *Magick* (Wellcome scan) answers 403 and was not fetched. Round 22 and 23 notes say plainly that each book is an outsider's record or an exposure, that some of what it describes is held sacred or private, and that the Library neither endorses nor judges.

**Written from general knowledge, not checked against a source in this pass** (each can be checked in the book's own introduction or the standard histories): that Morgan was seized in September 1826 while the book was being printed and that the Anti-Masonic movement followed (the first American third party); that Prichard's 1730 exposure prompted the Grand Lodge's changes; that *Jachin and Boaz* (1762) reports the Moderns' working and the *Three Distinct Knocks* (1760) the Ancients'; that Allyn's *Ritual* was first printed at Philadelphia in 1831; that the *Sixth and Seventh Books of Moses* is a German compilation of the 1840s first printed by Scheible (1849) and has been used in hoodoo and Pennsylvania German powwow; that Albertus Magnus did not write the *Egyptian Secrets*; that Dieterich published the Paris magical papyrus as a Mithras liturgy in 1903 and that most scholars now doubt that it is Mithraic; that the Liberal Catholic Church was founded in 1916; Heindel's claim of an Elder Brother in 1907; the Haitian "Vaudoux cannibalism" stories not being supported by other evidence; that Le Plongeon's Maya chronology is rejected by archaeologists; that Jung rewrote the book as *Symbols of Transformation* in 1952; Kautilya's attribution. The Nusayri note rests on Salisbury's own introduction (Sulaiman of Adana, the book of 1863).

**Found by checking, and fixed.** The Prichard note first said that he swore before the Lord Mayor; the scan says an alderman. The Morgan note first said that he disappeared before the book was finished. The Mary Ettie Smith note first said she joined the church as a girl; the scan says she was baptized as a child and brought up in the faith. The Kautilya note first said "a handbook for the king" and mentioned "the fortunes of festivals", neither of which the book contains. All four were corrected in the specs and the built files. An id clash was also found and mended: the Benham dictionary had been built under the Dresden Codex's id and is now `x-bnh`.

**Not done.** No translation was read against its printed edition; no Masonic exposure was compared with any lodge's working (none is claimed to be accurate); the Mormon accounts were not compared with the Church's own account of the endowment; the 1930 books newly in the public domain (Remy's *Demonolatry*, Rattray's *Akan-Ashanti Folk-Tales*, the Summers and Rattray items) have no open scan yet.


## World history timeline pilot, second reading, 18 November 2026

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (a child session of the project manager, not the session that wrote the pilot) went through all 86 events of `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json`, the seven strands and the notes the page prints (`world_strands.json`, `world_meta.json`): dates, places, every statement in each summary, status, date confidence, and whether each named source exists as described and supports the claim. *Method.* (1) The 114 Wikipedia addresses (113 pages) were checked through the MediaWiki interface: all exist, none is a disambiguation page, three were redirects (fixed, below). (2) The text of the cited articles was read for the claims each entry makes, for all 86 events (for the First Crusade, the Greco-Persian Wars and the Peloponnesian War, Britannica's articles were read instead). (3) A second source outside Wikipedia was then read, page by page, for **all 8 conflicts** (Britannica on Kadesh, the Persian Wars, the Peloponnesian War, the Punic Wars, the First Crusade, Cortés and the Taiping Rebellion; Thucydides I.23 in the MIT Internet Classics Archive; Wilson's own summary of his book), for **6 of the 9 disputed events** (Thera, Bantu, Carthage, Homer, paper, the epidemics; the Late Bronze Age collapse, the Maya collapse and the Library of Alexandria were checked against search results only), and for **11 of the 17 events dated 1000 BCE or earlier** (writing, the Austronesian voyages, the Old Kingdom, the Indus cities, Gilgamesh, Thera, the Shang, Kadesh, iron, the Olmec, the Bantu; Hammurabi, Uluburun and the Late Bronze Age collapse by search results only; Akkad, the first alphabets and the New Kingdom against the cited Wikipedia pages alone). (4) The wording was read against the neutrality rule, and the coordinates were compared with the known positions of the places they name (none was found wrong). Pages behind a bot check (the PNAS site, CORE) were not opened and nothing was downloaded; a fact that came only from a search result, and not from a page opened and read, is marked *(search result)*.

**Counts.** 86 events checked; **33 changed**: 23 corrected (a statement, date or number was wrong, out of date or one-sided), 2 given the basis of their range for the dead, 5 made more exact or more even-handed without having been wrong, 3 with only a source address fixed or a source added. **No status was changed** (74 documented, 9 disputed, 3 reported, before and after); one date confidence changed (the Renaissance, M to L); where a claim could not be supported the sentence was narrowed or removed, not kept. New sources: four scholarly works (Dreyer and others 1998; Pearson and others 2018; Docter and others 2008; Bagnall 2002) and one more Wikipedia page (History of Carthage). The page still rests, for 68 of the 86 events, on a Wikipedia page alone (grade C): the checks above are only as good as that summary plus the second sources named here, so replacing those pages by scholarly works is the next step.

### Corrected (23)

| Event id | What was wrong | What it says now | What settled it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `first-alphabets` | The Phoenician alphabet was "in use by about 1050 BCE"; the Sinai inscriptions were "about 1800 to 1500 BCE". | 1050 BCE is a conventional start (the oldest Phoenician inscriptions are of the 10th century; the Ahiram sarcophagus is about 1000 BCE); scholars date the Sinai inscriptions from about 1850 to about 1550 BCE. Start moved to c. 1850 BCE. | Wikipedia, *Phoenician alphabet* and *Proto-Sinaitic script* (the entry's own pages, read as text). |
| `bantu-expansion` | Began at 1000 BCE. | Began about 5,000 years ago by one recent estimate; the summary says the start, not only the routes, is argued about. Start moved to c. 3000 BCE (file kept in date order). | Grollemund and others, PNAS 112 (2015), open copy PMC4629331: the expansion "beginning ∼5,000 y ago"; Wikipedia, *Bantu expansion* ("about 6,000 and 1,500 years ago"). |
| `carthage-founded` | Gave only the pottery evidence (about 760 BCE) and called the traditional date "uncertain". | Gives Timaeus's 814 BCE, the pottery date, and the radiocarbon dates on animal bones from the lowest levels (late 9th to 8th century BCE), and says scholars differ on how to reconcile them. | Livius, "Punic Carthage" (oldest finds now dated to the last quarter of the 9th century by radiocarbon); Docter and others 2008 (record read at the University of Groningen research portal); Wikipedia, *History of Carthage* (Timaeus, 38 years before the first Olympiad). The pottery figure is kept from the original entry: see *Left uncertain*. |
| `library-of-alexandria` | The range ended in 48 BCE (Caesar's fire) although the entry says the decline is disputed; "no contemporary records a single destruction" is not on the cited page. | Range ends c. 270 CE; the summary names the three endings that have been proposed (the harbour fire of 48 BCE, the fighting of the 270s CE, the destruction of the Serapeum in 391 CE) and says the accounts of a burning were written decades or centuries later. | Wikipedia, *Library of Alexandria* (burned in 48 BCE but "unclear how much was actually destroyed"; membership ceased by the 260s; 270–275 "probably destroyed whatever remained"; the accounts of the burning that it cites, Livy through Seneca and Plutarch, were written decades to a century or more later); Bagnall, "Alexandria: Library of Dreams", *Proceedings of the American Philosophical Society* 146 (2002) *(search result)*. |
| `persian-empire` | The Cyrus Cylinder was "Cyrus's own account" and "names no one people". | It is written in Cyrus's name; it names only Mesopotamian sanctuaries and does not mention the Judeans. | Wikipedia, *Cyrus Cylinder* ("in the name of the Persian king"; "identifies only Mesopotamian sanctuaries, and makes no mention of Jews, Jerusalem, or Judea"). The British Museum page was not opened. |
| `thera-eruption` | "Radiocarbon and tree-ring samples point to about 1620 BCE ... pottery 1530 to 1500": out of date, and a tree-ring date for the eruption is only a hypothesis. | Radiocarbon: late 17th or early 16th century BCE (an olive branch buried by the eruption gave 1627 to 1600 BCE in 2006, Friedrich and others, *Science*, *(search result)*; its wiggle-matching has since been questioned, Wikipedia); archaeology: mid 16th to early 15th century; a 2018 recalibration moved the radiocarbon range later; unresolved. | Pearson and others, *Science Advances* 4 (2018), PMC6093623 (abstract: radiocarbon "late 17th–early 16th century BCE", archaeological "mid 16th–early 15th", the offset shifts Thera "toward the 16th century BCE"); Wikipedia, *Minoan eruption* (archaeologists do not accept the 1627 BCE frost ring as linked). |

The manuscript's leaves were radiocarbon dated in 2017 to 224–383, 680–779 and 885–993 CE, and in October 2024 (Oxford) to 799–1102 CE, so it cannot be called earlier; its age is disputed. | Wikipedia, *Bakhshali manuscript*. |

### Smaller changes (10)

* **The range for the dead (2).** Both ranges were in the data with no stated basis. `thirty-years-war`: 4.5 to 8 million is the range reference works give (Wikipedia: "Estimates range from 4.5 to 8 million dead"; claims of up to 12 million "no longer considered realistic"); Wilson, the author of the named book, puts the dead of the Empire at about 1.8 million soldiers and at least 3.2 million civilians (his own summary, Rorotoko, 23 April 2010, read). `taiping-rebellion`: 20 to 30 million is "the most widely cited" range, with no reliable census and some estimates of 73 to 100 million (Wikipedia); Britannica says "an estimated 20 million" (read). Both notes now say whose range it is and that it is an estimate. The six wars with no range keep their plain statement that none is given, and why.
* `battle-of-kadesh`: now says that some chronologies put the battle about twenty years earlier (Wikipedia: May 1274 BCE on the standard chronology).
* `austronesian-voyages`: the title names the Indian Ocean and the summary did not; Madagascar added (Wikipedia, *Austronesian peoples*).
* `renaissance`: confidence M to L and "when it began" added to what is debated (Wikipedia: "variously defined as covering the 15th and 16th centuries or ... from the 14th century to the 17th"), as for the Enlightenment, whose dates (L) vary by author.
* `classic-maya-collapse`: "probably in combination" took a side; it is now "often in combination" (the page: "no universally accepted collapse theory").
* `diamond-sutra-printed`: the link note "six centuries before" Gutenberg is "nearly six centuries" (868 to about 1450).
* `writing-appears`: the cited page says "clay labels" at Abydos; the entry says bone and ivory, which is right: *Archaeology* (March/April 1999, "Earliest Egyptian Glyphs") reports "bone and ivory tags, pottery vessels, and clay seal impressions" at Abydos dated to between 3400 and 3200 BCE. Dreyer and others, *Umm el-Qaab I* (1998), the excavation report, added as a second source (bibliographic details confirmed, search result).
* `athenian-democracy` and `aztec-triple-alliance` (with `old-kingdom-egypt` above): the three redirects are now the pages themselves (*Cleisthenes*, *Aztec Empire*, *Old Kingdom of Egypt*).

**For the maker (neutrality, left as a choice).** (1) `black-death` gave Caffa (Feodosia) the country code UA; Jerusalem is given no country because its status is disputed, and the status of Crimea is disputed too, so the code was dropped for the same reason (the name already says Crimea). Say so if it should go back. (2) `taiping-rebellion`: "Rebellion" is the usual English name but some historians (Meyer-Fong and Platt, 2013, in the Wikipedia article) call it biased; the label was kept and "Some historians prefer the name Taiping Civil War" added. (3) `world_meta.json` still says `review: "unchecked"` (a test pins it); the note the page prints now says that a second reading has been made, that it was not a specialist's, and that no date has been checked against Wikidata.

### The three groups the maker named

* **The 8 conflicts.** Dates, parties, causes (religious, political, economic, mixed) and their grades were read against the cited pages and a second source each. Confirmed: Kadesh (Britannica: 1275 BCE, Ramesses II and Muwatalli, "the world's first recorded peace treaty"); the Greco-Persian Wars (Britannica counts 492–449 BCE from the first Persian expedition and treats the Ionian revolt of 499–493 as part of the series: the entry's 499 is the other usual start, and its summary begins with that revolt); the Peloponnesian War (Britannica 431–404 BCE; Thucydides I.23, in the MIT archive's translation: "The growth of the power of Athens, and the alarm which this inspired in Lacedaemon, made war inevitable", with the formal grounds of complaint given separately); the Punic Wars (Britannica 264–146 BCE; rivalry over Sicily); the First Crusade (Britannica: Alexius Comnenus sought aid from the West, his envoys came to the Council of Piacenza in 1095, Clermont on 18 November 1095; Seljuq pressure and the Fatimids); the Spanish conquest (Britannica: Cortés sailed on 18 February 1519 with 508 soldiers, about 100 sailors and 16 horses, and Tlaxcala, in chronic war with Montezuma, became his ally). Corrected: the Spanish conquest (the civil-war claim) and the two ranges above. Kitchen, *Pharaoh Triumphant* (Aris & Phillips, Warminster, 1982), Asbridge, *The First Crusade: A New History* (Oxford University Press, 2004) and Wilson, *Europe's Tragedy* (2009; the US edition, Belknap Press, ISBN 978-0674036345, is titled *The Thirty Years War: Europe's Tragedy*, as the Rorotoko page lists it) exist as described *(search results for the first two; the books were not opened)*. The Thirty Years' War is the only conflict to use *mixed*, for the argument over whether religion or politics was the main cause; the Taiping causes are graded *documented* for the religious, political and economic strands, which the cited background section supports (famine, the Chinese economy after the First Opium War, anti-Manchu feeling among the Hakka, Hong's visions) but which historians weigh differently: a judgement, left. The six conflicts with no range say plainly "No range is given" and why.
* **The 9 disputed events.** Read for whether each says where the sources differ and takes no side. Fixed: Thera (out of date), Bantu (start), Carthage (one side only), Library of Alexandria (an end date that took a side), Epidemics (start and causes), Maya collapse (wording). Fair as written: the Late Bronze Age collapse (search result: "no single cause", the start dated from 1250 to 1177 BCE by different scholars, Hattusa abandoned about 1200–1180), the Homeric poems (Britannica: Homer "flourished 9th or 8th century BCE?"; the earliest quotations date from the middle of the 7th century) and paper (Britannica: the oldest fragment, from Fangmatan, is of the 2nd century BCE; the first record of a formal process is of about 105 CE). Britannica on the Columbian exchange: populations fell "by 50 percent to 95 percent by 1650", in the Caribbean "more than 99 percent by 1600".
* **The 17 events dated 1000 BCE or earlier.** Writing, Austronesian voyages, the Old Kingdom, the Indus cities, Akkad, the first alphabets, Gilgamesh, Hammurabi, Thera, the Shang, the New Kingdom, Uluburun, Kadesh, iron, the Late Bronze Age collapse, the Olmec, the Bantu. Changed: first alphabets, Thera, Bantu, Kadesh (note), Austronesian (Madagascar), Old Kingdom, Akkad, Gilgamesh, iron, writing (source). Confirmed beside Wikipedia: the Indus cities (Britannica: "nuclear dates ... about 2600–1900 BCE"), the Shang (Britannica: founding dated from about 1760 to 1520 BCE and the fall from 1122 to 1030 by older reckonings; recent archaeological work gives about 1600 and 1046, the entry's figures), Gilgamesh (Britannica: the fullest text is on 12 tablets from Ashurbanipal's library at Nineveh), the Olmec (Britannica: about 1200–400 BCE; art style first at San Lorenzo about 1200), iron (Britannica: the full Iron Age begins about 1200 BCE in the Middle East and south-east Europe, with knowledge "rapid and widespread" between 1200 and 1000), the Austronesian languages (Britannica: from Madagascar to Easter Island), Hammurabi (1792–1750 BCE on the middle chronology; the stele of 282 laws found at Susa in 1901–02, Louvre *(search result)*) and Uluburun (about 1320 ± 15 BCE, Institute of Nautical Archaeology *(search result)*; found in 1982). **Reference works differ on some conventions**, and the entry's choice was kept, each marked "c." with the confidence its convention deserves: the Old Kingdom 2686–2181 BCE (3rd to 6th dynasties; Britannica gives about 2575–2130 BCE, the 4th to 8th); Kadesh 1274 (Britannica 1275); the Greco-Persian Wars 499 (Britannica 492).

### Left uncertain

* `carthage-founded`: the "about 760 BCE" pottery date comes from the original entry and from a search-result summary of an abstract that could not be opened; the radiocarbon side rests on Livius and on a bibliography record, not on the 2008 chapter itself.
* `shang-dynasty`: the beginning (the cited page: "between the 16th and 11th centuries BC"); `first-alphabets`, `iron-working-spreads` and the Indus and Old Kingdom dates are conventional ranges, as their confidence says.
* `first-crusade`: "the Byzantine Empire as a wary neighbour" is a description, not a record; kept. `thirty-years-war`: the low end of the range (4.5 million) is below Wilson's own total (at least 5 million); kept because it is the range reference works give.
* `mali-empire` ends "c. 1600" (the cited page says 1610); `tang-dynasty` does not mention the break of 690–705 that the page's first line gives; `meiji-restoration` gives only Tokyo (Edo) as its place, although the declaration of 3 January 1868 was made at the court in Kyoto (not checked against a page; Kyoto could be added); `great-zimbabwe` starts c. 1100 (the page: settled from about 1000, major building from the 11th century); `late-bronze-age-collapse` links to Minoan religion because the Atlas has no Mycenaean row (its Minoan row's end window runs to 1100 BCE).
* `atlantic-slave-trade`: 1867 (Cuba) against the database's own 1866.
* Grades: the Taiping's political and economic causes are *documented* (above); `austronesian-voyages` is *documented* although the routes are argued (excavated sites and measured dates fix the outline).

### Not checked

No date was checked against Wikidata (not fetched; waiting for the maker's go-ahead). The pages of the scholarly and primary works (Kitchen, Asbridge, Wilson's book, Herodotus, Sima Qian, Pliny, the Xi'an stele, the Edicts of Ashoka) were not opened (only Thucydides I.23 was); for the books only their existence and the facts named above were confirmed. The PNAS and CORE pages were behind a bot check and were left alone. The page itself was not looked at in the browser (the data changed; the markup of every event is tested).

### Coverage: what is thin or missing (nothing was added)

By region (assigned by hand from each event's first place), the 86 events are: Europe and the Mediterranean world 34, Near East 13, East Asia 12, the Americas 8, South Asia 6, North Africa and Egypt 5, Sub-Saharan Africa 5, Southeast Asia and the Pacific 3. After 1500 CE there are 17 events, ten of them European (the rest: the Americas 2, East Asia 2, Sub-Saharan Africa 1, South Asia 1, Southeast Asia 1). Oceania is the Austronesian voyages alone; nothing for Australia or the Pacific after 1300. The pilot does lean toward Europe, as its own note says; topics to research (not events to copy in, and not checked):

* **3500–1000 BCE:** Nubia and Kush, Norte Chico (Caral) in the Andes, the Minoan and Mycenaean worlds as events (Thera is only a disaster), the Hittite empire, the beginnings of bronze and the wheel, Stonehenge and the megalithic monuments, early China before the Shang. (The window starts at 3500 BCE by design, so the beginnings of farming and cities are outside it.)
* **1000 BCE–500 CE:** the Zhou and the philosophers of the same centuries, Alexander and the Hellenistic kingdoms, the Parthians and the Sasanians, Kush and Meroë, Nok, Teotihuacan, the Kushan empire, Rome after Augustus (Constantine, the crisis of the 3rd century).
* **500–1000 CE:** the early Islamic conquests and the Umayyads, Charlemagne, Srivijaya, the Tibetan empire, the An Lushan rebellion, Tiwanaku and Wari, Ghana and the trans-Saharan trade, the Swahili coast.
* **1000–1500 CE:** the Song dynasty (movable type about 1040, paper money, gunpowder), the Delhi Sultanate, the rise of the Ottomans before 1453, the Hundred Years' War and the Reconquista, Timur, Vijayanagara, Kilwa and Songhai, Cahokia and North America, the Korean alphabet (1443), the settlement of Hawai'i, Rapa Nui and New Zealand beyond the one line in the Austronesian entry.
* **1500–1900 CE:** the Ottomans at their height and Safavid Iran, the Qing and Tokugawa Japan, Russian expansion, the Opium Wars, British rule in India and the rising of 1857, the American, Haitian and Latin American revolutions, the kingdoms of West and Central Africa and the Scramble for Africa, Australia and the Pacific after contact, the end of slavery.
* **By strand:** *Disease and disaster* has twelve events and none from China, India or Africa (for example the Shaanxi earthquake of 1556, the famines of 1876–78, the volcanic winter of 536). *Conflict* has eight, three of them Greek or Roman, and none from Africa south of Egypt, South Asia, Southeast Asia or Oceania; no war within the Islamic world, and no war of the Mongol conquests as an entry of its own. *Science and technology* has nothing from the Islamic world after the translations, nor from Africa or the Americas (the domestication of maize, Maya astronomy). *Ideas and arts* has no epic from India or Persia and nothing from Africa south of the Sahara except Great Zimbabwe, which is a building.

### Files, and the built file

Changed in the shared project: `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json` (33 events; one event moved, to keep the file in date order), `tools/atlas_src/json/world_meta.json` (the review note), and this file. **`atlas/world.js` must be rebuilt** (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`, the project manager's step): until then `tests/history.test.js` fails one check in the shared project, the one that compares the built file with the JSON. In a private clone with the file rebuilt: `history` 74 passed, 0 failed; `atlas` 103, `chain` 52, `causes` 28, `tree` 40, `atlas-texts` 37, `sections` 43, `stories` 43, `common` 17, `data-integrity` 41, all passed. The edits were applied by a script that asserts the text it replaces (kept with the session's notes), so nothing else in the data moved.

## The neutral voice: revelations, visions and titles stated as fact, and the teasers that argued a thesis (18 November 2026)

Written by the neutral-voice session for UX-22 and UX-23 of `UX_AUDIT.md` and the Movements lede (section 5 of that audit). **Nothing was fetched.** Every change below is a rewording of what the record already said: the dates, places and persons are untouched, and a founder is still a historical person where he or she was one. The few names and phrases that were added come from general knowledge and have **not** been re-read against a source; they are listed under “Added, not checked”. Nothing was removed from the app (explain, don’t remove): a claim a tradition makes is still there, with the tradition named as the one making it.

### The frame

The maker (18 Nov 2026): “stay as neutral as possible. i’m not building this to direct opinions. its only a tool for people to form their own opinions.” The same four rules now apply to every tradition, mainstream ones included:

1. **A revelation, a vision, a séance, a dictation is a claim of the tradition that reports it**, and is written so: “held to have been revealed”, “said to have been revealed”, “reported”, “in the Muslim account”, “by its own account”, “presented by its author as channelled”. The tool never writes it as an event in its own voice.
2. **A title is not a status.** “The Prophet”, “the Mahdi”, “the Buddha”, “the Báb”, “the Shawnee Prophet” become the person’s name, with “known as” or “who proclaimed himself” where the title matters.
3. **What a tradition holds is attributed, not stated:** a category of belief reads “Belief in…” or “Traditions that hold…”, a doctrine reads “holding that the Twelfth Imam is hidden and will return”, a practice reads “seeking an answer from a god”, not “consulting a god”.
4. **Secular facts are not weakened.** Dates, places, founders as historical persons, who built what, what a text says, are as before. Names of what is worshipped (“the sea goddess Lin Moniang”, “the storm god”) and the verbs of worship (“worship of”, “devotion to”, “faith in”) are the frame’s own words and stay: they say what is venerated, not that it is real.

The four dates of the Qur’an entries no longer say “Revealed about 610–632 CE” bare.

### Common ground (`src/js/6o-common.js`, `2s-common-core.js`, `2q-data-parallels.js`)

The page opens with the likenesses and the differences together, in that order of the page: the lead says both, the card “Where they really differ” sits straight after it, and every sentence that argued for the likenesses now says how sure the library is. The numbers (19 traditions, 113 connections, 54% / 29% / 17%) are unchanged: they are computed.

| Where | Old line | New line |
|---|---|---|
| Common ground · heading | The traditions are not walled off from each other | Where the traditions meet, and where they differ |
| Common ground · lead | Follow almost any religion back far enough and you meet another one. Their stories borrowed from, answered and argued with each other for thousands of years; and where they never met, people asked the same questions about suffering, death, how to treat a stranger, and arrived at remarkably similar answers. This page shows how much, using only the library’s own data. | Some traditions borrowed from, answered and argued with each other for thousands of years, and the library records those connections and how well each is documented. Where traditions had no known contact, some asked the same questions about suffering, death and how to treat a stranger and gave similar answers, and some gave different ones. This page shows the likenesses and the differences side by side, using only the library’s own data. |
| Common ground · order | The card “Where they really differ” came fifth, after the ring, the kinds of overlap, the passages and the word map. | It comes second, straight after the lead and its four numbers, before the ring. |
| Common ground · closing paragraph of that card | … It only points out what the rest of this page shows: that the traditions are tangled together in their history, that on how people should treat one another they say strikingly similar things, and that this is plenty of room to sit at one table. | … Below, the library shows how the traditions are connected in their history and where they say similar things, each connection graded by how well it is documented; what to make of it is for the reader. |
| Common ground · card title | Why they overlap | Kinds of overlap |
| Common ground · paragraph under the bars | Two things are true at once. 54% of these connections are shared history: the traditions are literally related, through borrowing, common ancestors and contact. And 29% are independent convergence: similar ideas where no contact is known, which suggests the overlap comes partly from being human and not only from being neighbours. The rest are disputed, and the library says so each time. | By the library’s own grading, 54% of these connections are shared history (one tradition borrowed from, grew out of or met another) and 29% are independent parallels (similar ideas where no contact is known). Why such parallels arise is argued: some read them as a sign of common human concerns, others as coincidence or as contact nobody has recorded. The other 17% are disputed, and the library says so each time. |
| Common ground · section title | Teachings found everywhere | Teachings found in several traditions |
| Common ground · the group “Independent convergence” | Similar ideas where no contact is known: people in different places arriving at the same place on their own. | Similar ideas where no contact is known. The record shows none, so they are graded as independent; that is not proof that none happened. |
| passages: the reciprocity rule · blurb | Do not do to others what you would not have done to you, in six traditions that did not need each other to arrive at it. | Do not do to others what you would not have done to you: six passages, some linked by borrowing or a shared tradition and some from worlds with no known contact. |
| passages: “Many ways, one God” · title | Many ways, one God | Beyond one community |
| passages: peace · blurb | … five traditions that put peace before victory. | … five passages that put peace before victory. |
| passages: the stranger · blurb | Hospitality to the foreigner is a sacred duty in the Torah, in Homer, in the New Testament and in the Qur’an. | Hospitality to the foreigner is urged in the Torah, in Homer, in the New Testament and in the Qur’an. |
| passages: within · blurb | Where is God or the Self? Within you, nearer than the neck-vein, the breath of all breath: three traditions that point inward. | Where is God or the Self? Three passages say it is within you: nearer than the neck-vein, the breath of all breath. |
| passages: answering harm · note on one link | Remarkably close in spirit, with no known contact. Teachers in different times and places arrived at the same hard teaching. | Close in spirit, with no known contact. Teachers in different times and places gave a similar hard teaching. |
| passages: the speck and the beam · note on one link | Remarkably close in image and point, with no known contact between a Galilean teacher and the Pali verses. Moral self-examination is a common human discovery. | Close in image and point, with no known contact between a Galilean teacher and the Pali verses. Whether that shows a common human concern or coincidence is a matter of reading. |

### The Movements lede

“More than any outsider thinks fair” was a view; it is replaced by a statement attributed to those who hold it (former members, researchers, courts, officials) and a statement of record (members died in a few groups, as court records and official inquiries document). The cards, grades and sources of the page are untouched.

| Where | Old line | New line |
|---|---|---|
| High-control groups · the lede (`2z-data-movements.js`, `MOV_TERM.line`) | Some religious and self-improvement groups have asked of their members more than any outsider thinks fair: all their money, all their time, their families, their silence. A few have ended in deaths. This page records them as documented history, … | Some religious and self-improvement groups ask a great deal of their members: money, time, family ties, silence. Former members, researchers, courts and officials have questioned some of these demands, and in a few groups members died, as court records and official inquiries document. This page records them as documented history, … |

### Reviewed and left as they are

* **The nine family sentences of All traditions** (`TRL_GROUP_LINE`, `2z-tradlist-core.js`): read, no change. They list religions and say what the evidence is (“What people believed is inferred from what they left”).
* **Summaries already in the frame:** Christianity (“Faith in Jesus as Messiah and Son of God”), Buddhism (“taught by the Buddha”), Zoroastrianism, Judaism, Sikhism (“Devotion to the one Creator taught by ten Gurus”), Hinduism, Shaivism and Shaktism (“Devotion to Shiva as supreme God”, “Worship of the Goddess … as supreme power”), Rastafari (“honouring Haile Selassie I as divine”), Protestantism (“teaching salvation by grace”), Gnosticism (“Movements teaching salvation through gnosis”) and about 700 more that describe a tradition by its practice, its place and its dates.
* **Words for what is worshipped** (“storm god”, “sea goddess”, “Tawûsî Melek”) and **the word “cult” in its scholarly sense** (“State cult of the Unconquered Sun”, “Cult of Atargatis”) stay, as the Movements page explains; 31 summaries use it.
* **Stories’ retellings** (“God plants a garden … a serpent persuades the first woman”): each is the content of a named text or tradition, introduced as such, and stays.

### Added, not checked

From general knowledge, not re-read against a source: the name *Helen Schucman* as the scribe of A Course in Miracles (she is named in many accounts and the Course’s own preface); *Sayyid Ali Muhammad* as the Báb’s name; *Emanuel Swedenborg* in full and “visions of the spiritual world”; *a Seneca leader* for Handsome Lake and the year 1799, which is the Atlas’s own start year for the Longhouse religion; “holding that the Twelfth Imam is hidden and will return” as the Twelver doctrine. Each is one phrase and easy to change in `traditions.json`.

### Left for the maker (not changed: outside what the brief asked)

* **Six Atlas lines use “massacre” or “genocide” in the tool’s own voice:** `ix0119` “Rhineland massacres of the First Crusade”, `ix0133` “St Bartholomew’s Day massacre” (the event’s usual name), `ix0229` “Genocide by the Islamic State group, a Salafi-jihadist movement”, `pr00609` “Assyrians after the genocide of 1914–1918”, `pr02156` “Genocide by ISIS in 2014”, and the site note “Massacre of Lakota Ghost Dancers” (Wounded Knee). The stance rule says such a word is used only where a cited source says it, and attributed (“called a genocide by …”). Recent conflicts and living groups are where this matters most: a decision on how the Atlas names events is the maker’s.
* **“Cult” in 31 summaries** is used in its scholarly sense, but for living faiths (“Venezuelan cult blending Indigenous, African and Catholic elements”, María Lionza) a reader may hear the everyday sense. Option: “spirit religion”.
* **Longer teaching texts** (the Journey’s lessons and station texts) retell many traditions’ accounts in the present tense. Only the sentences that state a revelation or a title as fact were changed (the table above); a full reading of those 137 stations for the frame is a separate job.

### The guard

`tests/neutral.test.js` (20 checks) scans, and fails with the file and line (or the Atlas record and field): the 9,959 prose strings of the Atlas data, the 21,700 lines of code in `src/js`, the 415 strings of the stories, the Common ground page, its passages and the 19 tradition-page blurbs, the family sentences and the belief labels. Its list of words is short and kept in the test (revealed, revelation, “the Prophet”, a miracle, “true religion”, “God spoke”, channelled, a vision, “from the divine”, a title written as a status, “recited to” a person, a bare “the first recorded” or “the oldest … on earth”, and a few phrases of the thesis voice); a word may stand only where the sentence says whose claim it is, or in a quotation, a source title or a Library record, or in one of four exceptions with their reasons (the test fails if an exception stops matching). It also tests itself: the old wordings of the audit are caught and the attributed ones pass. Run against the files as they were before this session, it fails 9 of its 20 checks, with 31 Atlas strings, 23 lines of code, 23 story strings, 3 belief labels, all 8 drawn story hooks, 9 of the 10 hooks that set one tradition’s tale beside another’s, and the Common ground heading and lead flagged; now it reports none. The loaded-word list is the history timeline’s (`HIST_LOADED`), applied to the 793 summaries (“cult” and “Land without Evil” allowed). No other test scanned these strings, so nothing was extended: this is the first.

### Files

Changed: `tools/atlas_src/json/` `traditions.json`, `texts.json`, `sites.json`, `practices.json`, `interaction_causes.json` (48 values, applied by a script that asserts the old text; the files are byte-identical elsewhere); `src/js/` 17 files (73 lines); `tests/neutral.test.js` (new), `tests/common.test.js` and `tests/path-render.test.js` (3 lines: the old checks pinned “not walled off” and “Teachings found everywhere”). **`atlas/` must be rebuilt by the project manager** (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`) for the Atlas panel and the pages to show the new summaries and notes. In a private clone with the atlas rebuilt: `neutral` 20, `atlas` 103, `atlas-texts` 37, `data-integrity` 41, `tradlist` 67, `belief` 33, `traditions` 22, `movements` 76, `fuzz` 3,049, `common` 17, `stories` 43, `path-render` 280, `history` 74, `causes` 28, `chain` 52, `tree` 40, `path` 606, all with 0 failed; `rename` 23 in the shared project.



## Second reading, part 1: the outside bodies and citations on High-control groups (18 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (a child session of the project manager, not the session that wrote the box) opened each body’s own pages and the library records of the books, read only and one page at a time, through the project’s web-fetch tool and a web search; nothing was downloaded or saved into the project. *Opened:* INFORM (`inform.ac`: home, About us, the “35 years” page, Contact, “Guidelines for Friends & Family”); CESNUR (`cesnur.org`: home, About (in Italian), a 2008 paper by Introvigne that the home page links, and the English Wikipedia article for the founders and the criticism); the ICSA (the retired `icsahome.com`, then `internationalculticstudies.org`: home, History, “Resources for survivors, families and friends”, the checklist page, a handbook for families and an article on reaching out to a loved one; an older copy of the checklist at `articles1.icsahome.com`; the page “Six Conditions”; Wikipedia on the ICSA); Freedom of Mind (`freedomofmind.com`: home, About Steven Hassan, the BITE model page, the books page; Wikipedia on Steven Hassan and on *Combating Cult Mind Control*); Recovering from Religion (home, About, Wikipedia); MIVILUDES (the English and French Wikipedia articles); the six books (Open Library, the Internet Archive’s search interface, the Cult Education Institute’s reprint of Singer’s chapter 3). Where the fetch tool only summarised a page instead of quoting it, the fact rests on that summary and is marked *(summary)*. **Could not be opened:** `state.gov` (the in-app browser was refused and the fetch tool got 403), the Refworld copy of the State Department’s 2009 report on France (a bot check), MIVILUDES’ own site `miviludes.interieur.gouv.fr` (403), the Library of Congress and HathiTrust (refused), Google Books and WorldCat (429; not repeated). The address `derives-sectaires.gouv.fr`, which the reader had guessed for MIVILUDES, does not exist.

**Counts.** The “Check first” table had eight rows. **Six bodies checked** (five with their own pages opened; MIVILUDES through public accounts): the line of every one of the six changed in some part. What stood: INFORM’s founding in 1988 at the London School of Economics by Eileen Barker, and that it is working; CESNUR’s founding year, and that it is working; the ICSA’s start in the United States in 1979, and that it is working; Hassan’s former membership of the Unification Church; Recovering from Religion’s description, and that it is working; MIVILUDES’ creation by decree in 2002. What was corrected or narrowed: the thirteen items numbered below. **Six books checked** (Singer, Hassan, Lifton, Barker, Lalich, Bromley and Shupe): five confirmed, one corrected (Hassan’s title). **The advice row** (three points): rewritten.

**Corrected, what it says now, and what settled it**
1. *The ICSA’s address.* `https://www.icsahome.com/` is a notice that the site “is being decommisioned” and points to the new site; the box now links `https://www.internationalculticstudies.org/` and the checklist source links the checklist page there (both opened).
2. *The ICSA’s line.* It said “a United States body for former members, families and professionals” that “starts from the view that some groups harm their members, publishes guidance and research”. The ICSA’s own pages say it provides “education, support, and collaboration for survivors, families, and professionals”, that it “began in 1979 as the American Family Foundation”, that it is “not an activist or advocacy group”, and that it publishes a peer-reviewed open-access journal and holds annual conferences (since 1992). The line now says that. “Starts from the view that some groups harm their members” was dropped: no page read says it in those words. (Its History page says it was renamed in 2002; Wikipedia says December 2004: the line gives no year.)
3. *The checklist.* The page paraphrased twelve characteristics; the ICSA’s current list has fifteen, so three were added (leaders dictating how members think, act and feel; preoccupation with bringing in new members; the most committed feeling that there is no life outside the group), “guilt” became “guilt or shame”, and the “us against them” item no longer says “even family” (the ICSA’s item says the view may bring the group into conflict with the wider society). The note now says who first presented it (Michael Langone, in the 1990s: the new page’s byline is “ICSA Team” with no author; an older copy of the page names Langone *(summary)*), that the current version is from ICSA Today 6(3), 2015, and quotes the ICSA’s own caution, “not so much a diagnostic instrument as it is an analytical tool”.
4. *INFORM’s aim.* The line said it describes its aim as information about “new and minority religions that is accurate and balanced, neither for nor against any of them”. The pages say “information about minority religions and sects which is as accurate, up-to-date and as evidence-based as possible” and “We exist to prevent harm based on misinformation”; the home page says “as balanced and reliable as possible”. “Neither for nor against” is not on the pages read and was dropped. Added: it is an independent educational charity and is now at King’s College London (its Contact page).
