5. *CESNUR.* It said “founded in 1988 in Turin”: its own About page says only “founded in Italy in 1988”; Wikipedia gives Turin as the headquarters, so the line says founded in Italy and based in Turin. “An international network of scholars” is not its own wording and was dropped (it says it was established “by a group of religious scholars”). “Publishes articles and holds conferences” became a journal (*The Journal of CESNUR*), books and an annual conference (2026 in Taiwan, 2027 in Buenos Aires). “Its scholars generally caution against judging a group by its label” became what its papers say: each group should be examined on its own merits, and it opposes special laws against “cults”. “Critics, among them support organisations” became “some other scholars and journalists”: the criticisms the English Wikipedia article records are by Stephen A. Kent (2001), the anthropologist Richard Singelenberg (*Trouw*, 1997), journalists (*Charlie Hebdo*, 1996; *L’Humanité*, 2001) and the Apologetics Index; none by a support organisation was found. No founders are named: Wikipedia names Introvigne, Mayer and Zucchini, the 2008 paper Introvigne, Mayer and Bishop Casale.
6. *Freedom of Mind.* “A United States counsellor” became what the site says: “a licensed mental health professional” (Wikipedia’s infobox adds LMHC); the site calls him a former member of “the Moonies” (Wikipedia: recruited at 19, a member for two and a half years). “Who set out the BITE model paraphrased above” was wrong twice: the page names the model but does not paraphrase it, and the claim of first publication is not supported (next item); the line now says his site says he developed it.
7. *BITE in 1988.* No page opened says that the 1988 book presents the BITE model. An older text of the Steven Hassan article (a search summary) says the 1988 book describes four “components of mind control” without the name and that the name BITE came with *Releasing the Bonds* (2000); the current article says *Freedom of Mind* (2012); an IBTimes article says 1988. “Where the BITE model was first set out” and “(1988)” after the model in the box were removed; the source now says that its later editions present the model (the English Wikipedia article on the book cites the 2018 edition, pp. 108 and 111, for it).
8. *The title of Hassan’s book.* It was cited as *Combating Cult Mind Control* (1988). The first edition is *Combatting* Cult Mind Control (two t’s) in Open Library (Rochester, Vt.: Park Street Press, 1988; ISBN 0-89281-243-5) and in the Internet Archive (identifier `combattingcultmi0000hass`); Open Library notes that the 2016 third edition “published in 1988 as Combatting cult mind control” is titled *Combating*; Freedom of Mind’s current book page also says *Combating*. The citation now gives the first edition’s spelling and says later editions are spelled with one t. The Library of Congress record could not be opened, and no title page was seen: two library-catalogue aggregators and Wikipedia’s reference list agree.
9. *Recovering from Religion.* Confirmed in substance (“a secular nonprofit that helps people who are questioning, leaving, or recovering from religion”; “a 501(c)(3) nonprofit since 2013”; the words “cult” and “high-control” do not appear on the pages read). “Takes no view on any one group” became what it says: it is “not against anyone’s faith” and “will not try to talk you out of belief”; its services were added (peer conversations, online groups, a therapist directory). Its pages disagree about its start (About: founded 2009 by Darrel Ray, first group on 19 April 2009; home page: “founded in 2013”), so the line gives no year.
10. *MIVILUDES.* Kept in substance. Added from the French Wikipedia (which cites the texts): created by decree no. 2002-1392 of 28 November 2002, replacing the earlier mission on “sectes”, attached to the Ministry of the Interior by decree no. 2020-867 of 15 July 2020 (law no. 2024-420 of 10 May 2024 gave it statutory footing; not in the line), and the decree’s own wording (groups “of a sectarian character” whose practices harm human rights, threaten public order or break the law; the English article gives the same). The dispute (*disputed*): at second hand only. French Wikipedia cites Olivier Bobineau (a sociologist of religion: that it blurs “secte”, “phénomène sectaire” and “dérive sectaire”), Nathalie Luca (who resigned in November 2005) and Sébastien Fath (who refused to take part; *Le Monde*, 20 December 2005); a search summary of the State Department’s 2009 report says some minority religious groups worried that its publications fed public distrust, and the 2004 report of the US Commission on International Religious Freedom judged the change from the earlier mission an improvement. None of these was read in the original, so the line says only that the use of the label is disputed. It keeps no link.
11. *The advice (“If you are worried about someone”).* Matched for the first time. INFORM’s “Guidelines for Friends & Family”: “Keep in touch”, “Listen!”, ask “What if?” questions, do not tell converts they are “mistaken, stupid, duped or brainwashed”, “get as much accurate, balanced and up-to-date information as possible”, and a warning against kidnapping or forceful removal. Pages on the ICSA’s site: “Stay connected”, “be ready to listen and learn”, “don’t argue”, gather information from “various sources”. **Not found in either:** ultimatums, “people who feel judged tend to defend the group”, and a doctor, lawyer or counsellor outside the group (INFORM names the police and social services for danger; the ICSA offers referrals). The first point now names both sources and drops the first two; the second says the professional part is the project’s own suggestion; the third (emergency number or child protection) agrees with INFORM’s police and social services.
12. *The box’s note* said the addresses “have not been re-checked”; it now says when five were opened and that MIVILUDES’ own site could not be.
13. *Singer.* The record agrees (Jossey-Bass, San Francisco, 1995, ISBN 0-7879-0051-6; Open Library lists Singer alone, the book itself names Lalich as co-author). “Chapter 3” was added because the Cult Education Institute’s reprint of the chapter is headed “Cults in our Midst, Chapter 3” and sets Singer’s conditions beside Lifton’s themes in a “Table 3.3”; the ICSA’s page “Six Conditions” cites “Singer, 1995”. The chapter number rests on that reprint, not on a copy of the book.

**Confirmed as written, or in substance.** Lifton, Norton, 1961 (Open Library: New York, first edition 1961), chapter 22 (two secondary pages cite Lifton’s eight criteria to chapter 22 of the 1989 reprint; the Norton contents were not seen); Barker, Blackwell, Oxford, 1984; Lalich, University of California Press, 2004 (ISBN 0-520-23194-5); Bromley and Shupe, Beacon Press, Boston, 1981 (Open Library lists only Bromley); the BITE letters stand for Behavior, Information, Thought and Emotional control (the site); that some scholars dispute “mind control” and parts of the model (Wikipedia names Shupe and Bromley, Gallagher, and, of his counselling approach, Langone).

**Not settled.** The Library of Congress record for the 1988 title; when the name BITE first appeared; the primary texts behind the dispute over MIVILUDES (the State Department report, the OSCE’s report of 2026 that the search summary mentions, the *Le Monde* article); the Norton contents for Lifton’s chapter 22; who first presented the ICSA checklist (the older copy’s byline was read through a summary); the English form of CESNUR’s name (the Wikipedia lead says “on New Religions”, the site’s title tag was read as “in”; the line keeps “on”).

**For the maker or the project manager.** (1) MIVILUDES’ official address, from French Wikipedia’s infobox, is `www.miviludes.interieur.gouv.fr`; it refused the fetch tool and may open in a browser. If the maker opens it and wants a link, add its host to `HELP_HOSTS` in `tests/movements.test.js` and set `url`. (2) For symmetry with CESNUR, Hassan and MIVILUDES, the ICSA’s line has no critic’s view: Cowan and Bromley, as the English Wikipedia article on the ICSA reports them, call it a “secular anticult” organisation and say its checklist gives no number of traits and is broad enough to take in mainstream faiths. Not added, because it was read at second hand only. (3) Nobody has looked for criticism of INFORM or of Recovering from Religion; their lines say only what each says of itself. (4) `POST1930_WANTED.csv` and `tools/post1930_wanted.py` (another session’s files) already give Hassan’s title as *Combatting* Cult Mind Control and were not touched.

**Files.** `src/js/2z-data-movements.js` (`MOV_HELP`, the ICSA yardstick, `MOV_ASK.from`, `.worried.points` and `.note`, the sources `icsa`, `singer1995`, `hassan1988`, one comment); `tests/movements.test.js` (the host lists, three assertions rewritten, six checks added: 84 checks, all passing in a private clone; `neutral` 20, `rename` 23, `privacy` 21, `data-integrity` 41, `sections` 45 also pass); `README.md` (the paragraph on the questions box). The built file `Lamplighter.html` is the project manager’s to rebuild.

## Conflicts and peacemaking (BACKLOG D8): the page, its guard, and the first eight entries, 18 November 2026

**What was built.** `tools/atlas_src/json/world_conflicts.json` (`{ meta, entries }`), laid over the events of the world by `tools/build_atlas.py` (`merge_conflicts`, then `check_world` and the new `check_conflicts`; the built `atlas/world.js` carries the entries in the conflict strand and the meta as `world.meta.conflicts`); the page `src/js/2z-conflicts-core.js` and `src/js/6zzd-conflicts.js` at `#/conflicts` and `#/conflicts/<id>` (Traditions → More → Conflicts and peace: Traditions already has four tabs); `tests/conflicts.test.js` (37 checks; the guard is run on 23 kinds of broken entry). **Eight entries** exist so far: `thirty-years-war` (replaces the pilot's entry of the same id), `westphalia-religious-terms`, `peace-of-augsburg`, `french-wars-of-religion`, `edict-of-nantes` (the Edict of 1598 and its revocation of 1685), `first-fitna`, `karbala`, `ashoka-edicts`: 4 wars and 4 settlements; Europe 5, Asia 3, Africa 1 (the First Fitna touches Egypt too); none within living memory yet. The other 77 or so planned entries are not written (see the hand-over note `conflicts-handover.md` in the session notes folder). The pilot's other seven conflicts (Kadesh, the Greco-Persian, Peloponnesian and Punic Wars, the First Crusade, the Spanish conquest, the Taiping Rebellion) are untouched: they have no richer fields, so they are on the timeline and not yet on the Conflicts page.

**What was opened, and how.** English Wikipedia through the MediaWiki interface, one request per article, six seconds apart, text kept only in the session scratchpad (one HTTP 429 at the start: waited, then slower): Thirty Years' War, Peace of Augsburg, Edict of Nantes, French Wars of Religion, First Fitna, Battle of Siffin, Battle of Karbala. **Primary texts:** the Treaty of Westphalia (the Münster treaty, Avalon Project's translation of the time: read in full; the Osnabrück treaty with Sweden was not opened, so the terms for religion rest on the Münster text, which confirms the Osnabrück agreement "also with those call'd the Reformed", and on Wikipedia's summary of Articles 5 and 7); the Revocation of the Edict of Nantes, 22 October 1685 (Fordham Internet Modern History Sourcebook: read; the Edict of 1598 itself was not opened because Fordham's page answered HTTP 500); Rock Edicts V, XII and XIII of Ashoka and the introduction, in S. Dhammika's translation (BPS 1993, the free-distribution edition on the Colorado State University site). **Scholarly:** Peter H. Wilson's own summaries of *Europe's Tragedy* (Rorotoko, 23 April 2010, parts 1 and 2; the book was not opened). **Reference:** "Nantes, Edict of" (Gale, encyclopedia.com). **Could not be opened:** Britannica (HTTP 403 to both the shell and the fetch helper), Encyclopaedia Iranica and World History Encyclopedia (403).

**Choices a second reader should look at.** (1) Wikipedia's list of "main contents" of the Edict of Nantes (militia paid by the state, universities, burial rights) was not used: it reads as unsourced, and the entry keeps to the four-part structure, freedom of conscience and worship in specified places, Catholicism established (Wikipedia and Gale) and the ministers' fortnight (Fordham). (2) The First Fitna and Karbala rest on two Wikipedia articles each (grade C); the early chronicles they summarise were not opened, so every statement about what a side said is marked reported. (3) The French Wars' "two to four million" is the article's lead figure: it names no one for either end. (4) Karbala's 70 to 72 is the classical accounts' count of Husayn's side as the article gives it; the other side's 88 (the article's text) and "more than 100" (its infobox) are in the note. (5) Ashoka's date is c. 260 to 250 BCE, confidence L: the text gives the Kalinga conquest "eight years after his coronation" and the introduction dates it 262 BCE; Dhammika renders the word he translates "religions", which other translators render "sects" (known from a search snippet, not opened). (6) Wilson's "neither inevitable nor a religious conflict" and "does not hold up" are quoted as his view and set beside the Wikipedia article's, which calls religion an important factor; the cause is graded mixed and disputed, with neither chosen.

**Changed in shared files.** `tools/build_atlas.py` (the merge and the guard only); `src/js/2z-sections-core.js` (one tab under Traditions, marked `more`); `src/css/app.css` (the `@conflicts` block before `@history`); `src/js/6pw-history.js` (a link from a conflict's panel to its full entry); `tests/sections.test.js` (the new key, and the Traditions More test); `tests/history.test.js` (the events are the merge of `world_events.json` and `world_conflicts.json`; five source hosts added to HOSTS: avalon.law.yale.edu, sourcebooks.web.fordham.edu, www.encyclopedia.com, www.rorotoko.com, www.cs.colostate.edu; any status allowed for the dead). Not changed: `world_events.json`, `world_strands.json` (its conflict blurb still says "A pilot of eight"), `world_meta.json`. **`atlas/world.js` must be rebuilt** (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`, the project manager's step): until then `tests/history.test.js` fails its built-file check and `tests/conflicts.test.js` skips its own. In a private clone with the atlas rebuilt: conflicts 37, history 80, sections 45, neutral 20, atlas 103, data-integrity 41, tradlist 67, chain 52, tree 46, causes 28, stories 43, all passing; the page was looked at in the browser pane at 1280 by 800 and 375 by 812 (no sideways scroll; every entry has the same six headings, and a seventh, Connected, when it has links).


## Second reading, part 2: the 179 cause rows of Compare traditions step by step (18 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (a child session of the project manager, not the session that wrote the rows) went through `tools/atlas_src/json/interaction_causes.json` and its meta file, in this order: the 33 rows marked “less sure”, then all 146 others for statements that contradict standard accounts, with the rows that connect the most traditions (Christianity, Catholicism, Islam, Rome, Buddhism) first. *Addresses:* every one of the 144 different English Wikipedia pages the rows cite was looked up through the MediaWiki interface (three requests, six seconds apart). 124 exist as written; 15 are redirects that land on the right page (left as they are); **one led to the wrong page** (“History of Buddhism in Japan” redirects to the general “History of Buddhism”), **one is a list of meanings** (“Kapu”), **four do not exist** (“Raid on Somnath”, “Settlement of New Zealand”, “Táino”, “Śrāṇa”). Ten addresses were replaced. *Pages:* 26 of the 33 less-sure rows and 8 others were read against the cited page and a second page, through the web-fetch tool; several pages were long and were read in part or through a model summary, and a fact that rests on a summary is marked *(summary)* below. The Wikipedia interface answered 429 twice (the first time because two of my own scripts were running; the second after four pages at eight seconds); both times the reading stopped, and the rest went through the fetch tool. *Library texts:* the rows that name a Library text were checked against the Library’s own files (Eusebius, Alberuni, Herodotus, Augustine’s *Confessions*, Fox, Ellis, Marshall’s *Karen People*, Mills’s *Lhota Nagas*, Saeki, Showerman, Ashoka’s edict).

**Counts.** 179 rows. **34 rows read against a source** (26 of the 33 less-sure rows, 8 others); **42 rows changed** (those 34, and 8 changed on other grounds: ix0024 relabelled, ix0026 matched to the Atlas row, ix0122 relabelled, ix0128 a chronology mended from standard knowledge, ix0148 a sentence mended from standard knowledge and its address looked up, ix0163, ix0187 and ix0234 addresses only); **0 rows removed**. Of the 33 less-sure rows: 26 read, 14 now “sure” (their doubt was settled), 19 still “less sure” with a new note of what is left, 7 not read (ix0024, ix0026, ix0148, ix0167, ix0174, ix0187, ix0188). **145 rows have not been read against a source by a second reader**; the meta file says so (`review` stays “unchecked”; `review_note` gives the numbers and `second_reading` lists the rows).

**Corrected: what was wrong, what the row says now, what settled it**
1. *ix0251 (Buddhism → Transcendentalism).* The row said that Thoreau made the English version of the Lotus Sutra chapter printed in *The Dial* in 1844. English Wikipedia’s pages on Elizabeth Palmer Peabody and on the Lotus Sutra say that **Peabody** translated it, from a French translation, and that it was the first English version of any Buddhist scripture. Now it says so. Who made the French text, its date and the month of the issue were not confirmed.
2. *ix0128 (Luther).* The row said that his refusal to recant at the Diet of Worms (April 1521) led to his excommunication; he was excommunicated in January 1521, and Worms led to the ban. Mended from the standard dates (bull of 3 January 1521; Diet of 17 and 18 April; edict of 25 May); no page was opened for it.
3. *ix0134 (Edict of Fontainebleau).* “150,000 to 200,000 left” became the range the page gives, “about 210,000 to 900,000”; the Edict of Versailles (1787) was added from the same page.
4. *ix0148 (Latter Day Saints).* The row said that the church was “organised” in the Salt Lake Valley; it was organised in 1830 and gathered there. Both addresses exist.
5. *ix0201 (Dawoodi Bohra).* The page gives 1588 and a dispute taken to Akbar in 1597, not 1592 (the Atlas’s year); the row now says the sources differ and gives Dawud bin Qutb Shah and Sulayman bin Hasan; the page does not name the Sulaymani (still “less sure”).
6. *ix0257, ix0258 (the Jain division).* The row said that “Jain tradition” puts the division in the first centuries CE. The Śvetāmbara account (82 CE, Sivabhuti) does; the Digambara account is the twelve-year famine under Bhadrabahu (the page cites an inscription of about 270 BCE). Both are now told, with the Valabhi council at 454 CE (467 was not found); “historians see a gradual parting” was removed because neither page says it *(summary)*.
7. *ix0036 (Serapis).* “Documented” became “reported”: the story of the statue from Sinope comes from Plutarch and Tacitus, centuries later, and the page notes that Plutarch may be wrong and that the origin is debated *(summary)*.
8. *ix0033, ix0032 (Tutankhamun, Akhenaten).* The court did not simply return to Thebes: the page says Memphis, and that Thebes became the capital again *(summary)*. “Moved the court to Amarna (about 1353 BCE)” confused his accession with the move; the page gives his reign as c. 1353–1336 or 1351–1334 and the name change in his fifth year.
9. *ix0051 (Decius).* “And obtain a certificate”, “certificates that survive”: the cited Eusebius (in the Library) does not mention them (no “certificate” or “libellus” in the text); removed.
10. *ix0054 (Bar Kokhba).* “Jews were barred from Jerusalem” was not in the cited Dio. It is in the Library’s Eusebius (4.6, quoting Aristo of Pella: the nation “totally prohibited, by the decree and commands of Adrian, from even entering the country about Jerusalem”); added as a source. Dio’s book 69 itself was not opened.
11. *ix0060 (Greek religion).* “In 529 Justinian issued a law against pagan teaching” was not found on the pages read; the Platonic Academy page says it was closed by Justinian in 529 and notes speculation that it did not wholly disappear; the row says that and drops the law.
12. *ix0087 (Armenia).* “Some scholars put it a little later” is not on the page (301 “according to tradition”, from the fifth-century history ascribed to Agathangelos); removed.
13. *ix0207 (Khotan).* “Recorded in the Karakhanid and Chinese sources”: the page gives 1006 and cites a modern history; removed.
14. *ix0208 (Nalanda).* The chronicle does not name Nalanda: the page says Minhaj’s passage describes Odantapura and that Nalanda went on for decades *(summary)*; the row said both monasteries “were ruined in these years”.
15. *ix0209 (Somnath).* The sack is dated January 1026 by the page; al-Biruni’s own words (in the Library: “utterly ruined the prosperity of the country”) are about the whole raiding, and do not describe Somnath; “Raid on Somnath” does not exist, “Sack of Somnath” does.
16. *ix0274 and lineage:japanese_buddhism.* The page names the Nihon Shoki for 552 and gives 538 only to “other sources”; the “temple chronicle” the row named was dropped. The lineage row’s page was the wrong one (see above).
17. *ix0339 (Hawaiʻi).* “By eating together” and the baptism of Kaʻahumanu “in 1825” are not on the page read (it says the kapu system was overthrown in 1819 by Kaʻahumanu and Keōpūolani with Kamehameha II, and that missionaries came from 1820); the row now says that. The address was a list of meanings.
18. *ix0340 (Tonga).* The page read gives only his reign (king from 1845); the missionaries’ years and his conversion year are not on it; they were taken out and the row is still “less sure”.
19. *ix0419 (Wahhabism).* “Took Mecca and Medina in 1803 and 1804” became “in a campaign that began in 1803”; the page records the destruction of the shrine at Karbala in 1801 but not of tombs at Mecca and Medina *(summary)*; Burckhardt was not opened.
20. *ix0451 (New Zealand).* The page gives 1320 to 1350 for the main settlement and DNA estimates of 1250 to 1300; the row now says both.
21. *ix0496 (Nagas).* Clark, Molung and 1872 are not in the cited book (*The Lhota Nagas* has no “Clark”); it says that the American Baptist Mission had its headquarters at Impur in the Ao country, and that it and Hinduism pressed on Lhota custom. The row says that.
22. *ix0023 (Babylon → Israel).* No page read says that Babylonian stories shaped biblical ones; Smith’s book (about Israel’s gods) was dropped as a second source; the second cause was relabelled from “court” to “travel” (the forced migration of the exiles); the Chronicle is now named (Chronicle 5, tablet BM 21946: the table on the English Wikipedia page lists both).
23. *ix0024, ix0122 (labels).* “Shared origin” cannot apply to two traditions with no common source; relabelled “court” (disputed). The Crusades were not a “mission” in the sense of the list; the pope’s call is now a “court” cause (reported).
24. *ix0035 (Isis).* The page read gives no date for the shrine at Delos and does not mention Piraeus; “by the 3rd century BCE … at Delos and Piraeus” was taken out (still “less sure”).
25. *ix0141, ix0412, ix0225, ix0226, ix0276, lineage:georgian_orthodox, lineage:russian_orthodox, ix0079.* Made exact, nothing wrong: the debated origin of the Baptists (the page gives four views, the English-Dissenter view held by most); the two sides’ reasons in the Lahore split; Jahangir’s memoir against Sikh tradition for Arjan, and the Sikh against the Mughal and Persian accounts for Tegh Bahadur (11 November 1675); the years Samye is given (Testament of Ba, Blue Annals) and that its pillar inscription is undated; the dates proposed for Iberia (312 to 337) and that no book or chapter of Rufinus is given on the page; “about 988, the year is disputed” and Chersonesus; Mani’s death at Gundeshapur in 274 (277 in another reckoning) under Bahram I.

**Confirmed.** Mani died in prison under Bahram I; Guru Arjan died in Mughal custody at Lahore in 1606; Samye about 779; Tiridates III and 301 as the traditional date; Smyth’s congregation at Amsterdam in 1609; Candomblé’s first houses in the early 19th century (Casa Branca, by tradition 1830); the Lahore split of 1914; the Primary Chronicle compiled in the 1110s; the Kara-Khanid conquest of Khotan in 1006 (the page, with no dispute noted); the Library’s texts for al-Biruni’s sentence, Fox at Pendle Hill in 1652, Augustine’s “books of the Platonists translated from Greek into Latin”, Herodotus at Naucratis, the lost-book tradition and Judson and Wade among the Karen, Ellis on Pomare. The other 120 or so rows were read through and no statement was found to contradict a standard account; that is a reading, not a check.

**Left.** 145 rows have had no second reading against a source. Not opened: Cassius Dio 69, Rufinus, Minhaj, Burckhardt, Agathangelos, the Syriac Acts, Murphy’s book (the title *Santería: An African Religion in America* is from memory, ix0167), and the pages for ix0024, ix0167, ix0174, ix0187 and ix0188; their notes say so. **For the page’s owner** (`6zzc-chain.js`, not changed here): its heading still says that the causes “have not yet been checked against their sources”; the note under it now gives the real position, and the heading could say “read once by a second reader”. **For the project manager:** `atlas/causes.js` is built from these files and must be rebuilt (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`); in a private clone with the Atlas rebuilt the tests below pass.

**Files.** `tools/atlas_src/json/interaction_causes.json` (42 rows), `interaction_causes_meta.json` (`review_note`, new `second_reading`), `tests/causes.test.js` (one check rewritten, three added). The script that made the edits asserted the old text of every row it changed.

**Tests (private clone, Atlas rebuilt there; the shared `atlas/` was not written).** `causes` 30, `chain` 52, `history` 80, `atlas` 103, `atlas-texts` 37, `data-integrity` 41, `neutral` 20, `movements` 84, `privacy` 21, `rename` 23, `sections` 45, `tree` 46: all passed, 0 failed.


## Second reading, part 2b: the cause rows again (21 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (another child session of the project manager, not the one that wrote the rows and not the one of part 2) took the 145 rows that part 2 had not read, in this order: the five rows marked “less sure” that nobody had opened (ix0024, ix0167, ix0174, ix0187, ix0188), then the rows that connect the most traditions (Christianity 34 rows, Catholicism 33, Islam 18, Rome 11, Buddhism 10), then the rows whose source is a text in the Library. *Library texts*, searched on the project’s own files and so for nothing: Eusebius (books 8 and 9), the Cyrus Cylinder (Rogers), Herodotus, Showerman’s *Great Mother*, Augustine’s *Confessions* and his writings on the Donatists, the Seven Ecumenical Councils (Nicaea, Ephesus, Chalcedon), the Xi’an stele in Saeki’s translation, Ashoka’s rock edict (Smith), Ellis, Marshall’s *Karen People*, Bede, Sozomen, Fox and Wesley. *Wikipedia*, through the web-fetch tool and never by script: the five less-sure rows’ pages were read in part (several pages, at two offsets each; Zoroastrianism, Haitian Vodou, Islamic–Jewish relations, Christianity and Islam, Santería, and the Open Library search for Murphy’s book); for 72 other pages only the **opening section** was read, as plain text through five single requests of the English Wikipedia interface, each asking for the openings of 7 to 20 pages (8 requests in all, none answered 429 or 503; three requests for longer extracts were refused by the interface, which allows 1,200 characters, and gave only a beginning). *Search summaries* (five searches for the rows, used only where said): the Haitian campaign, the Code Noir, the Neronian persecution, Hong Xiuquan, and Cakobau’s conversion (which changed nothing). Nothing was downloaded.

**Counts.** 179 rows. **54 rows read this time** (19 against Library texts, 5 against the pages they cite, 30 against the opening of the Wikipedia page they cite); **21 of them changed, and 33 found as written**; **2 more rows changed on other grounds** (a search summary: ix0173, ix0293); **0 removed**. With part 2, **88 rows have been read against a source** (34 + 54); **91 have not** (8 of them changed on other grounds: ix0026, ix0122, ix0128, ix0148, ix0163, ix0234, ix0173, ix0293). The five rows never opened are now all read; **all five stay “less sure”** with a note of what was not found (none could be made sure from what could be opened), and **one row became “less sure”** (ix0293): 20 rows are marked “less sure”, 19 before. The meta file’s `review_note` and `second_reading` give both passes, with the ids (`passes` lists each pass’s rows, and the 21 November pass says which were read in the Library, on a whole page, or at the opening only).

**Corrected: what was wrong, what the row says now, what settled it**
1. *ix0052 (Great Persecution).* “Ended in 311” was only half so: Galerius ended it with an edict in 311, Maximin, who held the east, held the edict back (Eusebius, book 9, “The pretended relaxation”) and the persecution there ended when Licinius defeated him, 313 by the usual dating (the opening of the Wikipedia page gives 311 for Galerius and 313 for Licinius). The Atlas’s own date for the connection (`interactions.json`) still reads 303–311: **for the page’s owner**.
2. *ix0050 (Nero).* “Documented” became “reported”: Tacitus, writing some fifty years later, is the only account that links the Christians to the fire (the page’s opening says “according to Tacitus”); the row now says that some historians doubt it describes a persecution of Christians as such (Brent Shaw, *Journal of Roman Studies* 105, 2015, from search results only: not opened) and that others answer them. Tacitus’s *Annals* is not in the Library and was not opened. The Atlas gives this connection the place Jerusalem; the events were at Rome (for the page’s owner).
3. *ix0173 (Code Noir).* “Forbade other worship” became “forbade the public practice of any other religion” (article 3), and “the French colonies” became “the French islands” (the text says “our Islands”). Read as translations in a search summary; the French text and the Library were not opened, so the row is marked as changed on other grounds.
4. *ix0106 (Clovis, the Saxon wars).* The baptism “about 496” is the Atlas’s date; the page’s opening gives “between 498 and 506”, and the Capitulary of 782 is “traditionally dated 782”, occasionally 785, with 795 also considered possible; its article 8 punishes by death a Saxon who wished to remain unbaptised and pagan.
5. *ix0085 (Reccared).* The row put his creed at the Third Council of Toledo (589); the page’s opening says he renounced Arianism in **587**; both now stand. The council’s year is not on the opening.
6. *ix0124 (Moriscos).* It said the Muslims *of Granada* were given the choice in 1502; the page’s opening says the Catholic Monarchs outlawed the practice of Islam in 1502 (it names the Crowns of Castile and Aragon) and that between 1609 and 1614 they began to expel the Moriscos. The row now says that and names no kingdom for 1502. The separate order for Aragon’s Muslims (1525 to 1526), which a reader might expect, is not on the opening and was not added.
7. *ix0123 (Reconquista).* “Took back territory over some seven centuries” took the term as settled; the opening dates it traditionally from Covadonga (about 718 or 722) to Granada in 1492, says the name belongs to a traditional history tied to Spanish nationalism in the 19th century and that recent scholarship treats it as a process of several episodes. The row now says “took territory southward over several centuries” and gives that.
8. *ix0125 (Toledo).* “From the 1130s” is not on the page, which gives the 12th and 13th centuries, a first period under Archbishop Raymond and a turn to Old Spanish in the 13th. Gerard of Cremona is not named in the opening; his place stays as written.
9. *ix0059.* The date of the Edict of Thessalonica, 27 February 380, and “state church” are the page’s; added.
10. *ix0034 (Naucratis).* In the Library’s Herodotus (2.178) it is Amasis (reigned from 570 BCE) who gave the Greeks Naucratis; “from about 620 BCE” is a later reckoning from finds and was not opened. The row gives both. Herodotus on the names of the gods (2.50) was found as written.
11. *ix0047 (Great Mother).* “A temple was built for her on the Palatine” was not in Showerman’s text, which says she was carried to the temple of Victory on the Palatine, inside the sacred boundary, “contrary to the usual practice” for a foreign god, on the day before the Nones of April (4 April), 204 BCE; the row says that, without the day.
12. *ix0064.* The source was “Confessions, book 7”; Marius Victorinus is named as the translator only in book 8, so both are cited.
13. *ix0088 (Church of the East).* “The Persian church did not accept the condemnation” is not what the page’s opening says: the church in Persia met in 410 and organised itself under one bishop, and later “came to align itself with Theodore of Mopsuestia, and by extension with his student Nestorius”, whom the Roman church condemned at Ephesus. The row now says that; the later formal steps (the synods of 424 and the 480s) were not read.
14. *ix0102 (Alopen).* “A monk travelled from Persia”: the stele says from Ta-ch’in (Saeki reads Syria), and a Chinese record Saeki quotes calls the temple Persian; the note says both. The year 635 and the minister sent to meet him are on the stele.
15. *ix0084 (Ulfilas).* “A Goth raised among Christians”, “ordained about 341” and “he translated the Bible” are not on the page’s opening, which credits him with the Gothic Bible and says analysis suggests a team; the Library’s Sozomen (6.37) calls him bishop of the Goths and says the Goths received Arianism. The row says that and leaves out the rest.
16. *ix0097 (Rus’).* The opening says “c. 988 (the year is disputed)” and that Vladimir was baptised at Chersonesus; the row now says so and its cause is “reported”, not “documented”.
17. *ix0465 (Fraticelli).* The opening says the Fraticelli were first declared heretical in 1296 by Boniface VIII; the row’s account of John XXII (1322 to 1323) stands and now says the name is older.
18. *ix0293 (Hong Xiuquan; search summary only, “less sure”).* The row put the tracts “(1832)” after his failure; 1832 is the year Liang Fa’s tracts were published; he was given them in 1836, had the visions in 1837 after failing the examinations, read the tracts closely in 1843 and studied with Issachar Roberts in 1847 (the page’s opening gives only the visions).
19. *The five rows marked “less sure”.* **ix0024:** the page says Jews under Achaemenid rule are believed to have been influenced by Zoroastrian ideas of angels, demons, the last things, predestination and resurrection and that other scholars disagree (the dispute is on the page); it does not mention Cyrus or 539 BCE, which the row keeps from the Conflicts page. **ix0167:** Open Library confirms Murphy’s title (*Santeria: An African religion in America*, first published 1988); the Santería page gives the 16th to 19th centuries for the whole trade and the late 19th century for the first houses, so “above all in the first half of the 19th century” and “mutual-aid societies” were taken out. **ix0174:** the Haitian Vodou page gives 1941, “Operation Nettoyage” and “Church-backed”, and neither Lescot nor 1942; a search summary of several articles (not opened: one site failed its certificate, one refused the reader, one was not tried) says the Church led it, the government and the National Guard backed it, it ended in 1942 and the sources differ on Lescot’s part; the row says all that with its sources marked and the cause is “reported”. **ix0187, ix0188:** the pages describe contact (Muhammad’s views of the Jews “shaped by his contact with the Jewish tribes of Arabia”, Islam “strongly influenced by Judaism”, and a historian who calls his message foreign to Judaism; Waraqah ibn Nawfal among the Christians) and do not call the Qur’an’s debt to Christian sources argued; “shared origin” fits only the Qur’an’s own account, and the fixed list of ten causes has no label for neighbours in contact, so the notes give both. Ethiopia, which the Christianity row named, was not on the part read and was taken out.

**Found as written (33 rows).** Against the Library: ix0025 (Cyrus, the gods brought back), ix0029 and ix0034 (Herodotus), ix0082 (the Donatist charge of traditorship, in Schaff’s introduction), ix0083, ix0089 and lineage:coptic_orthodox (the decrees of Nicaea and Chalcedon; Dioscorus deposed), ix0108 (Bede 3.3: Oswald asks for a bishop, Aidan is sent, Lindisfarne), ix0139 (Wesley’s conversion of 1738; the page: a separate denomination after his death), ix0142 (Fox on Pendle Hill; the Journal’s chapters 1624 to 1647), ix0263 (Rock Edict XIII, Tambapaṇṇi glossed as Ceylon), ix0457 (the *Duff* in Tahiti, March to August 1797; the first public baptism, Pomare’s, 16 July 1819) and ix0495 (the Lost Book; Wade putting Sgaw into writing in 1832). At the opening of the cited page: ix0093, ix0094, ix0095 (1054: Humbert excommunicated Cerularius, who excommunicated Humbert and the other legates; “conventional” date; the anathemas nullified in 1965 without reunion), ix0115, ix0117, ix0098, ix0469, ix0101, ix0149, ix0205, ix0204, ix0193, ix0045 (the page: the majority of scholars do not assume a noticeable Greek influence beyond the artistic realm), ix0273, ix0121 (authorised by Sixtus IV in 1478; originally aimed at converts from Judaism and Islam; the abolition of 1834 is not on the opening), ix0256, ix0214, ix0245, ix0394, ix0261 and ix0262 (the first split, Sthavira and Mahāsāṃghika, “during the reign of Aśoka” or shortly after, and historians disagree).

**Opened, but the part read did not settle the row (left as written, still among the 91 not read).** ix0126 (the openings of the *Guide* and of Samuel ibn Tibbon do not give the Hebrew translation of 1204 or the Latin one), ix0120 (only England, 18 July 1290, was on the opening), ix0112, ix0113 (the opening of the page on Lithuania names Mindaugas’s baptism in the 1250s and gives nothing for 1386 or 1387), ix0150, ix0166, ix0364, ix0058, ix0189 (the opening gives “December 629 or January 630”), ix0191, ix0192, ix0202, ix0246, ix0277, ix0278, ix0284 (the opening dates the persecution 841 to 845; the row says edicts of 842 to 845), ix0267 (the opening dates a conference to 13 October 1956; the row’s 14 October is not on it), ix0291, ix0195, ix0199, ix0200, ix0222, ix0247, ix0357, ix0395, ix0438 and lineage:hasidism, lineage:karaite (the page: “c. 7th–9th centuries”), lineage:rinzai, lineage:soto, lineage:zaydi, lineage:reform_judaism (the opening says its origins lie in “mid-19th-century Germany”; the row dates the first changes to 1810 and 1818), lineage:orthodox_judaism, ix0269 and ix0270 (the opening of the Blavatsky page says the move to India was in 1880 and the Ceylon vows in 1882; the rows give late 1878 or 1879 and 1880, which are the usual dates: the page’s body was not read and the difference is not settled). None was changed.

**For the page’s owner or the maker (nothing changed here).** (1) `6zzc-chain.js`: its heading counts `second_reading.read`, which is now 88; the note under it is the meta file’s `review_note`. (2) ix0189 (“the capture of Mecca, told in the early Muslim sources”) is “documented” while ix0191, the succession of 632, told in histories written one to two centuries later, is “reported”: the two follow different rules; one rule for events known from later Muslim accounts is for the maker. (3) “Shared origin” has no companion for neighbours in contact (ix0187, ix0188), and the legend’s “so no contact was needed” reads against summaries that describe neighbours in contact: whether to add a cause to the fixed list of ten is for the maker. (4) `interactions.json`: the place and date labels of ix0050 and ix0052 above.

**Not opened.** Tacitus’s *Annals* 15.44; Shaw (2015) and Jones’s answer (2017); the French text of the Code Noir; Murphy’s book and the Voyages database; the Haitian sources behind the search summary (FIU’s page failed its certificate, MDPI refused the reader); the body of most Wikipedia pages (only their openings were read); Agathangelos, Rufinus, Minhaj, Burckhardt and Cassius Dio, as in part 2; the *Mahavamsa*, Livy, Adam of Bremen and the other works the rows name which are not in the Library.

**Files.** `tools/atlas_src/json/interaction_causes.json` (23 rows), `interaction_causes_meta.json` (`review_note`, `second_reading`: the ids and the two passes), `tests/causes.test.js` (two checks changed, three added: 32 checks). **Tests (private clone, Atlas rebuilt there; the shared `atlas/` was not written).** `causes` 32, `chain` 52, `history` 80, `atlas` 103, `atlas-texts` 37, `data-integrity` 41, `neutral` 20, `sky` 238, `privacy` 21, `sections` 52, `tree` 46: all passed, 0 failed. **`methods` fails 3 checks** until the project manager’s `2z-data-methods.js` and `tests/methods.test.js` carry the new counts (88 read, 91 not; astrology 37 sources, five not opened): a tested patch is in the session notes (`second-reading-2b-methods-suggested.diff`).


## Second reading, part 3: the astrology entries (21 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** The same second reader (another child session, not the one that wrote `src/js/2z-data-astrology.js`) went back to the studies the entries rest on at second hand and to the ones the first reader could not open, through the web-fetch tool, one page at a time, stopping at any refusal; nothing was downloaded. **Opened:** the Europe PMC record of Phillips, Ruth and Wagner, “Psychology and survival”, *The Lancet* 342 (8880): 1142–1145 (1993) (the abstract, in the record’s own words); the Crossref record of Goodkind (1991) (the citation, with no abstract); two web pages on Voas’s study of married couples (the Gauquelin5 project’s data page, and a news article by James Felton on IFLScience of 5 August 2021); the page of the *Journal of Scientific Exploration* for McRitchie’s 2023 rebuttal of Dean and Kelly (title, volume, pages, date, no abstract); and an astrologer’s review of Dean and Kelly on astrology.co.uk (by Robert Currey). **Could not be opened:** Carlson (1985) in *Nature* (the page redirects to a login; neither PubMed nor Europe PMC lists it); Voas’s paper (Manchester’s news page is gone, HTTP 404; the Smithsonian blog post refused the reader, 403; the Live Science article’s body was cut off); McRitchie’s 2016 commentary (PhilPapers, PhilArchive and the publisher’s abstract page all answered 403); Dean and Kelly’s own answer; Goodkind’s text (JSTOR was not tried again: the first reader found it closed, and Crossref has no abstract).

**Counts.** Of the five studies the brief named (Carlson, Phillips and others, Voas, Goodkind, McRitchie), **four were opened in some part and one, Carlson, could not be**. The six entries: **two changed** (the Western and the Chinese), **four not touched**; **8 sources added** (29 to 37), of which **5 are marked “not opened”** (Carlson, Tedlock, Goodkind’s text, Voas’s paper, McRitchie 2016; two before). **No statement already in the entries was found wrong.**

**Added, and what it rests on**
1. *Chinese entry, a fourth study (Phillips, Ruth and Wagner 1993).* The record’s abstract: the deaths of 28,169 adult Chinese-Americans were compared with 412,632 matched controls coded “white” on the death certificate; Chinese-Americans, not whites, died 1.3 to 4.9 years earlier when they had a combination of birth year and disease that Chinese astrology and medicine consider ill-fated; the more strongly a group kept Chinese traditions the more years were lost, and the effect held for nearly all major causes of death; it was not fully explained by changed behaviour of the patient, doctor or registrar and at least partly reflected psychosomatic processes. Status “reported” (a study’s report). The paper itself was not opened; a letter with the same title by Rhoads and others, *The Lancet* 343: 174 (1994), appears in a list of citations and is only named. The “not found” note under the Chinese tests now says the first three studies are about a belief about one animal year and the Lancet study about an old pairing of birth year and illness, and that no test of the Four Pillars was opened.
2. *Western entry, a fifth study (Voas 2007).* The Gauquelin5 page gives the data (the 2001 census of England and Wales; 10,317,649 married couples in its main table) and the title of the paper (“Ten million marriages: A test of astrological ‘love signs’”); the news article says the analysis used “all 20 million birthdays of married couples” and reports that there was no evidence that people married someone of a particular Sun sign (“just what we’d predict on the basis of chance”, Voas is quoted). Status “reported”, grade C for both pages, grade B and “not opened” for the paper. Neither page describes an anomaly: a search summary spoke of one explained by errors in the census data, which was **not** added because no page opened says it.
3. *Western entry, a fourth reply on the practitioners’ side.* McRitchie answered Dean and Kelly in the *Journal of Consciousness Studies* in 2016 (the title and the volume come from Currey’s page and a search result: not opened) and again in the *Journal of Scientific Exploration* 37 (3): 576–579 (2023), “Defense of Emergent Effects in Astrology Research: Rebuttal of Dean and Kelly” (the page was opened: no abstract). Currey’s review says the 2016 commentary “refuted” the article and was “a comprehensive response”, and that the article’s test of people born minutes apart is unpublished: said as his view, “reported”, grade C (a practitioner’s page).

**Confirmed or corrected.** *Goodkind (1991):* the citation is as the entry gave it (title, author, *Population and Development Review* 17(4), first page 663, December 1991; the record carries no end page and the DOI is 10.2307/1973601); what he found (the boost in births in Dragon years, 15.5 percent in Taiwan in 1976) is still taken from Johnson and Nye, and is now cited to Goodkind as well as to them. A search result quotes Goodkind as saying in an interview that timing births to the Dragon year is “a new tradition”: not opened, not added. *Carlson (1985):* nothing could be checked; the numbers still come from Ertel (2009), and the source’s note now says why the paper stayed closed. *Dean and Kelly (2003):* unchanged (the abstract page of the first reading). The Western, Hindu, Hellenistic, Maya and Aztec statements that rest on Wikipedia pages, Ptolemy, Augustine, Narlikar and Hindupedia were not re-read.

**Changed in the tests.** `tests/sky.test.js` allows two to five tested items and one to four replies on each side (it was four and three) so that the added studies need not displace the first reader’s; the weight rule still holds (the longest entry, Western, is 2.76 times the shortest, Maya; the limit is 3); one check added for the new facts (238 checks). **Files.** `src/js/2z-data-astrology.js`, `tests/sky.test.js`. **Tests (private clone):** `sky` 238, `neutral` 20, `privacy` 21, `sections` 52 and `tree` 46 passed. **For the project manager:** the Methods page’s line for astrology (“29 sources … two … No second reading yet”) is now wrong; a tested patch is in the session notes.


## Second reading, part 4: the last 91 cause rows (22 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A third reader (another child session of the project manager; not the writer of the rows and not the readers of parts 2 and 2b) took the 91 rows that no second reader had read against a source, highest value first (the rows that touch the most traditions: Christianity, Catholicism, Islam, Rome, Buddhism, then the rest). *Pages:* the English Wikipedia pages the rows cite and, where a cited page did not hold a claim, the page that should (the page of the council, the king, the movement), about 120 pages in all, were opened one at a time in the Claude app’s built-in browser, a few seconds apart, and **read whole**: for each claim of a row (a date, a name, a number, a place, a cause) the text of the page was searched and the sentence around each hit was read; a claim that could not be found on the pages read was narrowed or taken out. This differs from part 2b, which read only the opening section of most pages and through a fetch tool’s summary: nothing in this part rests on a summary. No address was asked of the Wikipedia interface by script and nothing was downloaded or saved. Where a cited address redirects the page it leads to was read; two addresses tried do not exist (“Extirpation of idolatry”, “Methodist Church in Fiji and Rotuma”). One page outside Wikipedia (the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy’s *Maimonides*) was opened and not searched. *Library texts*, searched on the project’s own files and so for nothing: Josephus (the war; the burning of the Temple), the introduction to the Mishnah (Yavneh), Galatians, Tertullian, Rufinus and Socrates (Frumentius), Bede (Gregory’s mission), Ghose’s introduction to Rammohun Roy’s *English Works*, Lindley’s *Ti-ping Tien-kwoh* (Hamberg on Hung), Aulard (the Convention’s decree), Calvin (the editions of the *Institutes*), the Book of Concord (the Augsburg Confession), Dayananda’s *Satyarth Prakash* (caste by merit), Graetz (the Latin Guide; the chapter on Hasidism) and Williams’s *Fiji and the Fijians*. No site refused the reading.

**Counts.** 179 rows. **91 rows read now: 56 changed** (a statement corrected or narrowed), **10 found as written with a source added**, **25 found as written**; 16 of the 91 were also read in the Library; **0 removed**. No “documented”, “reported” or “disputed” label and no “sure” or “less sure” mark was changed (one cause, the viceroy’s support in ix0159, was taken out for want of a page). With parts 2 and 2b **all 179 rows have now been read by a second reader** (34 + 54 + 91); the eight rows once changed on other grounds (ix0026, ix0122, ix0128, ix0148, ix0163, ix0173, ix0234, ix0293) are among the 91. 20 rows are still marked “less sure” (ix0293’s note now says that its four years are read on the page). A second reader is not a specialist: none of the rows has been read by one.

**Every row read.** *Changed (56):* ix0055, ix0056, ix0067, ix0073, ix0090, ix0099, ix0105, ix0107, ix0109, ix0110, ix0111, ix0120, ix0122, ix0126, ix0130, ix0136, ix0150, ix0155, ix0157, ix0158, ix0159, ix0164, ix0166, ix0171, ix0189, ix0191, ix0202, ix0203, ix0210, ix0246, ix0250, ix0265, ix0267, ix0269, ix0270, ix0272, ix0277, ix0278, ix0284, ix0289, ix0293, ix0309, ix0310, ix0334, ix0347, ix0357, ix0364, ix0395, ix0459, ix0488, lineage:anglicanism, lineage:conservative_judaism, lineage:lds, lineage:reform_judaism, lineage:rinzai, lineage:zaydi. *Found as written, a source added (10):* ix0008, ix0010, ix0021, ix0053, ix0127, ix0148, ix0173, ix0192, ix0247, lineage:soto. *Found as written (25):* ix0026, ix0058, ix0071, ix0112, ix0113, ix0128, ix0129, ix0131, ix0138, ix0156, ix0163, ix0195, ix0199, ix0200, ix0222, ix0234, ix0249, ix0252, ix0285, ix0291, ix0438, lineage:hasidism, lineage:karaite, lineage:orthodox_judaism, lineage:rabbinic.

**Corrected: what was wrong, what the row says now, what settled it**
1. *ix0056 (the Bacchanalia, 186 BCE).* The row said the Senate’s decree was “against” the Bacchanalia and survives “on a bronze tablet”. The page: “The cult was not banned”; the legislation set limits on the size, organisation and priesthoods of the rites under threat of death, “survives in the form of an inscription” (the *Senatus consultum de Bacchanalibus*), and Livy, who tells the affair, wrote some two hundred years later. The row now says that. The Atlas’s kind for this connection (“suppress”) is left for its owner.
2. *ix0150 (Mormon fundamentalism).* “The 1890 Manifesto, and a second one in 1904, ended the practice of plural marriage”: the page says the 1890 Manifesto prohibited new plural marriages in the United States and did not dissolve existing ones, that the practice continued underground and in colonies in Mexico and Canada, and that after the Second Manifesto of 1904 the church excommunicated those who “entered into or solemnized new polygamous marriages”. Mended.
3. *ix0090 (Frumentius).* The Library’s Rufinus: the barbarians “fell upon” the ship of the Tyrian traveller, killed him and took the boys to the king (so no “shipwreck”); Rufinus is “the earliest extant authority” and Socrates, Sozomen and Theodoret follow him (the informant “Frumentius’s brother” was not found and is out); Athanasius “conferred upon him the episcopal dignity”. “A young Syrian Christian” is not said of the boys in the text read. The Ezana page: coins shift “from a pagan motif with disc and crescent to a design with a cross”, and it calls Frumentius both a “Phoenician slave-teacher” and a “Syrian Christian”.
4. *ix0310 and ix0309 (Kimbangu).* “Arrested him in September … then reduced the sentence to life” is on neither page: they say the ministry lasted from April to September 1921, that he and his followers were arrested on 6 June and he escaped, that he was sentenced to death on 3 October 1921, and that he was imprisoned until he died at Élisabethville on 12 October 1951; the church was recognised in 1959. “Trained by the Baptist Missionary Society’s station” is on neither page; the movement’s page has a Baptist mission school and a “Baptist mission catechist”, and the page on his life says both that he worked as a catechist and that a Baptist missionary refused to make him one: the row now gives both.
5. *ix0126 (Maimonides, Aquinas).* The page: the *Guide* written “between 1185 and 1190”, put into Hebrew in 1204 by Samuel ibn Tibbon; “Aquinas and Duns Scotus making extensive use of it”; the first complete Latin version printed is of 1520. The Library’s Graetz: the scholastics used Maimuni’s work “translated into Latin at an early period”. “c. 1190”, “Latin in the 1230s” and “Rabbi Moyses” are on none and are out.
6. *ix0109 (Boniface).* The page: he first left for the Continent in 716, returned “the next year” and went to Rome, where Gregory II “appointed him missionary bishop for Germania”; “under the protection of Charles Martel from 723”; “according to the vitae Boniface felled the Donar Oak … near the present-day town of Fritzlar”; martyred in Frisia in 754. “From 719”, “monasteries” and “Geismar” are not on it.
7. *ix0459 (Fiji).* The Cakobau page: “converted to Christianity by the missionary James Calvert … in 1854”; he “crushed the rebellion with the aid of the King of Tonga”; the Religion in Fiji page: “Christianity came to Fiji via Tonga”. The Library’s Williams names Cross and Cargill, from Tonga, as appointed to begin the mission. “1835” and “pressed by” were not found and are out.
8. *ix0364 (dechristianisation).* Aulard (Library): “On 16th Frimaire the Convention decreed that ‘all violence and measures against liberty of worship are forbidden’” and the churches “were closed again in all places where it was observed that gatherings for worship were as much political as religious”; the page: “by Easter 1794, few of France’s 40,000 churches remained open” and the government “briefly mandated” the Supreme Being in April 1794. “The campaign faded in 1794” is replaced by that.
9. *ix0357 (Anthroposophy).* The page: “in 1912, Steiner broke away from the Theosophical Society”, and “the split became irrevocable when Annie Besant … presented a child Jiddu Krishnamurti as the reincarnated Christ”. “Expulsion” of his section, “1913”, “world teacher” and the Order of the Star are not on it.
10. *ix0159 (the Andes).* The pages: Lobo Guerrero was installed as archbishop of Lima on 4 October 1609 and “is considered the initiator of the first systematic, centrally organized campaign for the extirpation of idolatry”; Arriaga completed his *Extirpación* in 1620 (Lima, 1621). “Sent visitors through the highlands … to destroy the shrines” and the viceroy’s support (a whole cause) are on no page read and are out.
11. *ix0334 (New Zealand).* The row cited a page that does not hold the story. Marsden’s page: the first known Christian sermon on New Zealand soil, at Oihi Bay on Christmas Day 1814, at Ruatara’s invitation (the page on Christianity there); schools taught “religious instruction, reading and writing”; the missionaries studied Māori and began to translate the Bible; “in 1845, 64,000 Māori were attending church services, over half”. “Taught reading in Māori” is now “reading and writing”.
12. *ix0210 (Islam in Bengal).* The page names the conquest of Bakhtiyar Khalji (early 13th century), the preaching of Sufi missionaries “in villages”, and, under the Mughals, the clearing of the forested east for cultivation; “the advantage of joining the ruling faith” and “rice farming” are not on it and are out. That historians disagree about the weight rests on Eaton, not opened.
13. *ix0111 (Iceland, 1000).* The page: Thorgeir is the “pagan lawspeaker” who proposed “one law and one religion”; in the summary of *Íslendingabók* ch. 7 “the old laws on the eating of horsemeat will remain in force” and “people can make pagan sacrifices in private”; scholars date the book to 1122 to 1133 (the row had “about 1125” and “a pagan priest”).
14. *ix0164 (Kongo).* The pages: Nzinga a Nkuwu “decided he would become Christian”; after his death in 1506 the Catholic bloc under Afonso I won in 1509 and “Catholicism became the state cult”; Afonso reigned to “late 1542 or 1543”. The baptism year is “about 1491” and the row’s “1509–1542” was widened.
15. *ix0166 (hidden Christians).* The 1614 statement on the “expulsion of all missionaries” and the revolt of 1637 and 1638 are on the pages; “who kept their faith until the ban ended in 1873” is now “the ban was lifted in 1873, and the name is used today for those who still keep the traditions of the years in hiding”.
16. *ix0158 and ix0155, ix0157 (the Americas).* Landa: “on July 12, 1562 … 27 books [by his own count] and approximately 5,000 images were burned”; “provincial”, “recalled” and “his trial” are not on the page. The Templo Mayor “was almost totally destroyed by the Spanish in 1521”; the twelve Franciscans arrived on 13 or 14 May 1524. The Maya conquest began in Guatemala in 1523 to 1524 and in Yucatán in 1527 to 1528 and is “generally considered to have ended in 1697” (the row had “campaigns from 1524” for both).
17. *ix0293 (Hong Xiuquan; “less sure”).* Now read on the page: in 1836 at Guangzhou he received tracts written by Liang Fa from the missionary Edwin Stevens and his interpreter (“given by Liang Fa” was mended); the visions of 1837, the careful reading of 1843 and the study with Issachar Roberts in 1847 are all on it; “published in 1832” is not and is out. The Library’s Lindley (Hamberg) agrees on the tracts and on the cousin who found them. It stays “less sure” (a test pins it) with a note of what is left.
18. *lineage:reform_judaism, lineage:conservative_judaism, lineage:zaydi, lineage:rinzai.* “Seesen in 1810” and “the Hamburg Temple … with an organ and prayers in German” are not on the pages (they give the 1810s and 1820s circles and the liturgy of 1818); “1886” and “1913” for the seminary and the United Synagogue are not on the Conservative page (“fully institutionalized during the mid-20th century”); Zayd’s revolt in “740” and the Yemeni state “in 897” are not on the Zaydi page; Eisai’s journeys (1168; 1187 to 1191) and the “it is also said” of green tea are on his page but the shogunate’s support is not.
19. *ix0189, ix0191, ix0202.* The Conquest of Mecca page says only that the early sources give different dates (December 629 or January 630); “the city came over almost without fighting” and “kept as the centre of the pilgrimage” are not on it or on the Kaaba page. The earliest written life of Muhammad is Ibn Ishaq’s (d. 761 or 767), kept in Ibn Hisham’s (d. 833) and al-Tabari’s recensions (“one to two centuries” is now “one to three”). “Orders formed from the 12th century” is not on the Sufism page.
20. *ix0203, ix0272, ix0265, ix0250, ix0289.* Narrowed to the pages: the page on the translation movement gives only “8th–10th centuries” (“under the Abbasid caliphs” out; the *Theology of Aristotle* page confirms Plotinus and Porphyry); Buddhism in China has “no definitive answer” on the first arrival (Emperor Ming’s dream is out); the Mahayana page has Lokakṣema in the “2nd century” (“about 170 CE” out); Wilkins’s Gita of 1785 is “the first translation into English”, but that Emerson and Thoreau read it and printed extracts is on no page read; Joseon repression is on the page as limits on clergy and land and a ban on monks and nuns in the cities.
21. *ix0270, ix0269, ix0267, ix0284, ix0277, ix0278.* The Blavatsky page says Bombay in February 1879 in its body and “1880” in its opening (the row follows the body); the Olcott page has the Three Refuges and Five Precepts in 1880, the *Catechism* of 1881 and the flag committee of 1885 (“organised … a flag” and “a revival that the Sinhalese already led” are out); the Dalit Buddhist movement page has the conversion on 14 October 1956 with about 365,000 followers (the Navayana page has the conference of 13 October); Wuzong’s edict of 845 abolished almost all monasteries (the page’s 841 to 845 replaces “edicts between 842 and 845”); Sakya Pandita met Godan in “early 1247” (“Tibet’s submission … followed” is out); Altan Khan designated Sonam Gyatso “Dalai” in 1578.
22. *ix0347 (Pico).* Added from the page: Flavius Mithridates, on whose translations Pico relied, is called “a convert and prolific forger” who supplied “doctored Kabbalistic and seemingly pro-Christian texts” by Brian Copenhaver; “In Rome” and “translated into Latin for him” are narrowed.
23. *ix0395, ix0171, ix0055, ix0488, ix0110, ix0130, ix0136, ix0246.* Kūkai: 804, Huiguo, Chang’an are on the page; “806” and “texts, mandalas and ritual tools” are out. Haitian Vodou: “Kongo, Fon and Yoruba” (not “Ewe”), “taking the form of Vodou around the mid-18th century”. Mona: the page gives the campaign and the rising of the Iceni, not “the sacred groves were cut down”. Dacia: Sarmizegetusa was razed in 106 (“sanctuaries in the mountains” is out). Scandinavia: Ansgar’s mission and the conversion of the kings (960 to 1020) are on the pages, with Winroth’s view that the kings took it up willingly (now in the row). Anabaptism: 21 January 1525 is “the most widely accepted date”. Scotland: the 1560 legislation was “formally ratified by James VI in 1567”. Roy: the Library’s edition has the Serampore missionaries from 1816, the Precepts of 1820 “separated … from the abstruse doctrines and miraculous relations of the New Testament”, and the controversy.
24. *The smaller ones.* ix0067 (Galatians dated by most “between the late 40s and early 50s”; the page on the split added), ix0073 (Marcion from Sinope, in Tertullian a “ship-master”, to Rome between 135 and 140; excommunicated 144), ix0099 (Rastislav’s request of 862; the Glagolitic alphabet “credited”; the pupils to Bulgaria, the script to the Rus later), ix0105 (Palladius named for Prosper), ix0107 (Gregory’s sending is 596 and Augustine’s arrival 597 in Bede), ix0120 (a “decision of the king” is “a royal decree”: the Alhambra edict was by the two Catholic Monarchs), ix0122 (the Clermont accounts differ in detail; Fulcher is the most reliable; “historians weigh other motives” is on neither page), lineage:anglicanism (the act of 1533 not looked for), lineage:lds (“an angel directed him to” the plates; the page on Mormonism and Nicene Christianity says some Christians call it non-Christian and scholars differ on a branch of Christianity or a “fourth Abrahamic religion”).

**Found as written, with what settled it (the 25).** The Library: Josephus for ix0053, lineage:rabbinic and ix0071 (the war began “in the twelfth year of the reign of Nero”, the Temple burned on “the tenth day of the month Lous … the second year of the reign of Vespasian”; the Mishnah’s introduction has Jochanan allowed “to go to Jabneh”), Calvin and Zwingli’s page for ix0129, the Book of Concord for ix0128. The pages: ix0026 (Samaritans), ix0058 (25 December 274), ix0112 (24 July 1198 to c. 1290; the page adds that “there are no independent sources” for the start), ix0113 (baptised 15 February 1386), ix0131, ix0138 (1693), ix0156, ix0163, ix0195 (765), ix0199 and ix0200 (1094), ix0222, ix0234, ix0249 (11 September 1893), ix0252, ix0285, ix0291, ix0438, lineage:hasidism, lineage:karaite, lineage:orthodox_judaism.

**For the page’s owner or the maker (nothing else changed here).** (1) `6zzc-chain.js`: the sentence that says who read the causes now says “every one of the 179 rows has been read a second time against its sources by another reader; none has been read by a specialist” (and keeps the old sentence for a partial reading). (2) *Atlas facts (`interactions.json`, not edited here):* ix0050’s place is already Rome and the note of ix0052 already says “313 in the east” (an earlier integration); the date label of ix0052 still reads 303 CE – 311 CE, which is the date of Galerius’s edict; whether it should run to 313 is the owner’s choice. Several Atlas date labels and notes are not borne out by the pages read: ix0109 (719), ix0395 (806), ix0291 (to 1871), ix0150 (to 1935), ix0459 (1835), ix0272 (68 CE, Emperor Ming’s dream), ix0488 (“destroyed its mountain sanctuaries”), ix0056 (kind “suppress”). (3) One rule for events known from later accounts is still the maker’s: ix0189 (Mecca) stays “documented” though its page says the sources vary on the dates, and ix0191 and ix0192 are “reported”. (4) The note under each “disputed” cause says only that the matter is argued; ix0252 (a reform within the devotional currents or a revelation of its own) and ix0210 (the weight of the causes) rest on books that were not opened.

**Not opened.** Tacitus (*Annals* 14.30), Adam of Bremen, the French text of the Code Noir, Hilton, Eaton, MacCulloch, Madelung, Gutas, Zürcher, Versluis, Mallory and Adams, Mark Smith, Weinstein, the Voyages database, Ricard, and the *Guide*’s Latin translation itself, and the Stanford Encyclopedia’s *Maimonides* (opened, not searched). Rufinus was read only in the Library’s English edition.

**Files.** `tools/atlas_src/json/interaction_causes.json` (91 rows read, 66 touched), `interaction_causes_meta.json` (`review_note`; `second_reading`: `read` now lists all 179, `passes` has a third pass with its ids, `changed_on_other_grounds` is empty and `changed_on_other_grounds_since_read` keeps the eight), `src/js/6zzc-chain.js` (the one sentence), `src/js/2z-data-methods.js` (a `causes3` row in METH_LOG and the `causes` row of METH_STAGES), `tests/causes.test.js` (three review-state pins for three passes, one pin added for the corrections above: 33 checks), `tests/label-sweep.test.js` (SA-15 also pins the new sentence), `tests/methods.test.js` (the two pins on the cause rows), `README.md`. **Tests (private clone, Atlas rebuilt there; the shared `atlas/` was not written):** `causes` 33, `chain` 52, `history` 100, `atlas` 103, `atlas-texts` 37, `data-integrity` 41, `neutral` 20, `methods` 84, `label-sweep` 35, `alpha-fixes-b` 44, `sections` 52, `tree` 51, `conflicts` 38: all passed, 0 failed. In the shared tree only one check is red until the Atlas is rebuilt (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`): the one in `causes` that compares `atlas/causes.js` with the JSON.


## World-history timeline expansion: 207 new events, 293 in all (18 November 2026)

**What this is.** BACKLOG D6: the pilot of 86 events (`tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json`) is being grown to about 300 with balanced regions, every new event written from pages the writer opened and read (the pilot was written from memory; its second reading found 23 factual errors in 86 events). This section covers fifteen batches: the first six (**102 new events, 188 in all**) in the paragraphs below, then batches 7 to 15 (105 more, **293 in all**) under their own heading at its end (the conflict strand is not touched: the conflicts session owns `world_conflicts.json`). Each event's own source list says what was read (the `note` of each source). **Review state stays "unchecked": every new event has had a first reading only, by the person who wrote it; none has had a second reading and none has been checked by a specialist.** `world_meta.json` says so in the note the page prints (86 events with a second reading, the rest first reading only).

**How the pages were read.** English Wikipedia pages were read as plain text through the MediaWiki interface (one request every six seconds; reading stopped at the first 429, the house rule), each page in full or in the sections the event rests on, and the figures, dates and names in each summary were checked against that text; coordinates are the ones Wikipedia gives its pages. Where a page disagrees with itself the entry says so or keeps to the statement the page makes most carefully (below). Pages that open were also used for **records from the time and a scholarly abstract**: al-Bakri on Ghana (1068), al-Umari on Mansa Musa in Cairo (1324) and Leo Africanus on Timbuktu, as printed by Boston University's African Studies Center (extracts, not the books); Suetonius, *Life of the Deified Julius* 40, and Diodorus Siculus XII.36 (LacusCurtius); Augustine, *Confessions* VII.6 (New Advent); the abstract of Wilmshurst and others, PNAS 108 (2011), in Europe PMC. **Not opened (refused automated reading, 403 or 429):** Encyclopaedia Britannica, UNESCO's World Heritage List pages, the PNAS site, the Metropolitan Museum's essays and worldhistory.org; so no event rests on them. The books and papers that the Wikipedia pages cite (Levitzion and Hopkins, Olivelle, McClish, Breunig, Meulenbeld, Wynne-Jones and Laviolette and the rest) were **not opened**; only their statements as the pages give them.

**The batches.** *1 (25): Nubia and Africa south of the Sahara, the Andes, Mesoamerica and North America* — Kerma, the Twenty-fifth Dynasty, Meroë and the Meroitic script, Nok, Ghana, the trans-Saharan trade, Mansa Musa's pilgrimage, the Timbuktu manuscripts, Songhai, Kilwa, Mapungubwe, Kongo, Benin, Sokoto, the Berlin Conference, rinderpest; Caral, Chavín, Teotihuacan, Tiwanaku, Wari, Cahokia, Chaco Canyon, the Haudenosaunee Confederacy (one Wikipedia page each, two for the last; records from the time for Ghana, Mansa Musa, Songhai; the Swahili summary for Kilwa). *2 (11): South Asia* — the 4.2-kiloyear drought, the Rigveda, Pāṇini, the Maurya Empire, the Arthashastra, the Kushans, the two epics, Sangam literature, Aryabhata, the Ayurvedic compendia, Nalanda. *3 (24): East Asia and the Near East* — Erlitou, Sanxingdui, the Zhou, the Hundred Schools, the Terracotta Army, Sima Qian, the examinations, the Grand Canal, Bi Sheng, the *Wujing Zongyao*, the Jikji, Hangul, the Tokugawa, the Qing; the Hittites, Behistun, Lydian coinage, the Parthians, the Sasanians, the Umayyads, the Dome of the Rock, the Ottomans (two entries), the Safavids. *4 (20): South and Southeast Asia, Central Asia* — the Cholas, the Delhi Sultanate, Vijayanagara; Đông Sơn, Funan, Srivijaya, the Khmer Empire, Pagan, Borobudur, Majapahit, Malacca, Ayutthaya, Samalas (1257), Krakatoa (1883), the Dutch East India Company; Sintashta, the Scythians, the Xiongnu, the Orkhon inscriptions, Ulugh Beg's observatory. *5 (16): the nine calendar events of BACKLOG and the Pacific* — Meton (1 Wikipedia page and Diodorus), the Babylonian zodiac, Hipparchus and precession, the Julian reform (Suetonius), the *Tetrabiblos*, Nicaea and Easter, the Gregorian reform, the Hijra and the Hijri epoch, Augustine against astrology (the chapter itself); the Lapita expansion, East Polynesia (the 2011 abstract), Rapa Nui, Nan Madol, the First Fleet, the Treaty of Waitangi, Kamehameha. *6 (6): Egypt* — the Rhind and Edwin Smith papyri, the Amarna letters, Eratosthenes, the Fatimids, the Rosetta Stone, the Suez Canal. 163 of the 188 events rest on Wikipedia pages alone (grade C); the 25 with a second kind of source are the pilot's and the new ones named above.

**Narrowed or left out because it could not be supported from what was read.** Ghana: the king's tax on salt is recorded (al-Bakri) but a tax on gold was not read, so none is claimed. Kilwa: a *legend* of a Persian founder (the page's word), not "the chronicle". Rinderpest: "present in Abyssinia by 1888", not "brought by the Italian army" (one study finds no evidence for that). Nok, Rigveda, Pagan, Funan, the Xiongnu and others: dates kept as the ranges the pages give, with "c.", and the confidence L where the page itself hedges. The Gregorian reform: Britain's adoption (September 1752) and "the last European country" (1923, civil use only) are on the page read, but Greece (named in BACKLOG) and Russia's 1918 were not found in the text read, so the entry names no country for 1923 and leaves Russia out. The 1789 smallpox epidemic at Sydney was **left out**: the page found is a stub of 680 characters with no discussion of the origin, which is disputed. Qing: dated from 1644 (the capture of Beijing), the page's "traditionally considered the start", as a point, because the dynasty runs to 1912, past the 1900 end of the line (so does the Ottoman state, shown by its two phases).

**Left uncertain.** *Dome of the Rock:* the page gives the date of the inscription, 72 AH, as 691/2 CE in one place and 692–693 in another (72 AH is 691/2); the entry follows the page's statement that construction was undertaken in 691–692 and says accounts of when work began differ. *Borobudur:* the page calls it a 9th-century temple and also says it was built in the 8th, with stages from about 780; the entry gives c. 780–825 and says so. *Pagan:* the chronicle's fortification of the city in 849 against the founding by Anawrahta in the 1050s and 1060s; the entry starts at the 1050s. *Tiwanaku:* a statistical founding date of about 110 CE and a rise between 200 and 500; the entry runs c. 200–1000. *Haudenosaunee:* "about 1102, 1142 or 1190" on one page, "likely between 1142 and 1660" on another; the entry runs 1142–1660 and is disputed. *Chola:* dominance from 907 to 1215 by one count; others run later; the entry ends 1215 and says it is one count. *Meroitic script:* deciphered in 1909 or 1911 (both on the page): the entry says "the early 20th century". *Orkhon:* "early 8th century" on the page: c. 700–735. *Hittites:* the empire's end is "about 1200" (Suppiluliuma II's reign began about 1207 and ended with the collapse). *Eratosthenes:* dated to the 3rd century BCE (c. 250) from his life, not from a record of the measurement; reported, because his own work is lost. *The Hijri epoch:* the epoch (16 July 622) is a convention; the story of Umar's adoption in 638 is reported (later accounts).

**The line itself.** With 188 events (337 with religion and the conflicts) the page still reads at 1280 by 800 and at 375 by 812 (looked at in a private preview): at the whole span each row still shows at most five lanes and says how many more appear on zoom; zoomed in, the Empires and states row becomes taller because empires that overlap in time cannot be separated by zooming. One change in `6pw-history.js`: `histCap` (5 lanes, 6 for religion, at the whole span; 9 from a zoom of about 2, and up to 40 from about 5), with the history test's lane check changed to match. The strand buttons and "Go to…" still suffice; a **region filter** (Africa, the Americas, Asia, Europe, Oceania) would be the next thing to add if rows grow crowded (it needs a `region` field in the data: the counts below were made by hand and are not in the file).

**Counts after six batches (188 events; the first place of each event, assigned by hand).** Europe and the Mediterranean 32, Near East and Iran 28, East Asia 26, South Asia 18, Sub-Saharan Africa and Nubia 21, Americas 16, North Africa and Egypt 16, Southeast Asia 13, Oceania and Australia 8, Central Asia and the steppe 5, global 5. By strand: empires and states 72, ideas and arts 38, science and technology 33, trade and migration 21, disease and disaster 16, conflict 8 (the pilot's). By status: documented 162, disputed 17, reported 9. *(The pilot's own regions were assigned by a rule, the modern country of its first place, so they differ by a few from the second reading's counts above.)* Still thin: Europe after 1500 and before 500 CE beyond the pilot, the Americas after contact, Oceania before 1000 CE and after 1840, the Islamic world's science after the translations (the Maragha observatory, Ibn al-Haytham, Avicenna), disease and disaster from China and India, and Africa's north and east between 1000 and 1800.

### Batches 7 to 15: 105 more events, 293 in all (18 November 2026, first reading only)

**What was added.** *7 (18): the Americas and two African entries* — Poverty Point, the Long Count (the earliest inscriptions and their doubt), Nazca, the Hopewell exchange, Monte Albán, Igbo-Ukwu (with the page on Thurstan Shaw), Lalibela, L’Anse aux Meadows, the Dresden Codex, khipu, Machu Picchu, the Popol Vuh, Tordesillas, Potosí, the Manila galleons, the Declaration of Independence (the Wikipedia page and the **transcription at the National Archives, read in full**), Haiti’s independence, Spanish America. *8 (13): the Islamic world and North Africa* — the Garamantes, Sundiata, al-Khwarizmi, Avicenna’s Canon, al-Azhar, the Jalali calendar, al-Idrisi’s atlas, the Toledo translators, Córdoba, the Almohads, Maragha, the Mamluks, the Muqaddimah. *9 (10): disease and disaster* — the Plague of Cyprian, the volcanic winter of 536, the Great Famine of 1315, the Little Ice Age, the Shaanxi earthquake of 1556, the Bengal famine of 1770, the Indian and Chinese famines of 1876–79, the first cholera pandemic, the third plague pandemic. *10 (11): India and China* — the Nine Chapters, Zhang Heng, the Marathas, the Taj Mahal, the Jantar Mantar, the Ming, the Forbidden City, Su Song’s clock, Zhu Xi, the Treaty of Nanjing, the Dunhuang manuscripts. *11 (12): Europe before the Empire* — Stonehenge, the Minoans, Mycenae, the Etruscans, the Olympic Games, Greek colonisation, the Twelve Tables, the Roman Republic, the Parthenon, the Hippocratic Corpus, the Hellenistic kingdoms, Pytheas. *12 (10): Europe from Constantine to the telegraph* — Constantinople, Hagia Sophia, Anno Domini, Charlemagne, the Hansa, the Magellan–Elcano voyage, the Sidereus Nuncius, the Napoleonic Code, germ theory, the telegraph. *13 (9): Central Asia, the Pacific and the Philippines* — the Bactria–Margiana complex, Pazyryk, the Sogdian merchants, the First Turkic Khaganate, the Timurids, Siberia, Macassan trepangers, the Tuʻi Tonga, the Laguna Copperplate. *14 (12)* — Madagascar, Kanem–Bornu, the Asante, sleeping sickness, cocoliztli, Brazil, the Louisiana Purchase, the East India Company, the Silk Road, the Book of Optics (its date, 1011–1021, is on the Ibn al-Haytham page), the periodic table, the abolition of slavery. *15 (10)* — the Shahnameh, Marco Polo, Ibn Battuta, Müteferrika’s press, Rangaku, the Convention of Kanagawa, the Yellow River flood of 1887, Tupaia’s
