chart, Cook’s voyages, the Sydney smallpox of 1789 (from the longer article “Smallpox in Australia”; the stub page first tried was not used). 267 of the 293 events rest on Wikipedia pages alone (grade C); the same hosts as before were used (the National Archives was added to the test’s list), and the same ones refused automated reading.

**Narrowed or left uncertain in these batches.** *Long Count:* the earliest contemporaneous inscription is read as 36 BCE “although this date is not certain”, and Takalik Abaj Stela 2 may be earlier but is controversial: the entry says both. *Lalibela:* the churches are traditionally dated to King Lalibela’s reign (about 1181–1221) but the page says it is more likely that they reached their form in several phases from the 7th to the 13th century: marked disputed. *Madagascar:* the earliest unambiguous evidence is 490 CE and earlier dates have been claimed: disputed. *Kanem–Bornu:* “believed to have been founded around 700” with later and earlier dates proposed. *Cocoliztli:* the cause (Salmonella enterica Paratyphi C against an indigenous haemorrhagic fever) is debated. *Bengal 1770:* seven to ten million dead against at least one million. *India 1876–78:* the page’s range, 5.6 to 9.6 million; it also says that grain export continued and that relief spending was criticised at the time, and the entry says no more than that. *Yellow River 1887:* “at least 930,000”, from a short page that gives no range (higher estimates exist and were not read). *Reported, not documented:* the Twelve Tables, the Roman Republic’s start (509), the Olympic Games (776), Pytheas and Eratosthenes (lost works), Marco Polo (dictated to Rustichello), Zhang Heng’s seismoscope (“he claimed”), the Epic of Sundiata (oral tradition). *The Tuʻi Tonga “empire”* is a description sometimes given, and whether it fits is uncertain: marked disputed. *Hippocratic Corpus:* the entry says only that the works are “closely associated with” Hippocrates; who wrote which was not examined. *Left out:* the Great Ethiopian Famine of 1888–92 (no page found under the title tried), the Mutapa state (no dates found in the text read) and Chichén Itzá (no specifics verified).

**The line with 293.** Looked at again in a private preview at 1280 by 800 and 375 by 812 with 293 events (459 with religion and the conflicts): the whole span is as calm as before (rows of at most five lanes saying how many more appear on zoom). One further change in `histCap`: the **religion row stays at six lanes at every zoom**, because its long “adopted by a ruler or state” bars made it a wall of twelve lanes or more when zoomed; the world’s rows lift as before (nine lanes from a zoom of about 2, up to 40 from about 5), which is what makes the overlapping empires reachable.

**Counts after fifteen batches (293 events; first place of each, by hand).** *By region:* Europe and the Mediterranean 57, East Asia 40, Near East and Iran 36, Americas 33, Sub-Saharan Africa and Nubia 28, South Asia 24, North Africa and Egypt 23, Southeast Asia 14, Oceania and Australia 13, Central Asia and the steppe 11, global 14 (entries that belong to no one place, such as the drought of 2200 BCE, the Little Ice Age, the volcanic winter of 536, the Silk Road, the two East India companies, the Magellan voyage and abolition). *By strand:* empires and states 99, ideas and arts 62, science and technology 57, trade and migration 37, disease and disaster 30, conflict 8 (the pilot’s; the conflicts file adds its own). *By era (year of beginning):* 3500 to 1000 BCE 38, 999 to 1 BCE 57, 1 to 500 CE 22, 501 to 1000 CE 41, 1001 to 1500 CE 64, 1501 to 1900 CE 71. *By status:* documented 249, disputed 27, reported 17. **Still thin:** the first five centuries CE (22 events), Central Asia (11), Oceania before 1000 CE, and North America north of Mexico after 1500 (only 1776 and the Louisiana Purchase).


## Conflicts and peacemaking (BACKLOG D8), second window: 86 entries (18 November 2026)

**What was added.** `tools/atlas_src/json/world_conflicts.json` grew from 8 to **86 entries**: 43 wars, 10 between communities (schism, persecution, communal conflict), 33 settlements. By region (an entry can touch more than one): Asia 29 (33.7%), Europe 25, Africa 22 (25.6%), North America 18 and South America 5 (the Americas together 23, 26.7%). By era band of the start year: before 500 BCE 2, 500 BCE to 500 CE 8, 500 to 1000 9, 1000 to 1500 7, 1500 to 1700 23, 1700 to 1900 15, 1900 to 1945 4, after 1945 18. Causes: every entry has a political cause, 82 a religious one, 43 an economic one, 13 a "mixed"; of all 224 cause notes 57 are documented, 118 reported or claimed and 49 disputed; of the 82 religious causes 26 are documented, 39 reported and 17 disputed. 207 parties; 176 sources.

**Within living memory (18 entries, the maker should read these first):** Partition of India (1947), Liaquat–Nehru Pact (1950), La Violencia in Colombia, Nigerian Civil War, the Troubles, Addis Ababa Agreement (1972), Lebanese Civil War, Taif Agreement, Algerian Civil War, Second Sudanese Civil War, Bosnian War, Dayton Agreement, Chapultepec Peace Accords (1992), Rome General Peace Accords (1992), Sant'Egidio Platform (1995), Good Friday Agreement, South African Truth and Reconciliation Commission, and the Comprehensive Peace Agreement (2005). Every statement in them carries its own label.

**What was opened, and how.** English Wikipedia through the MediaWiki interface, one request per article, eleven seconds apart (the first window met one HTTP 429 at six and a half seconds), the text kept only in the session scratchpad: **131 different articles** are cited by the 78 entries added after the first eight (their titles are in each entry's source list), and one Portuguese article (Guerra do Contestado, for the Contestado War: its passages tagged "needs sources" were not used). **Primary texts opened in full or in the part cited:** Lactantius (edict of Galerius) and Augustine, Letter 185 (New Advent); Urban II at Clermont according to Fulcher (Fordham); the Maryland Toleration Act (Avalon); the Good Friday Agreement (CAIN) and Sutton's index of deaths (CAIN, a dataset). **Not opened:** Britannica, Iranica, World History Encyclopedia and sacred-texts.com all refuse automated reading (HTTP 403), so none is cited; and none of the books, chronicles, treaties and reports the Wikipedia articles name, among them 1 and 2 Maccabees and Josephus, the text of the Cyrus Cylinder, Ibn Hisham, the texts of the Baqt (no copy survives), the Edict of Torda, the Boxer Protocol, the Chapultepec Accords and the South African commission's report, the Gersony report, Peires's book on the cattle-killing, the Church Missionary Society papers, and the historians' books named under Taiping, Pontiac, Haiti, Salem and Algeria. **73 of the 86 entries rest on Wikipedia alone** (grade C); each says so in its source notes, and a statement is "documented" only where the article gives it without attribution (a date, a signing, a clause) and "reported or claimed" where the article attributes it.

**Choices a second reader should look at.** (1) The three entries on the first Muslim community (Constitution of Medina, Treaty of al-Hudaybiya, early Muslim conquests) follow the traditional account as the articles give it; the Constitution is marked disputed in its date, its form and which Jewish groups it names, and the 627 events are given as the article gives them with its note that Muslim and Western historians differ. (2) The Cyrus Cylinder and the Maccabean Revolt are written around the scholars' disagreement, each reading labelled disputed (Honigman, Schwartz and Ma for the Maccabees; Kuhrt, Bedford and Walker for the Cylinder). (3) The Haitian entry says the Bois Caïman ceremony is reported and that the sources read do not say how it is evidenced. (4) The Algerian entry gives the attacks on villages as claimed by the GIA and says its responsibility is disputed, with the army-barracks reports given as the article gives them. (5) Death tolls are ranges only where an article gives two ends (Taiping 20 million to 100 million; Algeria about 44,000 to 200,000; Nigeria 500,000 to 3 million; Contestado 10,000 to 20,000; Maji Maji 75,000 to 300,000); otherwise "Not recorded as a range" and the figures as given. (6) The Cape cattle-killing is filed as between communities, which fits it loosely; the Boxer, Taiping and Haiti entries use the article's figures for the killing of Christians, missionaries and colonists with the same labels as the rest. (7) Words the page does not use in its own voice (massacre, martyr, victim, perpetrator, invader, regime, slaughter, genocide, glorious) are paraphrased even where an article or a name uses them: the Buganda entry cites the article on the converts executed there without its title, the Salem and Haiti entries say "killed" and "hanged", and the English revolution of 1688 is not called by its common name.

**Left out, for a specialist or because no source could be opened:** the Armenian question, the Holocaust, the Israeli–Palestinian conflict and the 1948 war (the Avalon text of Israel's declaration is readable; the Arab League's cable could not be found), the Rohingya, Gujarat in 2002, Kashmir, the Mughal–Sikh wars (the one article read is written from the Sikh side; no neutral article was found by title), the Sri Lankan and Iran–Iraq wars (the articles were read; religion's part in them is thin in the text and the sources were too long to read fairly), the Reconquista, the Northern Crusades, the English civil wars, the Greco-Persian, Peloponnesian and Punic wars (timeline entries only), and the Buganda wars after 1892.

**Changed in shared files (small edits):** `tests/history.test.js` (the host list gained pt.wikipedia.org; a conflict-strand event may end as late as 1950, for the Caste War of Yucatán, 1847 to 1915); `src/js/6pw-history.js` (one expression: a bar on the timeline is cut at 1900). The History timeline still draws only events that start by 1900.

**Tests (private clone refreshed from the shared tree, Atlas rebuilt there; the shared `atlas/` was not written):** `conflicts` 38 (the guard on 23 broken entries; a quarter each from Asia, Africa and the Americas; every era band), `history` 80, `sections` 45, `neutral` 20, `atlas` 103, `data-integrity` 41, `tradlist` 67, `chain` 52, `tree` 46, `causes` 30, `stories` 43: all passed, 0 failed. Page checked in the in-app browser at 1280 by 800 and 375 by 812: the list (86 entries, counts beside each kind, Africa 22 of 86, Settlement and Asia 12 of 86), a war and a settlement opened as entries, no horizontal scroll.

## High-control groups: first reading against sources (22 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A child session of the project manager (not the session that wrote the cards) read the 156 claims of the 13 cards, and the seven “not yet written up” lines, against pages it could open. It worked read only, one card at a time, through the project’s web-fetch tool, a web search (to find stable addresses) and, once, the in-app browser (to look at the finished page on a private build at 1024 and 375 pixels wide: no sideways scroll; two console messages, “An unknown error occurred when fetching the script”, were not traced and are not from the page code). Nothing was downloaded or saved into the project. For each card the claims were set out as numbered questions and each answer was asked for as *confirmed*, *contradicted* or *not in the page*, with the figure or words quoted. Court opinions, official reports and the groups’ own pages were tried first; where a court or official site refused the tool, an encyclopedia page that cites it was read instead (English Wikipedia, through its API: the plain text of the article, or the list of its reference links to find a primary page). Some 200 page requests were made in all, a number of them refused (below).

**What the marks mean (they are in the data, `r` and `fx`, and on the page).** *Found*: everything the claim says was found in a page that was opened. *Found in part*: some of it was found and some not; the text is kept as written. *Not found*: the pages read did not say it; the text is kept as written, so that nothing is removed. *Corrected after reading*: the text was changed to what the page says (a figure, a date, a name or a title was wrong, or the claim said more than the page does). A claim with no mark was not read. A card is marked *checked* only when every claim on it is *found*; none is, so all 13 still say **Under review**, now with one line each that says how far the reading got: for example “Read against sources on 22 November 2026: 12 of 12 claims read: 10 found, 0 found in part, 2 not found; 6 corrected after reading; 0 not yet read.” The page says the same once for all 13 cards (147 of 156 claims read: **98 found, 37 found in part, 12 not found; 60 corrected after reading; 9 not read**), explains the marks inside “How this page works”, and says that the books the claims name were not re-read and that several court sites refused the reading tool. The seven lines under “Not yet written up” carry the same marks (four found, three found in part with what was not found) and say they were read on 22 November 2026.

**What was and was not a primary source.** *Opened and read as text:* the opinions *United States v. Heldt*, 668 F.2d 1238 (D.C. Cir. 1981; the head matter and the end word for word, the middle only as the tool’s summary), *United States v. Layton*, 855 F.2d 1388 (9th Cir. 1988; the same), *United States v. Moon*, 718 F.2d 1210 (2d Cir. 1983; the first quarter of the opinion and the end), *In re Texas Department of Family and Protective Services*, 255 S.W.3d 613 (Tex. 2008) and *State v. Jeffs*, 2010 UT 49 (all through the Caselaw Access Project’s texts at `static.case.law`; *United States v. Branch*, 91 F.3d 699, only citation lists and a summary); the press summary of *R (Hodkin)*, [2013] UKSC 77 (National Archives case law); the Commons written answer of 16 July 1980 and the Lords’ debate of 8 August 1980 (historic Hansard); the Foster Report (1971; chapters 1, 2, 6, 8 and 9 in full, the rest in part) and the Anderson Report (1965; its recommendations and conclusion) through Carnegie Mellon’s mirror; the US Senate staff statement on Aum (31 October 1995; index and parts 2 to 4); the Jonestown Institute’s pages on the deaths, the children, the House staff report (foreword and findings), its “About” page and its FAQ; Scientology’s own site (home page, “What is Scientology?”, the FAQ index and the pages on auditing, the E-Meter, Clear, Operating Thetan, reincarnation, disconnection, church funding, the billion-year pledge, court cases and opposition); heavensgate.com (home page, “Our Position Against Suicide”, the exit list, two pages of its book); the Twelve Tribes’ home, “Who We Are” and child-discipline pages; the Family’s sites; 1983 newspaper reports of the High Court decision (copies on suburbia.net); a legal commentary on the French decisions (Le Petit Juriste); Deseret News (29 March and 5 April 1997), the Irish Times, the Spokesman-Review and Encyclopedia.com on Heaven’s Gate; CESNUR’s page on the English case; two press-release or excerpt copies of the Danforth report (culteducation.com). *Read through English Wikipedia:* everything else; the page names are in the tables (`W:` before a title). A claim that rests on Wikipedia alone is *found*, but it has been read at second hand, and the tables say which those are.

**Pages that refused or failed (do not retry).** Refused (403 or similar): law.justia.com, austlii.edu.au, bailii.org, jurist.org, slate.fr, ucanews.com, sacred-texts.com, the Oregon Encyclopedia, moj.go.jp (the Public Security Intelligence Agency), japaneselawtranslation.go.jp, rael.org, apnews.com, japantoday.com, nytimes.com, latimes.com, cnn.com, chicagotribune.com, tollbit (Rolling Stone); crimelibrary.org (404); the St. Petersburg Times pages on Lisa McPherson (404); web.archive.org (the fetch tool refuses it); swarb.co.uk (certificate expired); courtlistener.com’s citation lookup (300), hcourt.gov.au (a redirect to an index); justice.gov (200 with no text: the Waco report and the NXIVM releases); PubMed (a cookie wall); the childabuseroyalcommission.gov.au case-study page (timed out twice). Over the tool’s size limit or returned as an unreadable PDF: the 1984 *Armstrong* decision (archive.org), the New West article of 1977 (jonestown.sdsu.edu PDF) and the High Court’s text of 1983 (a PDF copy). Where the tool only summarised a long page, the note says so.

**The old “Check first” table, answered.** *Scientology:* the French appeals are 2 February 2012 (Paris Court of Appeal, upholding) and 16 October 2013 (the Court of Cassation, rejecting the last appeals): added, with the fines (€400,000 and €200,000). Operation Snow White: nine appellants in the Court of Appeals’ opinion (decided 2 October 1981), eleven convicted in all (two more in 1980) on Wikipedia’s count; the card said “several”. Lisa McPherson: 17 days and 5 December 1995 stand; the finding of a blood clot after dehydration and immobility was the medical examiner’s *first* finding (manner “undetermined”), later changed in 2000 to “accident” with the dehydration wording dropped (one reference page only; its two dates for the change differ). Victoria: the Psychological Practices Act 1965; the references to Scientology were removed in 1982 and the Act repealed in 1987, not “repealed in 1982”. The British bar was announced on 25 July 1968 and lifted by the Home Secretary on 16 July 1980 (Hansard); it covered foreigners coming to work or study. Judge Breckenridge’s decision of 1984: **not read**. The census claim: England and Wales 2,418 (2011) and Australia 1,655 (2021) found; Canada was not found and is dropped. *Peoples Temple:* 918 = 909 + 5 + 4 is confirmed (the four in Georgetown: Sharon Amos, two of her children and her older daughter); “about a third” children is 304 of 909; Layton’s conviction was for aiding and abetting the killing of Ryan (life) and, by two sources, conspiracy; the Ninth Circuit affirmed on 16 August 1988; the church was founded earlier than the card said (a student pastor in 1952, his own church in 1954, renamed in 1955). *Heaven’s Gate:* 21 women and 18 men, 26 to 72 (an early report said 20 to 72) stand; the number castrated is “several” (Los Angeles Times), seven (Encyclopedia.com) or eight (Wikipedia), not “eight reported by the medical examiner”; the medical examiner’s ruling of suicide was **not found** (the first report did not state a cause of death). *Aum:* 13 death sentences, the executions of 6 and 26 July 2018 and eight dead at Matsumoto stand; Tokyo is 13 or 14 dead on the pages; Aleph “apologised and set up a compensation fund” (the acknowledgement and payment were not found); Hikari no Wa’s founding by a former spokesman stands; the 1999 Act could not be confirmed (its pages refused the tool). *Branch Davidians:* 76 dead and nine survivors, “more than eighty” (86 with the agents) stand; the Danforth conclusions are confirmed from a press-release copy; the 1993 Treasury and Justice Department reports could not be read; the 1994 verdicts were five of voluntary manslaughter and eight of firearms offences, and the Fifth Circuit vacated the machine-gun sentences; “girls under seventeen” was **not found**. *Solar Temple:* 74 = 48 + 5 + 16 + 5 stands, with 23 at Cheiry (night of 2 to 3 October 1994) and 25 at Granges-sur-Salvan; the Quebec family and their son are named on the page; court outcomes found on one reference page (a symbolic sentence in Canada in 1993; acquittals in France). *NXIVM:* the seven counts, the pleas and the sentences are found (the justice.gov pages returned no text; the counts are as a web-search summary of the release gives them); “about 2015” for DOS stands; the appeal, reported as decided on 9 December 2022, rests on Wikipedia alone. *Family International:* the judge was **Lord Justice Ward**, and he also found that by 1995 the Family had ended the practices (added); the end of the communal Homes about 2010 was **not found**. *Rajneesh:* 751 and 45 stand; Sheela’s pleas were Alford pleas of 22 July 1986 to poisoning-plot and attempted-murder charges, and the plot against the US Attorney is not among them on the pages (it was the subject of a Swiss conviction in 1999); the plea of Rajneesh followed his arrest of October 1985 (he first pleaded not guilty on 8 November). *Unification Church:* the conviction (verdict 18 May 1982; 18 months and a $25,000 fine; affirmed 13 September 1983) is read in the opinion; the 13 months served is Wikipedia’s; Barker’s figures were **not found**; the Tokyo order of 25 March 2025 is confirmed and its appeals are reported as upheld on 4 March 2026 (Tokyo High Court) and 22 June 2026 (Supreme Court of Japan) by two Wikipedia articles. *Raëlism:* birth year 1946 and the meeting of 13 December 1973 stand (another article gives 3 December); the movement began in the 1970s, the book of 1974, MADECH in December 1974 and the Raëlian Movement in February 1976. *Twelve Tribes:* Chattanooga 1972 and the 1984 raid (112 children) stand, but the cases were dismissed because the search warrant was unconstitutional, and in 2013 some of the children were not returned (the European Court of Human Rights upheld the German action in 2018, on one reference page). *FLDS:* in Utah the sentence was two consecutive terms of five years to life (not “ten years to life”) and the reversal was for erroneous consent instructions; the Texas Supreme Court’s own words are “removal of the children was not warranted” and its number is 468 children; the dates of the Texas verdict (4 August) and sentence (9 August) were not confirmed (two pages give 9 August for both).

**What the maker or the project manager should decide.** (1) **No card is marked “checked”**, not even Aum (all 12 claims read and found): most claims were read in an encyclopedia page that cites the source the card names, not in that source, and the named books were not opened. The wording “checked” is the maker’s to give. (2) **Claims that rest on Wikipedia alone** include several about living people or recent court results: Layton’s counts (with the opinion’s own “Count 2” and “life”), the Second Circuit’s decision on Raniere and Bronfman (9 December 2022), the two Japanese appeal results (4 March and 22 June 2026), Allison Mack’s and Nancy Salzman’s sentences, Sheela’s pleas and sentence, and the Bavarian outcome of 2013. The page labels them *found* because Wikipedia says so; a second reader should open the courts’ and news sources it cites. (3) **Larry Layton’s release on parole in April 2002** is on Wikipedia (the article on Leo Ryan) and was not added to the card, because the card’s claim about him is a court record and no record of the parole was read; adding it would make the card fairer to a living man. (4) **The “not found” claims were kept as written** and labelled; the most consequential are the medical examiner’s ruling of suicide at Heaven’s Gate, the Treasury and Danforth reports’ sworn statements about girls at Mount Carmel, the court’s finding that the Unification Church’s solicitation was “illegal and harmful”, and the Church of Scientology’s account of Operation Snow White. If the maker prefers that an unconfirmed claim about a living person or a recent court finding be taken off the page until it is found, these are the ones. (5) **The claims about what a group says of itself** were read on the group’s own pages where they opened; the Church of Scientology’s, the Raëlian Movement’s (refused) and the Family’s (the 2010 change) were only partly found. (6) The header lines of the cards (`what`, `where`, `founder`, `also`) were not claims and were not read; Heaven’s Gate’s “Begun in Texas” in particular was not looked for.

**Claims not read, highest value first.** (1) Scientology 12, the 1984 *Armstrong* decision (a record claim naming a living person; the scan is over the tool’s limit, and a plain-text copy is needed). (2) Branch Davidians 10 and 11, the survivors’ account and the scholars’ view (a record of what the group and the scholars say). (3) NXIVM 3 and 4, the size and the group’s own description. (4) Unification Church 5 and 8, the church’s own description and the former members’ account. (5) Rajneesh 3, the size. (6) Raëlism 2, the present state. For the claims *found in part* and *not found*, the tables below say what is missing.

**Claim by claim.** `W:` is the English Wikipedia article of that title, read as plain text through its API (the opening or the whole article); `case.law` is the Caselaw Access Project’s text of the opinion; “Institute” is the Jonestown Institute. “Corrected” means the text was changed; the note says what it said before (“Was:”).

## World history timeline, second reading of batch 1 (22 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (a child session of the project manager, not the session that wrote the events) went through 99 events of `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json`: the 73 of the strand *empires and states* and the 26 of *trade and migration* that were added after the pilot and had a first reading only (88 documented, 7 disputed, 4 reported; the ids are listed at the end). The other 108 first-reading-only events (ideas and arts, science and technology, disease and disaster) were left for a later reading. *Method.* (1) The 100 Wikipedia citations (99 distinct pages) were looked up through the MediaWiki interface (`redirects=1`, the disambiguation property): all exist, none is a disambiguation page, six were redirects (fixed, below). The five other pages the entries cite (four of the Boston University African Studies Center, one article at PubMed Central) were opened. (2) The plain text of every cited Wikipedia page, as it stood on 22 November 2026, was read through the same interface, one request every three seconds (a faster pass was answered with “too many requests” and slowed down, see the end). A script listed, for each summary, every number and every capitalised name that is not on its page: 17 events were flagged, most of them only because a full stop followed a number; the others (the Hittite “1200”, East Polynesia, the Haudenosaunee dates, Kilwa and Portugal, Songhai, Tordesillas, Vijayanagara) were read one by one. Every sentence of every summary was then read against its page for numbers, who, where, “first”, “largest”, tense and cause. (3) The places: the coordinates of 75 place names were compared with those of the Wikipedia page of the same name (all within 10 km, except four region markers: Eurasia, Siberia, Madagascar and the Hawaiian Islands); the other names, regions and sites with no page of that name, were judged from known positions, and the ISO country of every place was read; four pins were moved. (4) A second source outside Wikipedia was then looked for where an event is disputed or reported, is dated 1000 BCE or earlier, or where its page was thin or disagreed with its own lead: **11 events** against a page that was opened and read (the four Boston University pages and the PubMed Central article that the entries already cite, for Kilwa, Ghana, Mansa Musa, Songhai and East Polynesia; Burley and others for the Lapita; Bakewell for Potosí; and four pages that are not linked, below), and **14 more** against a search result only, marked *(search result)* (a search summary is not a page read). (5) The wording was read against the neutrality rule (`tests/history.test.js` pins the list of loaded words), and the links to traditions were checked against the Atlas rows and their own dates (a tradition row whose years do not overlap the event is not a fit). No date was checked against Wikidata (not fetched; waiting for the maker’s go-ahead).

**Counts.** 99 events read (73 states, 26 trade). **39 changed**: 12 corrected (a statement, a date, a place or a link was wrong, unsupported or one-sided), 22 made more exact or more even-handed without having been wrong, 5 with only a source address or title fixed. **No status was changed** (88 documented, 7 disputed, 4 reported, before and after); **one date confidence was** (the Parthian Empire, H to M); two links to traditions changed (Hopewell, L’Anse aux Meadows); four events had a pin moved (Haojing, Huari, Nan Madol, Funan, where Óc Eo was added as a second place). No event was added or removed and no year moved, so the file is still in date order. 60 events were read and left as they were. Two sources were added (Burley and others 2015 for the Lapita; Bakewell’s article on Potosí in Encyclopedia.com); five Wikipedia addresses now name the page itself (the sixth redirect, L’Anse aux Meadows, differed only in the apostrophe). Of the 99 events, 92 still rest on Wikipedia pages alone (grade C), against 94 before: the checks above are only as good as that summary plus the second sources named here.

### Corrected (12)

| Event id | What was wrong | What it says now | What settled it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `bmac-oxus-civilization` | “Its farming includes crops typical of irrigation in an arid land, such as hexaploid bread wheat”: the page says this of the late Neolithic site of Chagylly Depe, before the complex, not of its people. The dates were one reading only. | “Its people practise irrigation farming of wheat and barley”; “(other authors start it about 2300 or 2200 BCE)”. | Wikipedia, *Bactria–Margiana Archaeological Complex*, “Agriculture and economy” (“practised irrigation farming of wheat and barley”). Starts of 2300 and 2200: Lamberg-Karlovsky (2013, 2200–1700) and Hiebert (1994, 2200–1750) *(search result)*. |
| `kilwa-swahili-coast` | “The sultanate dates from about 960 to 1000” reads as a rule of forty years; “has authority at its height along the whole coast” left out the page’s own caution. | “The sultanate’s history begins about 960 to 1000”; “holds or claims authority at its height along much of the coast”. | Wikipedia, *Kilwa Sultanate* (“The history of the Kilwa Sultanate begins around 960–1000”; it “owned or claimed overlordship” of the cities it names; the writ of a later emir “probably only covered the city of Kilwa itself”). The Boston University page (Wynne-Jones and Laviolette 2018) confirms the 11th-century coral-stone towns and the links to Arabia, India and China. |

The address had a curly apostrophe (a redirect). | `kushan-empire` | “Its rulers … issue inscriptions in Sanskrit”: the page says that Sanskrit inscriptions in the Brahmi script, such as the Mathura statue of Vima Kadphises, *refer to* the emperor as Kushana; it does not say the rulers issued them. | “Its rulers put the name Koshano on their coins in Greek letters, and inscriptions in Sanskrit in the Brahmi script call the emperor Kushana.” | Wikipedia, *Kushan Empire* (the sentence on the dynastic name and the Mathura inscription). |
| `hopewell-exchange` | Linked to “mississippian” (Mississippian religion, 800–1600 CE), a later culture; the Atlas has its own row, “hopewell” (−100 to 500). | Linked to “hopewell”. | The Atlas rows (`traditions.json`); the page’s own dates (100 BCE to 500 CE). |
| `louisiana-purchase` | Left out what the page says about what was bought: “France only controlled a small fraction of this area, most of which was inhabited by Native Americans; effectively … the United States bought the preemptive right to obtain Indian lands by treaty or by conquest”. | Says so, in the summary’s own words (the summary was shortened elsewhere to keep it under the build’s 520 characters). | Wikipedia, *Louisiana Purchase* (lead and “Native Americans” passages). |
| `haudenosaunee-confederacy` | Gave only the dates drawn from oral history and the eclipse (1102, 1142, 1190, and “likely between 1142 and 1660”) and left out the date the same page gives for the archaeologists. | Adds that 1142 is read from a solar eclipse and that “many archaeologists and anthropologists favour about 1450”. | Wikipedia, *Haudenosaunee* (“Many archaeologists and anthropologists believe that the League was formed about 1450, though arguments have been made for dates as early as the solar eclipse of August 31, 1142”); *Great Law of Peace* (about 1102; about 1190). Mann and Fields (1997, the eclipse) and Henige’s criticism (1999), and a mid- to late 15th-century view, *(search result)*; the eScholarship page refused. |
| `macassan-trepang-trade` | “Begin to visit … about the middle of the 18th century” is the page’s lead; the page’s own section says the fleets came “from at least 1720 and possibly earlier”. The address was a redirect. | Keeps the middle of the 18th century and adds “earlier starts are argued, about 1720 and the mid-1600s or earlier from rock art”. | Wikipedia, *Makassan contact with Australia*, “Voyage to Marege’ and Kayu Jawa” (Macknight: about 1720, the earliest recorded voyage 1751; a Sulawesi historian about 1640; Arnhem Land rock art dated in 2010 to the mid-1600s; some scholars 1500s). Clark and May, introduction to *Macassan History and Heritage* (ANU Press 2013, opened): Macknight first 1650–1750, later 1750s–1780s; “an important and controversial debate”. |
| `first-fleet-sydney` | “Among them the Cadigal at Botany Bay”: the page puts the Cadigal “of the Botany Bay area”, but the people who knew Botany Bay as Kamay were the Gweagal, and the Cadigal (Gadigal) are the people of the Sydney region. | “Among them the Cadigal.” (the clause about the bay is cut, not replaced) | Search results only (State Library of New South Wales; *History of Australia (1788–1850)*) *(search result)*; the State Library page itself returned no text. |
| `majapahit` | “It collapses in 1527”: one end date. | “Dates for its end range from 1478 to 1527, the later after an invasion by the Sultanate of Demak.” | Wikipedia, *Majapahit* (“Dates for the end of the Majapahit Empire range from 1478 … to 1527”). |
| `nan-madol` | The Saudeleur dynasty and Isokelekel’s overthrow of it (about 1628) were stated as events; the page’s own account of the dynasty is Pohnpeian legend. The pin was 14 km from the site. | “By Pohnpeian tradition it is the capital of the Saudeleur dynasty until about 1628, when Isokelekel overthrows the Saudeleurs.” Pin moved to the site (6.842, 158.332). | Wikipedia, *Nan Madol* (“Pohnpeian legend”; “Pohnpeian tradition claims”) and its coordinates. The UNESCO nomination dating the fall “about 1600” from oral tradition, and the National Park Service’s 1628, *(search result)*. |
| `etruscan-civilization` | “In the 4th century BCE they succumb to the expanding Rome.” | “From the late 4th century BCE.” | Wikipedia, *Etruscan civilization* (“Beginning in the late 4th century BC, it succumbed to the expanding Rome”). |

### Made more exact or more even-handed (22)

* `hittite-empire`: “collapses about 1200 BCE” is now “ends between about 1200 and 1180 BCE … with its capital Hattusa gradually abandoned” (the page: abandoned “around 1190 BC”; Hattusa about 1200 and about 1180 *(search result)*).
* `erlitou-culture`: “has been disputed” now says who: “some Western scholars argue the link is premature, as no contemporary written records have been found” (the page). A dispute between Chinese and overseas scholars is also named in a review *(search result)*.
* `mycenaean-greece`: “about 1750 to 1050” kept, with “or from about 1700 or 1675 by the page’s own table of the Late Helladic period”. Livius (opened) gives about 1600 to 1050 for the same phase.
* `lapita-expansion`: adds the first Lapita landfall in Tonga, at Nukuleka, “at about 890 BCE” by uranium–thorium dating (2838 ± 8 calendar years before 1950); the page’s “around 1000 BCE” for Fiji kept. New source: Burley, Edinborough, Weisler and Zhao, PLoS ONE 10 (2015), PMC4370570 (abstract and the section on Nukuleka read).
* `zhou-dynasty`: “about 1046 BCE (some scholars give 1045)” (the page: Xia–Shang–Zhou Chronology Project and Pankenier, after Nivison and Shaughnessy 1045; the project’s own date, 20 January 1046 BCE, and Shaughnessy’s 1045 *(search result)*). The pin for Haojing was 21 km east of the site (it stood in the centre of Xi’an); it now stands at Fenghao (34.21, 108.72), still marked approximate.
* `lydian-coinage`: “one view places them at or near the start of King Alyattes’s reign” is the page’s “most common opinion”; the page’s range for the first coins, 700 to 550 BCE, added. (Kroll, in the Sardis Expedition essay, opened: ninety-three electrum coins under the Artemision at Ephesus, from the later 7th century BCE; Alyattes about 610–560 BCE: not linked, see below.)
* `tui-tonga-empire`: “whether ‘empire’ fits is uncertain” now says who differs: some academics prefer “maritime chiefdom”, others call “empire” the most convenient term (the page, “Modern scholarship”).
* `mapungubwe`: “what caused this is unknown” kept, with the proposals the page names (land degradation amid drought, Voigt; a shift of trade to Kilwa and Mogadishu, Killick and Ehret).
* `khmer-empire`: “ends in 1431” is now “its end is traditionally marked by the fall of Angkor … in 1431” (the page).
* `parthian-empire`: adds that some scholars place the conquest of Parthia about 238 BCE (the page: Curtis and Brosius against Garthwaite’s 247); **date confidence H to M**.
* `teotihuacan`: adds that the page also gives 100,000 to 200,000 for about 400–500 CE (Millon’s 100,000–200,000 and Cowgill’s 85,000–125,000 for its height are in the search results, not used).
* `wari-empire`: “dated to about 1100” is now “its end is dated to about 1000 or 1100” *(search result: the Middle Horizon ends about 1000; the city depopulated by 1000)*; the pin for Huari, 11 km off (it stood in the city of Ayacucho), now at the site (−13.06, −74.20).
* `constantinople-founded`: adds “apart from the Latin Empire, 1204–1261” to “capital … until the Ottoman takeover of 1453” (the page).
* `ottoman-state-emerges`: adds that sources for these years are scarce, so 1299 is approximate, and that a Byzantine defeat at Bapheus in 1302 contributed to Osman’s rise (the page). The date rests on later narratives *(search result)*.
* `marco-polo-travels`: the journey and the meeting with Kublai Khan are now “by his book’s account”; the page’s section on doubts added (some doubt that he reached China; others find his account of it more accurate and detailed than other travellers’).
* `ibn-battuta-travels`: adds that scholars doubt some of the journeys the Rihla describes, such as a trip up the Volga to Bolghar (the page’s section “Historicity”).
* `kingdom-of-kongo`: adds that its early history comes from oral traditions first written down in the late 16th century (the page).
* `maurya-empire`: “The written record is thin: …” is now “The main written sources are …” (the page’s “primary sources”).
* `silk-road-network`: adds that some historians prefer “Silk Routes” (the page).
* `kamehameha-unites-hawaii`: “conquers Oʻahu in the summer” is now “later that year”: the page says “the summer of 1795”, and the article on the Battle of Nuʻuanu gives May 1795 *(search result)*; the season is left out.
* `funan`: the one pin stood at Angkor Borei (Cambodia) while the name said Óc Eo as well; both sites are now places (Angkor Borei, KH; Óc Eo, VN, 10.233, 105.152, from Wikipedia’s coordinates), neither marked approximate.
* `potosi-silver`: adds “its population probably exceeds 100,000 by 1600 and, on the Wikipedia page’s account, eventually exceeds 200,000”. New source: Bakewell, “Potosí”, *History of World Trade Since 1450* (Encyclopedia.com; “by 1600 probably exceeded 100,000”).

### Source-only fixes (5)

`kanem-bornu-empire`, `caliphate-of-cordoba`, `asante-empire` and `magellan-elcano-circumnavigation`: the Wikipedia address was a redirect and is now the page itself (*Kanem–Bornu Empire*, *Umayyad state of Córdoba*, *Asante Empire*, *Magellan expedition*; the Caliphate of Córdoba is a section of that page, 929–1031). `ghana-empire`: the title of al-Bakri’s book now says “usually dated 1068; the page gives 1057” (the Boston University page is headed 1057; the usual date is 1067–68 *(search result)*). The address of `macassan-trepang-trade` also moved (above).

### Left uncertain, and not verified

* **Four pages were opened and read but are not linked**, because their hosts are not on the list in `tests/history.test.js` (`HOSTS`: “a new host is added there on purpose”), which this reading was not to change: Livius, *Mycenaean Greeks* (about 1600 to 1050 BCE); Kroll, “The Coins of Sardis” (Sardis Expedition, 2010); Werner, “Libya’s Forgotten Desert Kingdom” (Saudi Aramco World, 2004); Clark and May’s introduction to *Macassan History and Heritage* (ANU Press, 2013). A change to the `Garamantes` entry was made and taken back for this reason: the page says they settled the Fezzan “at least as early as 1000 BC”; Aramco World reports excavated walls at Zinchecra of the ninth century BCE, foggaras built “as early as the sixth century BC”, and a date of the fourth century BCE for Old Garama; a search result gives radiocarbon dates for the foggaras from about 400 BCE to 700 CE. The entry says what the page says, and this is the record of the difference.
* **Read against the cited page alone** (no second source opened or searched): 74 events; among the disputed and reported ones, `madagascar-settlement`, `kanem-bornu-empire`, `roman-republic`, `pytheas-voyage`, `kingdom-of-benin` and `marco-polo-travels`; among the 8 earliest, `kerma-kingdom` and `minoan-civilization`. Their dates and numbers are the pages’; where a page repeats a figure that a book gives differently (Cahokia’s 15,000 to 20,000, Garama’s 4,000 and 6,000, Mapungubwe’s 5,000, the Sokoto Caliphate’s 10 to 20 million) it was not checked.
* `east-polynesia-settlement`: the abstract of Wilmshurst and others (PNAS 2011) was read in the PubMed Central record: 1,434 radiocarbon dates, the Society Islands about 1025–1120, four centuries later than previously assumed, then after 70–265 years the other islands about 1190–1290, chronologies that had differed by more than 1000 years: all as written. Only the one study’s chronology is given; the rest of the article was read in part.
* `hopewell-exchange`: the dates kept (the page: 100 BCE to 500 CE); the National Park Service gives about 1–400 CE for the Middle Woodland period and “2,200 to 1,500 years ago” for the Hopewell, and a 2023 study of 425 radiocarbon dates puts the end of most activity about 395–430 CE *(search results)*. The Atlas row has −100 to 500.
* `macassan-trepang-trade`: the order “first in the Kimberley, some decades later in Arnhem Land” is the page’s lead only; the second source names both coasts without an order. Status stays documented (the confidence L carries the doubt about the date); it could be argued to be disputed.
* `tui-tonga-empire`: the date 950 is the page’s; no source was found for how it was reckoned.
* `first-fleet-sydney`, `kamehameha-unites-hawaii`, `nan-madol`, `wari-empire`, `hittite-empire`, `ottoman-state-emerges`: the second sources named above were search results, not pages read.
* The pin of `independence-of-brazil` (“Ipiranga, São Paulo”, marked approximate) is 4.5 km from the Museu do Ipiranga; `xiongnu-confederation` and `first-turkic-khaganate` give the Mongolian Plateau the code MN although the plateau reaches into China (the point stands in Mongolia); both left.

**For the maker (neutrality and the list of hosts, left as a choice).** (1) The four hosts above: add them to `HOSTS` on purpose if the pages may be linked; the entries for Mycenaean Greece, Lydia, the Garamantes and the Macassans could then cite them. (2) `funan` still uses the page’s word “Indianized”, which some scholars now avoid; kept as the page’s. (3) The label “Macassan trepangers” keeps the spelling common in Australia; the page now spells it “Makassan”. (4) `louisiana-purchase` now says in the page’s words that France controlled only a small part of what it sold and that most of it was inhabited by Native Americans; the label (“buys Louisiana from France”) is unchanged.

### Sources that refused or failed (not retried)

britannica.com (403 for the reading tool), whc.unesco.org (the UNESCO World Heritage pages, 403), www.metmuseum.org (the Heilbrunn timeline, 429), www.worldhistory.org (403), escholarship.org (403). Two pages, nps.gov (*Hopewell Culture*) and the State Library of New South Wales (*Eora*), returned a page with no text (built by script), so nothing was read there. The Wikipedia interface answered a pass of one request every 1.2 seconds with 429 (too many requests) after seven pages; the pass was repeated at one request every three seconds, with a wait where it asked for one, and finished. No page was downloaded to the project; the text of the pages was kept outside it and used to read.

### Files, and the built file

Changed in the shared project: `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json` (39 events; no event added or removed, no year moved) and this file. **`atlas/world.js` must be rebuilt** (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`, the project manager’s step): until then `tests/history.test.js` fails one check in the shared project, the one that compares the built file with the JSON. In a private clone with the Atlas rebuilt there: `history` 95 passed, 0 failed; `neutral` 20; `data-integrity` 41; `atlas` 103; `conflicts` 38; `related` 165; `sections` 52, all passed. The build’s own guard limits a summary to 520 characters (three summaries were longer in a first draft and were shortened). The edits were made one by one with the editing tool, each on a string that occurs once, so nothing else in the file moved.

## World history timeline, second reading of batch 2 (22 November 2026)

**What was read, and how.** A second reader (a child session of the project manager, not the session that wrote the events) went through the last 108 events of `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json` that had a first reading only: the 47 of the strand *ideas and arts*, the 43 of *science and technology* and the 18 of *disease and disaster* (87 documented, 10 reported, 11 disputed; the ids are listed at the end). With the 86 of 18 November 2026 and the 99 of the second reading of batch 1 (also 22 November), every one of the 293 events has now had a second reading. *Method*, the same as for batch 1. (1) The 115 Wikipedia citations (all of them distinct pages) were looked up through the MediaWiki interface (`redirects=1`, the disambiguation property): all exist, none is a disambiguation page, 13 were redirects (fixed, below). The four pages the entries cite outside Wikipedia (Diodorus Siculus XII.36 and Suetonius, *Julius*, at LacusCurtius; the National Archives’ transcription of the Declaration; Augustine, *Confessions* VII, at New Advent) were opened and read. (2) The plain text of every cited Wikipedia page, as it stood on 22 November 2026, was read through the same interface, one request every three seconds (11 short waits where it asked for one). A script listed for each summary the numbers, the capitalised names and the longer words that are not on its page, and for each sentence of it the best-matching sentences of the *whole* page; a second script listed the sentences of the page that carry a hedge (“disputed”, “may have”, “some scholars”, “claimed” …), overlap the summary and are not in it. Every sentence of every summary was then read against the body of its page for numbers, who, where, “first”, “largest”, tense and cause; the first-reading summaries mostly paraphrase the lead, and most of the corrections below were found in the sections (Zhang Heng, Su Song, Rapa Nui, Charaka, the Declaration, the Islamic calendar, Nalanda) or in what the lead leaves out (Dunhuang, the Twelve Tables). (3) The places: the coordinates of 109 place names were compared with those of the Wikipedia page of the same name where there is one (59 names; all within 10 km except two region markers, Egypt and the Deccan Plateau, and the Nazca Desert at 15 km); the other names, sites with no page of that name and regions, were judged from known positions and from the coordinates of the page of the event (the Pazyryk burials, Nalanda), and the ISO country of every place was read; two pins were moved. (4) A second source outside the cited Wikipedia page was then looked for in 15 events: **7 events** against a page that was opened and read (the four pages above, which the entries already cite; McIntosh’s introduction to the Igbo-Ukwu symposium at PubMed Central, now added as a source; the MacTutor page on the *Nine Chapters*; the Environment & Society Portal’s page on the flood of 1887: the last two are not linked, see below), and **8 more** against a search result only, marked *(search result)* in the text (a search summary is not a page read). (5) The wording was read against the neutrality rule (`tests/history.test.js` pins the list of loaded words), the 19 notes of the `related` links were read, and those that state a fact (Tupaia’s death at Batavia, the Revolution and the Code, the end of the Atlantic slave trade in 1867) were set against the page or the linked entry, and the links to traditions were checked against the Atlas rows and their own dates. No date was checked against Wikidata (not fetched; waiting for the maker’s go-ahead).

**Counts.** 108 events read (47 ideas, 43 science, 18 disaster). **37 changed**: 12 corrected (a statement, a date, a place or the title of an entry was wrong, unsupported or one-sided), 17 made more exact or more even-handed without having been wrong, 8 with only a source address or title fixed. **No status was changed** (87 documented, 10 reported, 11 disputed, before and after); **one date confidence was** (the Igbo-Ukwu bronzes, M to L); two pins moved (Pazyryk, Nalanda) and one place name was reworded (Pāṇini’s Salatura); two titles changed (the Müteferrika press, the Igbo-Ukwu bronzes); one source was added (McIntosh 2022, grade B); no link to a tradition changed (six that do not overlap in years were kept on purpose, below). No event was added or removed and no year moved, so the file is still in date order. 71 events were read and left as they were. Of the 108 events, 103 still rest on Wikipedia pages alone (grade C), against 104 before; 4 rest in part on a record from the time (the Declaration, Diodorus, Suetonius, Augustine), 1 now also on a scholarly article: the checks above are only as good as that summary plus the second sources named here.

### Made more exact or more even-handed (17)

* `amarna-letters`: “most are in a mixed Canaanite-Akkadian” is now “most are in a variety of Akkadian sometimes described as a mixed Canaanite-Akkadian” (the page).
* `hundred-schools`: “compete for the patronage of rival states” is now “developed and patronised by competing bureaucracies” (the page).
* `hippocratic-corpus`: “from the last decades of the 5th century BCE and the first half of the 4th” is now “most of them” (the page: “The majority of the works … date from the Classical period”; later works are named).
* `arthashastra`: the 1st to 3rd century CE was given as Olivelle’s date for “the present text”; the page gives the earlier treatises as his (2nd century BCE to 1st century CE), the compilation to the 1st century CE as McClish’s and Olivelle’s, and a major expansion and redaction to the 2nd or 3rd century CE; “most likely completed in the available form between the 1st and 3rd century CE” is the page’s own sentence. The entry says so.
* `panini-ashtadhyayi`: “a grammarian from Salatura in Gandhara” is now “whom later sources call a man of Salatura” (the page: the 8th-century Maitraka copper plates and Xuanzang’s memoir; “it is even unclear where and when he lived”), and the place name says so too; adds that the coins named in the rules lead some (von Hinüber, Falk) to the mid-4th century, Cardona to “no earlier than 400 BCE”.
* `shiji-sima-qian`: “the Yellow Emperor” is now “the legendary Yellow Emperor” (the page); the address was a redirect.
* `nalanda`: “It is burned by Muhammad Bakhtiyar Khalji about 1200” is now “raided and burned about 1200 by forces of … Khalji, on the evidence of ash and Tibetan records” (the page: a thick layer of ashes in the excavations of 1920–21, “held to be arson, blamed upon the troops of … Khalji”; Tibetan records, among them Dharmasvamin about 1234; the passage of Minhaj-i-Siraj that is often cited “actually describes the destruction of Odantapura”; “remain operational for decades (or possibly even centuries)”); “the Chinese pilgrim” and “many” were cut for length; the pin moved 10.5 km to the site (25.136, 85.443); the address was a redirect.
* `khipu-record-keeping`: “one claim of a phonetic decipherment is debated” is now “how non-numeric information is encoded is disputed, and one claim of a phonetic decipherment rests on khipus of the colonial period” (the page: Hyland’s khipus are of the 18th-century rebellion, and “their encoding may have been influenced by the introduction of European writing systems”). The page’s sentence that a proto-khipu at Caral “has yet to be thoroughly evaluated” was not added (length).
* `hijra-hijri-epoch`: “its date equates to 16 July 622” read as the date of the journey; it is the first day of the year of the Hijra, 1 Muharram (the Islamic calendar page: “1 Muharram of the new fixed calendar corresponded to Friday, 16 July 622 CE”; the page on the Hijrah says Muhammad left Mecca “in May 622”).
* `chaco-canyon`: “began with a 50-year drought from 1130” is now “Climate change is thought to have led to … abandonment … which began about 1140, perhaps triggered by an extreme 50-year drought from 1130” (the page).
* `ibn-al-haytham-optics`: “it survives in a Latin edition” left out that the Arabic text survives (the page on the *Book of Optics* lists Sabra’s editions of it); now: translated into Latin at the end of the 12th or the start of the 13th century, influential in the Middle Ages, cited by Galileo, Descartes, Kepler and Huygens (the two pages).
* `bi-sheng-movable-type`: adds that Shen Kuo’s text places the invention in the Qingli period, 1041–1048 (the page quotes it; its lead says 1039–1048, kept; “1039 appears to be an outlier” *(search result)*).
* `zhu-xi-neo-confucianism`: “Korea and Vietnam” is now “Japan, Korea and Vietnam” (the page).
* `muqaddimah`: adds “where he wrote its first version” (the page).
* `dunhuang-library-cave`: adds that some scholars call the removal of the manuscripts “plunder” (the page, which also says the Chinese government has called for their return and that Wang “allegedly” found them behind a sealed wall: not added, length).
* `twelve-tables`: the entry (reported) now says that the accounts were written a couple of centuries later and that scholars do not agree on the exact account (the page: “There is no scholarly agreement about the exact historical account of the creation and promulgation of the laws of the Twelve Tables”), and that the plebeian agitation is “in those accounts”.
* `nine-chapters-mathematical-art`: “composed by several generations of scholars from the 10th to the 2nd century BCE” is now “by the page’s account”. MacTutor (opened, not linked): Liu Hui believed the text was “originally written around 1000 BC”, “most historians think the text originated around 200 BC”, “others give dates between 100 BC and 50 AD”; Chemla puts the compilation in the 1st century CE, Guo Shuchun at the end of the 1st century BCE *(search result)*. The page itself says Han records name no author. Status kept (documented); confidence L.

### Source-only fixes (8)

`behistun-inscription`, `nazca-lines`, `volcanic-winter-536`, `samalas-eruption-1257`, `timbuktu-manuscripts`, `cocoliztli-epidemics`, `first-cholera-pandemic` and `north-china-famine-1876-79`: the Wikipedia address was a redirect and is now the page itself (*Behistun inscription*, *Nazca lines*, *Volcanic winter of 536*, *1257 Samalas eruption*, *Timbuktu Manuscripts*, *Cocoliztli epidemics*, *1817–1824 cholera pandemic*, *Northern Chinese Famine of 1876–1879*). Five more addresses were fixed inside events changed for other reasons (`muteferrika-press`, `nalanda`, `shiji-sima-qian`, `sleeping-sickness-1896`, `igbo-ukwu-bronzes`): 13 in all. All 13 new addresses were looked up again: none is a redirect, a missing page or a disambiguation page.

### Left uncertain, and not verified

* **`chavin-de-huantar` is not verified.** The entry says what the page says (built in stages “from before 1200 BCE”, most of it by about 750 BCE, used until about 500 BCE). A search result gives Burger’s radiocarbon sequence (*Latin American Antiquity*, 2019): Urabarriu about 950–800 cal BCE, Chakinani 800–700, Janabarriu 700–400, the site founded about 950 cal BCE and abandoned by 400 cal BCE, “a lack of consensus” on its chronology *(search result)*. The article itself could not be opened (403), so the sentence was left as it was.
* **`yellow-river-flood-1887`**: the page (1,740 characters) says “at least 930,000”; the Environment & Society Portal (opened, not linked) says estimates “range from one to two million” and that nearly as many died of the sickness that followed; the English article’s range is 900,000 to 2 million *(search result)*. “At least” holds on all three; the entry was left.
* **`jalali-calendar`**: the page (4,119 characters) gives the adoption on 15 March 1079; an arXiv paper gives the vernal equinox of 1079, Friday 21 March *(search result)*: the same day if the page’s date is Julian (not verified). The entry was left.
* **`nok-culture`** and **`babylonian-zodiac`**, **`popol-vuh-recorded`**, **`bi-sheng-movable-type`**, **`su-song-clock-tower`**, **`ayurvedic-compendia`**, **`chavin-de-huantar`**, **`jalali-calendar`** were checked by a search result only: Breunig and Rupp’s chronology of 1500 BCE to the turn of the era, terracottas after about 900 cal BCE (the page’s dates); the first star list with the signs about 410 BCE and the page’s 409–398 BCE; the original of the *Popol Vuh* “probably … the mid-sixteenth century”, lost, Ximénez’s copy of 1701–03; the points above.
* **Read against the cited page alone** (no second source opened or searched): 93 events. Among the 11 disputed ones all but `ayurvedic-compendia` (`sanskrit-epics`, `sangam-literature`, `arthashastra`, `lalibela-churches`, `rapa-nui-moai`, `little-ice-age`, `plague-of-cyprian`, `cocoliztli-epidemics`, `bengal-famine-1770`, `sydney-smallpox-1789`); among the 10 reported ones all but `meton-cycle` (Diodorus), `babylonian-zodiac` and `popol-vuh-recorded` (`ancient-olympic-games`, `twelve-tables`, `eratosthenes-earth`, `hipparchus-precession`, `zhang-heng-seismoscope`, `hijra-hijri-epoch`, `sundiata-epic`); among the 11 dated 1000 BCE or earlier all but `nok-culture` and `chavin-de-huantar` (`stonehenge`, `drought-4200-years-ago`, `sintashta-chariots`, `poverty-point`, `egyptian-science-papyri`, `rigveda`, `amarna-letters`, `sanxingdui`, `dong-son-culture`). Their dates and numbers are the pages’; where a page repeats a figure that a book gives differently (the 345 acres of Poverty Point, the 382 tablets of Amarna, the 8,000 soldiers of the Terracotta Army, the 5.6 to 9.6 million of the Indian famine, the 5 to 15 million of the cocoliztli, the 15 million of the third plague pandemic, the 36,417 of Krakatoa) it was not checked.
* **`hipparchus-precession`**: the clause that Hipparchus “could not reconcile his data with the earlier observations of Timocharis and Aristillus” looked unsupported in the section on the precession of the equinoxes and is on the page in its section on the stars (“according to Ptolemy”): read, unchanged.
* **Links to traditions whose years do not overlap the event’s** (the rule used for batch 1), kept on purpose because each is the tradition that holds the text or council, or whose texts the event opened: `rigveda` and *Hinduism* (row from 500 BCE; the Rigveda is one of its sacred texts, and the Hinduism filter would otherwise not show it), `council-of-nicaea` and *Catholicism* and *Eastern Orthodoxy* (rows from 451), `augustine-against-astrology` and *Catholicism*, `cocoliztli-epidemics` and *Aztec religion* (row to 1521; the entry names the Aztecs’ word), `rosetta-stone-decipherment` and *Egyptian religion* (row to 391; the stone’s decree is a priests’ decree and its decipherment opened the texts). Decision for the maker.
* **`su-song-clock-tower`**: the page contradicts itself (“moved north to Beijing in 1127” and “brought south after the imperial capital moved southeast”); the entry follows the first.
* `egyptian-science-papyri` and `india-famine-1876-78` carry region markers (Egypt, the Deccan Plateau) that are 122 and 222 km from the page’s coordinates of those names; left. `terracotta-army`: the pin is 5.5 km from the pits; left.
* `hipparchus-precession`, `eratosthenes-earth` and the other reported entries rest on the later accounts the pages name (Ptolemy, Cleomedes); that is what the status says.

**For the maker (neutrality, hosts and one status, left as a choice).** (1) Hosts: `livius.org`, `sardisexpedition.org` and `press-files.anu.edu.au` (batch 1) are still not on `HOSTS`; two more pages of this reading were opened and could be linked if their hosts were added on purpose: `mathshistory.st-andrews.ac.uk` (the *Nine Chapters*, where the page’s 10th-century start is contradicted by most historians) and `www.environmentandsociety.org` (the flood of 1887: one to two million). The Cambridge page of Burger’s Chavín chronology refused; a link by `doi.org` is on the list but nobody has opened the article. (2) `rigveda` is documented with confidence L; its date is read from language, not from a record, so it could be argued to be reported. (3) The six links above. (4) `igbo-ukwu-bronzes` keeps Shaw’s window (c. 800–900 CE) in its date label: the summary says the date is contested; the window could be widened (c. 800–1200). (5) `dunhuang-library-cave` uses the page’s word “plunder”, given to “some scholars”. (6) The Müteferrika label now says what the page says; the old label overclaimed. (7) No “the Atlas” wording was found in `world_events.json` (the only “Atlas” is the Atlas Mountains).

### Sources that refused or failed (not retried)

`doi.org` for the Cambridge Core article of Burger (2019) on the Chavín radiocarbon sequence (403 for the reading tool). The Wikipedia interface answered 11 times with “too many requests” during the pass of one request every three seconds and was waited for (it also answered once when a second script ran at the same time as the first); every page was read. No page was downloaded to the project; the text of the pages was kept outside it and used to read.

### Files, and the built file

Changed in the shared project: `tools/atlas_src/json/world_events.json` (37 events; no event added or removed, no year moved), `tools/atlas_src/json/world_meta.json` (the `review_note` only: it now says that a second reader checked all 207 events added after the first 86), `src/js/2z-data-methods.js` (the log row `history2b`, the stage row of the History timeline, now “All 293 events have had a second reading”, and the last sentence of the row `history2a`, which said that the 108 had “a first reading only”), `tests/methods.test.js` (the History pins moved to 86 + 99 + 108 and a pin for the new row, mutation-checked: changing `read`, `second` or `changed` fails it) and this file. **`atlas/world.js` must be rebuilt** (`python3 -I tools/build_atlas.py`, the project manager’s step): until then `tests/history.test.js` fails one check (the built file against the JSON) and `tests/methods.test.js` one (the grades of the sources, which count the built file and now see one more grade B), both only for that reason. In a private clone with the Atlas rebuilt there: `history` 100 passed, 0 failed; `methods` 83; `neutral` 20; `data-integrity` 41; `atlas` 103; `conflicts` 38; `related` 187; `sections` 52; `random-context` 59, all passed. The build’s own guard limits a summary to 520 characters (three drafts were longer and were shortened). The edits were made one by one with the editing tool, each on a string that occurs once, so nothing else in the file moved.


## High-control groups: second reading, part 2 (22 November 2026)

**What this is.** The first reading of the High-control groups page (the section above, 22 November 2026) left 24 lines it could not settle: 12 claims marked “Not in the page read”, 9 claims with no mark (never read) and 3 of the seven lines under “Not yet written up” that carried an “unf” note. A child session of the project manager (not the session that wrote the cards, and not the one that made the first reading) read those 24 lines again, one at a time, against pages it could open: court opinions, the groups’ own pages, a news agency’s or newspaper’s report where the line is about a hearing or a ruling, and reference pages. It worked read only, one request at a time about six seconds apart, with no retries; a page that refused was left and is listed below. It used `curl` with a generic client name for pages (text and JSON), and the English Wikipedia API for the reference pages and their lists of references; web searches were used only to find an address, and nothing a search summary said is counted as read. Nothing was saved into the project. Where the claim is a group’s own account the group’s own pages were opened first.

**The marks are the same as in the first reading** (`r` and `fx` in the data; “Found in the page read”, “Part of it in the page read”, “Not in the page read”, “Corrected after reading”). A line that is now found has a mark; if its text was changed, it also has `fx`. A line still not found stays as written. A card is marked *checked* only when every claim on it is found, and none is: **No card is marked “checked”**, and all 13 still say “Under review”.

Branch*, 91 F.3d 699; *Andrade v. Chojnacki*, 338 F.3d 448; *Synanon Church v. United States*, 579 F.Supp. 967 and 820 F.2d 421; 299 Or. 344), the Internet Archive’s metadata for the Armstrong papers and its plain-text copy of the 1984 decision (a small text file; the 17 MB scan was not fetched), `govinfo.gov` (House Report 104-749), the Church of Scientology’s own sites (`scientology.org`, `scientologynews.org`, `freedommag.org`, `scientologyreligion.org`), `jonestown.sdsu.edu` (tapes Q279 and Q987), `heavensgate.com`, `twelvetribes.org`, `familyfed.org`, `abc.net.au`, `sbs.com.au`, `deseret.com`, `irishtimes.com`, `time.com`, `cbsnews.com`, `pbs.org`, `asianews.network`, `ucpress.edu`, `archiveswest.orbiscascade.org`, `archive.seattletimes.com`, `bishop-accountability.org` (a copy of the Royal Commission’s own news release) and the English Wikipedia API. **Refused or failed:** `childabuseroyalcommission.gov.au` (the connection was reset on two different pages, one after a redirect), `abuseincare.org.nz` (HTTP 403 for the copy of the Australian report), `archive.spectator.co.uk` (HTTP 202 with no text), `upi.com` (403), `opposing.scientology.org` (connection reset), a University of Vienna PDF (404), and the search tool could not search `theguardian.com`, `smh.com.au`, `orlandosentinel.com`, `sandiegouniontribune.com` or `chicagotribune.com` at all. One address was guessed wrongly (232 Cal.App.3d 60 is another case) and one Twelve Tribes address was a guess that did not exist (404); neither was repeated.

Breckenridge, Jr., Judge of the Superior Court, dated June 1984. It orders that the Church and Mary Sue Hubbard “take nothing” on the tort claims and that Armstrong is entitled to judgment and costs; the court found the testimony of Armstrong and others credible and the defence of justification established. It says the Church, with its “Fair Game” doctrine, “has harassed and abused” people outside it whom it saw as enemies, in addition to “violating and abusing” its members’ civil rights, and that the evidence portrays Hubbard as “virtually a pathological liar” about his history, background and achievements. Ron Hubbard’s official biography.” Now the text gives the ruling (June 1984), the judgment (10 August 1984), the ground of justification, the two criticisms in neutral words (the Church’s treatment of members and of people it saw as enemies; Hubbard not truthful about his history, background and achievements) and the 1991 affirmation; “official biography” is dropped, since the decision speaks of his history, background and achievements. **Not read, or not settled.** (1) The Church of Scientology’s own account of Operation Snow White and the Guardian’s Office (Scientology 15): a lead exists (above) but its page could not be opened. (2) The Twelve Tribes’ own head-count (Twelve Tribes 3). (3) For the lines found only in part, what is missing: Branch Davidians 10 (“unprovoked”, “willing to talk”); Heaven’s Gate 8 (charges; the medical examiner’s own wording, not a news report of it); NXIVM 3 (the size of DOS); Rajneesh 3 (“no reliable count of followers since”); Unification Church 3 (the independent estimates), 8 (heavy pressure in so many words). (4) Everything that rests on Wikipedia alone: the Branch Davidians scholars (11), NXIVM’s mission statement (4), the Rajneesh and Raëlism sizes, the Unification Church’s infobox figure, the FLDS’s concentration in two towns; a reader with the books or the primary pages should open them. (5) The Royal Commission’s report itself (Jehovah’s Witnesses), whose site did not open. (6) The books the claims name were not re-read.

**What the maker or the project manager should decide.** (1) Still no card is marked “checked”: all 156 claims are read, but 43 only in part, 2 not found, and many rest on a reference page. (2) The line on Jehovah’s Witnesses now states the Commission’s count in its own words and the 2017 hearing on shunning; whether to add the Commission’s findings and the organisation’s reply (the brief said: narrow, correct or confirm, never sharpen) is the maker’s. (3) The Armstrong line quotes none of the judge’s sharper words; it says the Church’s treatment of members and critics was criticised and that Hubbard was found untruthful about his own history; the strongest phrases (“schizophrenic and paranoid”, “pathological liar”) are in the decision and are not on the card. (4) The Scientology “own account” line is now sourced to the Church’s own pages and names Germany and the United States; the Church’s statements about “apostates” and “disgruntled” former members are quoted by one word only (“disgruntled”). (5) Peoples Temple 5 now says that one of the two tapes is a rehearsal; that is what the transcript shows and it was kept because leaving it out would have misrepresented what the recording is.
